The inside story of a secular Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of literature and philology and mortgaged his pen, intellect and scholarship to wreck Hinduism

# Max Muller a Lifelong Masquerade

## Preface

This book, Max Muller - A Lifelong Masquerade, is the inside story of a German secular Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of scholarship with the ignoble purpose of wrecking the religious faith of the Hindus.

Christianity is an imperialist religion and no other proselytizing creed has more constantly and systematically made greater use of the written word to indoctrinate people of other faiths and to undermine their religious beliefs. Literature evangelism has been one of the mainstays of Christian imperialism and from time to time Christian litterateurs have wielded their pens in the service of the Christian Church and of Jesus Christ. Some of them have excelled in their respective fields and the one who devoted all his life endeavouring to serve the Christian Church and in trying to win over to Christianity people from Hinduism Max Muller stands supreme.

Max Muller was a fugitive from Germany. In his youth he had been completely down and out finding it extremely difficult to earn sufficient even to provide two square meals for himself. At such a time British imperialism which can only be described more correctly as Christian imperialism run to ground came to Max Muller's rescue. The East India Company hired Max Muller for rendering the Rigveda mantras from Vedic-Sanskrita into English which the theo-politicians who then ruled and wielded power in London called translation and for his services the Company agreed to pay Max Muller a sum of £ 4 for each page which he was to get ready for print. And this was notwithstanding the fact that neither Sanskrita nor English was Max Muller's mother tongue. In his own words Max Muller had never expected such a reward which gave this fledgling from Germany a lucrative assignment for the next eight years. All this happened in the year of Grace 1847 when one English pound was equal to about ten Indian rupees. The assignment for which Max Muller was not at all fit by any standards proved to be a virtual windfall for the German student of Sanskrita (he was then about 24 years young) who at that time understood neither Patanjali's Mahabhashya nor Yaska's Nirukta and much less Panini's Ashtadhyyayi.

That Max Muller was a scholar of unusually high calibre cannot be denied. Nevertheless, Max Muller had mortgaged the scholar in exchange for English bread and butter. He had pawned his soul to feed his body; he had turned a hireling. The English used him as a stooge to gain their theo-political ends and this must be admitted that Max Muller served his English masters well and to the best of his ability. More than once he went out of his way to fawn and truckle to ingratiate himself with his Christian masters who in turn did their best and whatever lay in their power to boost and bolster his prestige as a scholar of the Vedas throughout the world generally and in particular throughout Hindustan and other British colonies which they, then, held in political bondage.

The true story of Max Muller the secular Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the facade of Sanskrit literature is long. It has often been designedly hidden and camouflaged behind praises and laudatory compliments paid to him during his lifetime and after is death. Concerted and organized attempts have often been made to project Max Muller as a friend of Hinduism and of the Hindus. The substerfuge did work for some time — for a long time indeed covering a long period of more than half a century while Max Muller lived in Oxford — but as no one has ever been able to fool all the world for all the time Max Muller too was discovered but only after he had been dead for long time.

Max Muller lived for more than 50 years in Oxford where he died in 1900. He was an able scholar and a prolific writer. He delivered lectures, wrote books and essays, gave Maxmullerian discourses to private gatherings. Whatever he said and wrote was considered and applauded by theo-politicians throughout Christendom as something coming from a messiah or from someone descended direct from heaven above. Though his writings and speeches had made Max Muller famous and a celebrity in his lifetime throughout the world as a great scholar the man in him had remained mysteriously unknown and un-understood till his first biography by his widow became available.

When Max Muller's first biography (The Life and Letters of the Right Honourable Friedrich Max Muller) by Georgina Max Muller appeared in 1902 something strange, unexpected and providential happened. His biographer, Max Muller's widow, made a significant closure. She told the world:

"It may be thought that the publication of these volumes (the biography was in 2 volumes—Br. D.Bh.) is superfluous after the two works Auld Lang Syne and the Autobiography, written by Max Muller himself. But it seemed that something more was wanted to show the innermost character of the real man, .... The plan pursued throughout these volumes has been to let Max Muller's letters and the testimony of friends to his mind and character speak for themselves, whilst the whole is connected by a slight thread of necessary-narrative. The selection from the letters has been made with a view to bring the man rather than the scholar before the world." (Preface to November-1902 New York Edition.).

The Life and Letters ... at once lifted the veil of secrecy with ich Max Muller had consistently covered himself during this time. The world came to know for the first time that Max Muller ; in fact more a Christian missionary than a scholar of the Vedas. j Hindus were particularly disillusioned when they discovered to ir great dismay that Max Muller was neither a great scholar of lic-Sanskrita nor a friend of the Hindus. They recognized in Max Her an enemy of the Hindus who easily surpassed Nobili, Dubois I Carey. In his lifetime Max Muller as a scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita already been likened by Swami Dayananda to a child who was learning to walk! By now both Max Muller the man and Max Her the scholar had been exposed and as a result Max Muller was gotten both as a friend of the Hindus and as a scholar. In its place emerged a new Max Muller — Max Muller the Christian sionary who worked all his lifetime, covertly and in a clandestine mer — to subvert Hinduism and to win over converts for Christ n among the Hindu elite.

In 1947, when India once again became a free country Christian erialism at once recognized the urgent and great need of jencing and befriending the Hindus of India who still constituted lindustan the single largest, most progressive and most influential lie group. Organized attempts were, therefore, made toward irrecting the memory of Max Muller as a scholar extraordinary of Vedas and through this gimmick to re-establish his dubious itation as a great friend of the Hindus. In a carefully planned 'e meant once again to put Max Muller on the high pedestal on which he once stood throughout the second half of the 19th century the name of Goeth House was changed (1957) to Max Muller Bhawan. The purpose was to refurbish and polish up the image of Max Muller in the land of the Vedas and to re-introduce Max Muller to the Hindus as their great friend and benefactor. By this time the biography of Max Muller had become nearly extinct. It was not available even in the well appointed and expensively maintained libraries housed in Max Muller Bhawans so carefully named after him. To fill the void the Oxford University Press came out (1974) with a new biography of Max Muller (Scholar Extraordinary) for which purpose the OUP had commissioned Nirad C. Choudhuri, an Indian writer well known in knowledgeable circles in India for his personal views on Hinduism.

The year 1973 was celebrated as Max Muller's 150th birth anniversary. Special lectures, discussions, seminars and radio and TV programmes were arranged, sponsored, subsidized and financed, to which, by and large, committed and commissioned writers, speakers, educationists and journalists were especially invited to air their views and opinions on the good work done by Max Muller in the service of India and of Hinduism. The purpose of all these politico-cultural exercises seemed, however, to build afresh the image of Max Muller who had since long fallen from grace especially in knowledgeable Hindu circles.

All these exercises and political manoeuvres had proved, however, only of little avail. These failed to achieve the desired goal and the scholar extraordinary of Vedic-Sanskrita continued to remain forgotten and forsaken. The scholar who for more than half a century had merrily basked in favours freely heaped upon him by his English masters and who nearly monopolized the stage for more than 50 years was nearly completely forgotten in less than about as many months after his death.

Even though in 1902 when his first biography (The Life and Letters. . . ) became available it had immediately torn as under the mask from behind which Max Muller had masqueraded all his lifetime he really remained undiscovered for more than 100 years. The present book, Max Muller — A Lifelong Masquerade, is an attempt, the first of its kind ever made, to unmask and unrobe in public the Christian missionary so insidiously hidden behind Max Muller the scholar. Whatever is recorded in these pages has behind it, more often, the authority from Max Muller himself. The evidence against Max Muller for having masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of scholarship (or, might we say, scholarship extraordinary?) is so overwhelmingly revealing and authentic that no amount of explanation or gerrymandering would ever absolve the German scholar of the grave charge that like Nobili, Dubois and Carey he too was a Christian missionary whose main aim in life had been to gather converts for Christ from among the Hindus.

More than one readers might find some expressions and remarks made in the text of Max Muller —A Lifelong Masquerade a bit too outspokenly strong and acerbic. The author takes full responsibility for all that has been said and the manner in which it has been done. He feels convinced that the truth would not go home if it were not told straight from the shoulder or if the truth was given a polish and veneer.

It has taken a number of years to get the manuscript ready for print. Though it necessitated several visits to more than one libraries and individuals in Calcutta, Bombay, Madras, Nagpur, Thanjavur and Kathmandu (Nepal) no claim is being made for having succeeded in fully unmasking this gigantic theo-political masquerade. The story told here is yet incomplete; much more will have to be told when time comes, if Providence permits, to present to the reading public a revised and enlarged edition, of the book.

More than one friends who have chosen to remain unnamed have lent a helping hand in the preparation of the MS for the printers and correcting of proofs received from them and in getting the book ready in its present form. To all of them the author remains gratefully indebted. He must especially thank Mr. Krishna Kapoor whom in spite of more than one attempts he has yet to meet in person. At some cost to himself in money and more so in time Mr. Krishna Kapoor had painstakingly procured for the author a copy of Col. Boden's will which appears in India in print for the first in Appendix B of this book.

The author commends the book especially to every Hindu and Indian Christian interested in history, religion and the proselytizing activities — overt and covert — of the Christian Church in mission lands and also to those who care more for truth from whatever quarter it might come repeating once again the famous Vedic dictum:

Satyameva Jayete.

New Delhi Brahm Datt Bharti

November 21, 1992

## 1 - The Background

India has often been described by some English writers as a rich country inhabited by the poor. A few others have called it the land of tigers and of ash smeared sadhus. Nevertheless, India has been and continues to be very important both to political adventurers and to Christian missionaries. In the past, more than one attempts were made by Christian missionaries to convert India to Christianity and in their endeavour to succeed they made use of every weapon and artifice though without much avail.

Among the Christian missionaries no one has perhaps been more interested in converting India to Christianity than the Jesuits. In the seventeenth century one of them, Robert de Nobili, a Jesuit from Italy, practised even fraud to achieve this end. In his Christian overzeal he went to the extent to forging a Veda with the clandestine purpose of misleading the Hindus of Madura into accepting the Christian religion. Nobili was followed in the eighteenth century by Abbe Dubois who "was a Jesuit missionary. A refugee from the French Revolution he came and settled down in India." Though he acted and simulated in a manner similar to Nobili's he too failed in his mission of bringing the Hindus of Karnataka to Christ. Then came the Baptists in 1793 headed by William Carey who founded (1799) the Serampore Mission near Calcutta. Carey and his colleague, Marshman practised deception for the same purpose but failed in their endeavours mainly as a result of the robust and very effective opposition which they received from Rammohun Roy (b. 1772 - d. 1833) who, between the years A.D. 1815 and A.D. 1831, nearly monopolized the intellectual scene in and around Calcutta which then was the seat of the British government in India as it was then represented by the East India Company.

On March 19, 1818, a missionary of the Mission Society, Rev. William Adam, joined the Serampore coterie and was soon entrusted with the holy task of bringing Rammohun Roy to Christ. His assignment inevitably brought Adam in frequent contact with Rammohun Roy but subsequent events soon made it clear to Adam that he had been given a formidable task and that he was no match for Rammohun Roy. Rev. Adam was so impressed with the arguments which Rammohun Roy advanced in support of Unitarianism as propounded by the Vedas and against Trinitarianism of the Bible that Adam soon started having serious doubt about the Christian cult of three Gods. In a letter which he wrote to N. Bright, a friend, Adam willingly conceded the point which Rammohun Roy had been repeatedly making in favour and support of Unitarianism. Rev. Adam had written:

"It is several months since I began to entertain some doubts respecting the supreme deity of Jesus Christ, suggested by frequent discussions with Rammohun Roy, whom I was trying to bring over to the belief of that doctrine, and in which I was joined by Mr. Yates, who also professed to experience difficulties on the subject. ... I do not hesitate to confess that I am unable to remove the weighty objections which present themselves against this doctrine. . . the objections against it, compared with the arguments for it, appear to me like a mountain compared with a mole hill."

In about three years of his joining the Serampore Mission Adam had reached a point of enlightenment where he had begun to doubt very seriously his earlier Christian convictions. As time passed the conversion of Rev. Adam from Christian Trinitarianism to Vedic monotheism and the fact of his subsequent apostasy soon came to be too widely known. The conversion of a Christian missionary from biblical Trinitarianism to Vedic monotheism, a heresy in the eyes of the Church, created an unprecedented commotion among Christian missionaries in London and an emotional turmoil in mission circles of the United States of America. Most Christian missions which were then operating in India for the purpose of converting the heathens of India to Christianity and their headquarters in London suffered, in consequence, such psychological convulsions that no one really knew how to control and assuage them. The conversion of Rev. Adam gave such severe jolt to Christian missions that it nearly shook the entire missionary network in India down to its foundations. Fearing that a discussion would have serious repercussions the Christian propaganda machine purposely kept away from the notice of the lay Christians, Indian converts in particular, the news of Rev. Adam's apostasy. Nevertheless, the news of his conversion and subsequent excommunication on that account, which the Church had tried to keep closely guarded as a secret, became publicly known when Anglo-Indiareported this extraordinary event in its pages.

Rev. William Adam had gone to gather wool but had returned cleanly shorn. As a result of some further discussions which Adam had with Rammohun Roy he soon sloughed off whatever little remained of his belief in the divinity of Jesus and now Adam started preaching in the company of Rammohun Roy the tenets of Unitarianism (monotheism). As Adam's conversion from biblical Trinitarianism to Unitarianism became more widely known Christian missions, especially those with their headquarters in London, started clamouring for something really effective to be done to check any further advance of the cult of Unitarianismso that no more harm might possibly be done to the Christian belief in the divinity of Jesus. As a first step, therefore, of reprisal against Rev. Adam he was declared a heretic and a Socinian. A statement appearing in the Annual London Report of the Mission Society regarding Rev. William Adam declared: "We mention with deep regret that Mr. Adam, late one of their member (workers with Serampore mission -Br.D.Bh.) had embraced opinions derogatory to the honour of the Saviour (Christ - Br. D. Bh.), denying the proper divinity, of 'Our Lord Jesus Christ' in consequence of which the connection between him and the Society has been dissolved."

The Christian mission organizations in London were now seized of the problem of doing something, something, tangible to counterbalance the effect of monotheistic preachings of Rammohun Roy who traced all his religious beliefs and thinking to the one universal Vedic truth that God must be, and is, One only. As against this the Christian Church had repeatedly failed in the past in her endeavours to sell to the Hindus the biblical cultus of three gods in one. Abbe Dubois, the Jesuit missionary from France, who lived and laboured in India for more than thirty years while confessing his failure to make any success of his mission had admitted: "For my part I cannot boast of my success in this sacred career during the period that I have laboured to promote the interests of Christian religion. The restraints and privations under which I have lived, by conforming myself to the usages of the country; embracing, in many respects the prejudices of the natives; living like them, and becoming all but a Hindu myself; in short; by being made all things to all men, that I might by all means save some — all these have proved of no avail to me to make proselytes. During the long period I have lived in India in the capacity of a missionary, I have made, with the assistance of a native missionary, in all between two and three hundreds converts of both sexes. Of this number two-thirds were Pariahs or beggars; and the rest were composed of Sudras, vagrants, and outcastes of several tribes, who, being without resources, turned Christians in order to form connexions, chiefly for the purpose of marriage, or with some other interested views."

On his return to France (January 15, 1823) the Abbe wrote and compiled his Letters on the State of Christianity in India. In his Letters he pronounced his considered opinion in these words: "Let the Christian religion be presented to these people under any possible light, . . . the time of conversion has passed away, and under existing circumstances there remains no human possibility of bringing it back." But Dubois had not given up hope. Despite this opinion he tried to find a way out — a method by which another Christian missionary to come after him might more easily be able to make a headway in this field. After giving very careful thought to this proposition he made a novel Jesuitical suggestion: "Nevertheless I am persuaded that the notes which I have taken so much trouble to collect will afford some useful material to others more favourably situated than myself; and I have therefore no hesitation in offering them to the public. There is one motive which above all others has influenced my determination. It struck me that a faithful picture of the wickedness and incongruities of polytheism and idolatry would by its very ugliness help greatly to set off the beauties and perfections of Christianity. It was thus that the Lacedaemonians placed drunken slaves in the sight of their children in order to inspire the latter with a horror of intemperance."

The book, Hindu Manners, Customs and Ceremonies of Abbe Dubois was, and continues to be, an important text book for all those young missionaries who opt to work in India and who aspire, like Dubois, to convert the Hindus of India to Christ. Before being given charge of a mission, of a parish or a diocese anywhere in India the new entrants are expected to have chewed and digested with meticulous care the contents of Dubois' book. Whosoever studied Hindu Manners would seldom fail to understand the Christianish motives of Dubois. Though Christian missionaries wanting to make India their field of labour noted with great satisfaction that though the Hindus were, as Dubois had alleged in his book, polytheists and idol worshippers they failed to comprehend how then in spite of this polytheism Rammohun Roy was able so successfully to convince Rev. Adam of Hindu monotheism? The stalwarts of Christian missionary world, most of whom were then to be found clustered in and around London, justifiably argued and agreed among themselves that all air could easily be taken out of Rammohun Roy's arguments which he so successfully employed in support of his Vedic monotheism if they could only find someone, someone, who would prove to the world that the Vedas preached polytheism.

The Christian missions in London now set out to look around for this mysterious someone who might deliver the goods of which Christendom then so desperately stood in need. The choice fell ultimately on a non-descript German youth who was virtually picked up from the road and who had yet to come out of his Sanskrit studies. As the language of the Rigveda was very much akin to Sanskrita it was generally believed that only a person with sufficient knowledge of this ancient language (Sanskrita) would be able to unlock the mysteries of the Rig and thus be in a position to do the job satisfactorily.

The young man who was chosen had yet to complete his 25 years and his name was Friedrich Maximillian Mueller. He was born in Dessau in the year 1823 — the year hi which Rammohun Roy had been in the thickest of his epoch making religious discussions and disputations with the Baptists of Serampore. No doubt Max Muller was a scholar of great promise and of an unusually high calibre but much that has been said and written to extol him to the skies has come either from those who had an axe to grind or from a group who have been blissfully ignorant of the real truth. None the less the fact remains that while he lived in Oxford for more than 50 years he fully enjoyed and basked in the reputation which his writings and speeches had given him as a philologist and a scholar of the Veda.

At this point of our narrative of Max Mullet's lifelong masquerade as a great Sanskrita scholar we shall allow ourselves a disgression. Rammohun Roy had died on the 27th September, 1833 with the mystic AUM on his lips. Hardly a year before (1832) Horace Hayman (H.H.) Wilson had been elected to be the first occupant of the Boden Chair of Sanskrit at Oxford which had been founded with the help of funds bequeathed by (Lt.) Col. (Joseph) Boden who at one time was a "Bombay colonel" with the army of the East India Company. As an ardent Christian Col. Boden had wanted to do whatever lay in his power to help Christian missionaries to christianize India in general and the Hindus in particular. Therefore, when he retired with a fortune made in India he bequeathed his estates estimated at about £ 25,000 to the University of Oxford to enable it to found a Chair of Sanskrit which the University, justifiably and as a mark of gratitude, named after him. In his will Col. Boden had made clear his intentions which otherwise might have remained hidden behind his Christian charitable instincts. The object of Col. Boden's bequest, according to his will, was to "enable his countrymen to proceed in the conversion of the natives of India to the Christian religion" by providing translations of the Bible into Sanskrit.

At the time when Christian missionaries were bristling up with the idea of converting the Hindus of India to Christianity Professor Wilson, the first occupant of the Boden Chair, wrote a book, The Religion and Philosophical Systems of the Hindus. While explaining the reasons for undertaking this work Wilson had said: "These lectures were written to help candidates for a prize of £ 200 given by John Muir, a well known Haileybury man and a great Sanskrit Scholar, for the best refutation of the Hindu Religious Systems." The real purpose behind Wilson's book, which served more like a catechism, however, was to provide novices and inexperienced missionaries with ready-made answers toward refutation of the Hindu religion. H.H. Wilson who had gone to Calcutta in 1808 only as a member of the medical profession had later taken to Sanskrit studies. As before leaving for England in 1832 Wilson had been for some time secretary of the Asiatic Society of Bengal it is not at all unlikely that between the years 1816 and 1830 he had witnessed from close quarters the religious controversies that had so fiercely raged between Rammohun Roy on one side and the Baptists on the other. In the position that he held at Oxford it became a part of his job to help his countrymen in the task of converting the Hindus of India to Christianity. He had come out, therefore, with The Religious and Philosophical Systems of the Hindus which happened in fact to be his personal contribution toward achieving this Christian objective.

In the year 1814, on May 8, about 20 years before the renewal of the Charter of the East India Company (1833) Thomas Fanshaw Middleton had been consecrated as "the first Bishop of the Church of England in India." Almost simultaneously the Christian Knowledge Society (CKS) had decided to hand over their mission work in India to the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel (SPG) placing at the disposal of Bishop Middleton a sum of £ SjOOO "for the promotion of Christianity in India." However, it seems that Bishop Middleton, who was perhaps hoping to be able to buy converts in India like slaves, considered this amount to be inadequate for spreading the religion of Christ in a country with a population many times more numerous then that of England. At his request, therefore, "the Archbishop induced the Prime Minister, Lord Liverpool, to obtain from the Prince Regent a royal Letter authorising collections in all churches for the Society for the Propagation of the Gospel (SPG). By this means £ 50,000 were obtained. Grants were made of £ 5,000 each from the Christian Knowledge Society, and the Bible Society, the last being for the translation of the Scriptures into some of the Asiatic languages." Thus armed and equipped and with plenty of English money in his pocket and more promised Bishop Middlcton set his foot on the Indian soil (1820) with the only avowed purpose of converting the Hindus of India to Christianity.

At the time when Bishop Middleton was planning to convert the Hindus of Calcutta to Christ Rammohun Roy was actively engaged in his religious disputations with William Carey and other Baptists of Serampore one of whom after having laboured initially to bring the Rajah to Christ had himself in the end forsaken the Saviour. Once when Rammohun Roy went to meet Middleton in his house the Bishop made a brazen-faced offer to Rammohun Roy: "This Bishop had called him and then had entered into a long argument to persuade him to accept Christianity, and not content with this singular stretch of the hand of hospitality, had wound up by expatiating on "the great career which would open to him by a change of faith." "He would be honoured in life and lamented in death, — honoured in England as well as in India; his name would descend to posterity as that of the modern Apostle of India."

Bishop Middleton had made overtures to Rammohun Roy as no honourable man would ever make to another; the feel of English money in his pocket seems to have gone to his head. But little did he realize that Rammohun Roy stood in need neither of Middleton's riches nor of Christian salvation which Christian missionaries so open handedly promised to all those who show any signs of agreeing to renounce their ancestral religion or those who allow their minds to be subverted. "Rammohun Roy was very highly incensed" and treating the offer the Bishop had made with utter disdain he left him without uttering a word! Once again another messenger of Christ had failed to win over a Hindu intellectual.

In the year 1820 year another even of importance had taken place. The Precepts of Jesus, the Guide to Peace and Happiness written by Rammohun Roy made its appearance. The Baptist missionaries of Serampore took strong exception to it only because Rammohun Roy had scrupulously excluded from his narratives all biblical miracles in general and in particular the virgin birth of Jesus and his more miraculous/ resurrection and the subsequent ascension (going bodily up into heaven above) on which alone rested, though only precariously, the claim of divinity of Jesus. In the course of their vituperative attacks on Rammohun Roy the Baptist missionaries called him a "heathen" which gave Rammohun Roy such an umbrage that in reply he mercilessly lashed out at some of their Christian dogmas.

The Serampore missionaries looked more like quacks when compared to Rammohun Roy. In every respect he stood head and shoulders above any one of them. Their description of Rammohun Roy as a heathen had immensely angered him who unlike them had studied Christianity from original sources. Since Rammohun Roy had all along been speaking and writing against idolatry in any form and had been a staunch supporter of monotheism by no stretch of imagination could he be declared, he argued, as one not believing in One God.

In reprisal Rammohun Roy wrote in 1820 The Precepts of Jesus, First Appeal to the Christian Public which was later followed (1821) by the Second Appeal and then in 1823 by the Third Appeal. In all these he made his stand decisively clear. He thoroughly repudiated and refuted the allegation made against him of being a heathen. Very cogently he advanced arguments to prove that since he was "a believer in One true God" and "in the truths revealed in Christian system" he was not a heathen. In his attempt to prove Trinitarianism as a sort of limited polytheism Rammohun Roy reasoned so incisively that his arguments, irrefutable as they were, cut through Christian Trinitarianism as a knife would run through a block of butter. He argued with a sharp sting that the only difference between Hinduism of his days and Christianity was in "a few multiples of the number three." According to him "the same omnipotence which makes three one and one three can equally reconcile the unity and plurality of three hundred and three millions, both being supported by a sublime mystery which far transcends all human comprehension."

The Baptist missionaries of Serampore and William Carey approached nowhere near Rammohun Roy in matters of talent, theological knowledge, intellect, wide vision, objectivity and comprehension. Thereafter, Rammohun Roy relentlessly continued to make such merry sport of Christian Trinitarianism founded on the biblical cultus of three gods (God, the Father; God, the Son Jesus Christ; and God, the Holy Ghost) that it gave Serampore missionaries some unbearable heartaches and many a sleepless night. In furtherance of his views Rammohun Roy also published a biting satire, A Dialogue between a Missionary and Three Chinese Converts which made the Christian dogma of Trinity not only a thing to be laughed at but it also dramatically transformed the Serampore missionaries into a target for widespread ridicule mixed with derision.

The Baptists had no reasonable counter explanation to what Rammohun Roy had offered in support of Unitarianism and in

denunciation of biblical Trinitarianism. None the less, since that day entire Christendom led by London-based mission societies came to be seized of the urgency of finding an effective antidote to the attacks which Rammohun Roy had so successfully mounted upon the central Christian dogma of the Trinity. As Christianity is founded upon, and wedded to, Trinitarianismit would not give up its belief in three gods except at grave peril to its very existence. The Christian missions wanted, therefore, as the only other alternative available to them, to show somehow to the world at large that Hinduism was a polytheistic religious faith. Since the Hindus traced their monotheism back to the Veda, to the Rig in particular, it was considered absolutely necessary that the concocted evidence in order to be readily acceptable to the Hindus must have behind it the sanction and authority of the Rigveda. Once the decision had been made and the target fixed the only thing that remained to be done was to find the right marksman. The search for the right man continued till Max Muller happened to come their way.

George Bernard Shaw also had a fling at the three gods of Christianity: The missionary says that God is a magic number that is three in one and one in three," said the black girl.

That is simple" said the Arab; "for I am the son of my father and the father of my sons and myself to boot: three in one and one in three." (The Black Girl in Search of God, p. 63).

## 2 - The East India Company Hires Max Muller

In 1847, while he occupied the Boden Chair of Sanskrit at Oxford, Professor Wilson was perhaps the most eligible scholar to take up the job of translating the Rig or to suggest the name of another man who, in his opinion, might have done the job equally well. In Paris, Max Muller had been an understudy to Eugene Bournouf who was a noted scholar of the Zend. Bournouf had repeatedly impressed upon the young and ambitions mind of Max Muller the great importance of translating the Rigveda. It would thus be easily understood that the search that had been going on in London for the man who would do the job was not outside the knowledge of Bournouf. As a result Bournouf had in fact recommended Max Muller's name to Professor Wilson. When Max Muller went to London (1846) on his errand "Bunsen, in conjunction with Professor H.H. Wilson, prevailed upon the East India Company to undertake the expense of publication" of the translation of the Rigveda as a result of which alone the East India Company had agreed to hire (1847) Max Muller to do this job. How and why this choice fell on Max Muller (b. 1823—d. 1900), who was then an inexperienced man of less than 25 years, we shall soon examine at some length.

The East India Company hired Max Muller despite the fact that there were then in the field some other candidates who could have performed the task either better" or at least equally well. It is noteworthy that though some other candidates had been trying for some time to be given the same assignment it ultimately went to Max Muller who, by all known standards, was less qualified and only a new comer on the scene. In Max Muller's own words: "At last the long conflict is decided, and I have carried off, so to speak, the prize!" His excitement over his success in getting this assignment makes it abundantly clear that there was plenty of easy money in the bargain and that the struggle to pocket this easy money and been going on in London for quite some time.

The Christian missions had been nearly restless since the day when Rev. Adam, one of their tribe in Serampore (Calcutta), disclaimed any divinity for Jesus disassociating himself completely from the basic Christian dogma of the Trinity. That was not all. His joining hands with Rammohun Roy to preach (Vedantist) monotheism had made matters worse for Christian missions. As Rammohun Roy's arguments against Trinitarianism had given an all-damaging blow to the prestige of the rulers of India and of the Christian Church the missionaries had been clamouring for something to be done, and quickly, to restore this loss of prestige which they had so grievously suffered. Since the Church knew and realized it too well that Christianity would, and could, never give up the cult of the Trinity without inviting total disaster Christian missions wanted, as the only other option open to them, somehow to put on Hinduism a label of polytheism. And the Christian missions understood it full well that in order to be readily acceptable to the Hindus it must be done in a manner that it should have behind it the sanction and authority of their Dharamshasthras the foremost among which indisputably and undeniably was, and continues to be, the Rigveda. The task as it had been envisaged was gigantic. It required in addition large funds, continuous work and uninterrupted efforts stretching over a long number of years none of which the Christian missions of those days could manage on their own. Therefore, in order to overcome these formidable hurdles in their way they enlisted the support of the East India Company which at that time of history happened to control the enormous revenues of the Indian sub-continent.

The merchants of London who then controlled the East India Company had been repeatedly told that if they wanted to "greatly strengthen the foundations" of their government in India it was absolutely necessary for them somehow to convert the Hindus of India to Christianity. Since they were naturally very keen to perpetuate their stranglehold on the large Indian revenues this suggestion had carried an irresistible appeal to their commercial instincts. In consequence the directors of the East India Company ultimately threw in their lot with the Christian missions who promised to perform the miracle. The Company, after hesitating a while, agreed to provide, as a price for the services to be rendered to them, the much needed funds for the translation of the Rigveda from Vedic Sanskrita into English believing that this will go a long way in subverting Hinduism as a direct result of which they expected the 'Hindus to become Christians. Seemingly the directors would have argued among themselves that it was only the Indian money with which they were going to gamble and if all went well, as Wilberforce had predicted, they stood an excellent chance of reaping a rich harvest without either incurring any extra expense or exposing their trading interests to any additional hazards.

The East India Company which was a politico-commercial organization had agreed to enter the religious field of evangelization and proselytization from the back door apparently with the only purpose of safeguarding their commercial empire. None the less the directors knew it that the real object of translating the Rig was first to uproot Hinduism from India and then to implant in its place the religion of Christ. Since the operation christianization of India resembled very much a political operation aiming at sabotage and subversion it had to be carried out in strict secrecy. The entire operation was intended, therefore, to be carried out from behind a screen which was to be especially rigged up in the name of culture and philology so that no one might ever suspect the real motives and intentions of those who were to be engaged on the job.

The plot was laid out with great care and precision. As the approach had to be psychologically appropriate it was thought only prudent to entrust the job to someone who was not an Englishman. In consequence they picked up Max Muller who was a German and who did not belong even to the Anglican Church. By choosing a German who was a Lutheran the directors of the East India Company had played a very clever ruse which was to ensure a semblance of impartial objectivity to whatever Max Muller was in future to write or say on religious matters. They had particularly wanted Max Muller's diabolic and Jesuitical writings to look, at least to the more credulous and on the surface, free from the opprobrium of religious bias and political partisanship. And it has to be admitted that they succeeded a great deal in their mission of imparting to Maxmullerism a gloss and a polish of respectable credibility which nearly fooled all the world for more than fifty years.

The Rig is voluminous. It "consists of 1017 (1028) poems, 10,580 verses, and about 153,826 words." Anyone wanting to translate the Rig must of necessity be prepared to devote a long number of years to the job, and a translator who learnt Sanskrit only as his second language would normally be struggling harder at the job for a much longer period. The directors of the East India Company wanted, therefore, to assign the job only to him who, in their opinion, had a reasonable chance of completing the work in this lifetime. They knew also that the work of translating the Rig from Vedic-Sanskrita to English would draw heavily on the physical as well as mental resources of the incumbent. It would, they seemed to be fully conscious of the fact, amount, therefore, to almost indifferent planning if the man failed to complete the work in his lifetime apparently because he was already too old and advanced in years when he started. The man to be chosen to carry this arduous burden for a long distance of years had to be young and in good health. Max Muller seemed to fulfd all these conditions admirably well. He was not an Englishman and was only about 24 years old when in 1847 he was commissioned to carry out this all-important assignment. Yet another factor which had decisively worked in his favour and helped him to "carry off the prize" is often not very widely known. On reaching London he was introduced by Bunsen to ''Queen Victoria and the prince consort and to Oxford University." And this introduction had gone a long way in pushing a fledgling on the road to great fame as a great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita.

There were some other non-Englishmen who might have been picked up for doing this thpo-political masquerade. While Friedrich Maximillian Mueller belonged to the first batch of German students who had taken to the study of Sanskrita Herman Brockhaus was the other German who, like Max Muller, had devoted himself to the study of this great language. However, since Brockhaus already occupied the Chair of Sanskrit at Leipzig he was perhaps not much interested in being hired out. In addition Brockhaus was already 41 years old in 1847 and was not young enough to carry the burden to its destination. Eugene Bournouf was another scholar but more of the Zend. He lived in Paris and was already too old for this job. Rudolf Roth and Theodore Goldstucker were the other two contemporaries of Max Midler who might have filled the vacancy with equal merit. Franz Bopp was yet one more German who was known for his philological studies but he was already in his fifties when the East India Company decided in favour of Max Muller. Of all these Goldstucker was perhaps too honest to agree to do the dirty job in the manner it was intended to be done to serve Christian ends.

Rudolf von Roth had been known chiefly for the Sanskrit-German Dictionary (Sanskrit worter-buch) which was the result of collaboration among Professors (otto) Boahtlingk, Abrecht Weber, Roth himself and Kuhn. But for more than one reasons no one from among them was selected by the East India Company to do that job. First, because for having gone over to the Russian Imperial Academy and for having placed their services at its disposal they had virtually become persona non grata both with the English court and with the Company. Secondly, the Englishman still had not forgotten the attempts that had been made by Nicholas I to revive the political plans of Catherine II of Russia to extend and enlarge Russian political influence and power towards India and Constantinople. And all this apart the directors of the Company knew it too well in addition that the Germans had "made a determined attempt to find a footing in Bengal a hundred and fifty years ago" and for this reason ordinarily they could view no German with equanimity. This left Max Muller alone in the field whom the directors of the Company ultimately picked up for doing the job they had in mind.

There was then in London an Englishman who would have done the job equally well if not better. He was Professor H.H. Wilson who had returned from India in 1832 to become the first Boden Professor of Sanskrit at Oxford. Wilson had been left out for three very strong reasons. First, he was an Englishman and belonged to the race which after enslaving India now wanted also to convert her people to Christianity. Secondly, he was too old and perhaps already grown senile. In addition, since he already occupied the Boden Chair of Sanskrit no one seriously considered it possible for him to find sufficient time and energy to be able to do full justice to these two positions of great importance. Another Englishman, John Muir, who might have made an eligible candidate was ruled out because he was still in the service of the East India Company and thus was not free to devote his time to this new vocation.

The mischievous design under which Hinduism was to be declared a polytheistic religion had now all the actors ready to play the parts which had been assigned with great care to each one of them. The East India Company was to provide the much needed finance for the translation of the Rig and Max Muller was to lay the intellectual foundations of that infrastructure in which Christian missions later wanted to house Jesus Christ. Max Muller who had continuously suffered from want and youthful zeal and an insatiable ambition willingly agreed to prostitute his pen, intellect and scholarship for the filthy lucre the new job promised him in plenty. He soon launched himself upon the project with the zeal and devotion that can be expected only from a religious zealot. It must, however, be said to Max Muller's great credit that he did the job well and to the satisfaction both of himself and his hirers. He did his best to equate Hinduism with polytheism even though he had to invent for this purpose a new Jesuitical definition for the religion of the Rig.

Though Max Muller has often been projected and presented to the less informed and the more gullible Hindus as a benefactor of Hinduism the Hindu of today accepts all this only with a grain of salt. As Max Muller now stands unmasked the Hindu feels greatly relieved in his disillusionment because it has brought him nearer to truth. He now earnestly feels more than convinced, more than ever since after 1947 when India once again became free, that the establishment of several Max Muller Bbawans in different parts of India is a piece of political effrontery that has been merrily practised in Hindustan to resurrect the memory of a secular Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of Sanskrit scholarship with the only ignoble purpose of subverting Hinduism from within. For sufficiently strong and justifiable reasons the Hindus, particularly those who presently live in India, doubtlessly feel very much inclined to regard Max Muller at par with Nobili, Abbe Dubois and Carey with perhaps the only difference that Max Muller was able successfully to hide for about 50 long years his true identity under the secular gloss of scholarship.

It looks like a riddle that while in the past so many other scholars tried to translate the Vedas and although they happened to be pioneers in this field nearly all of them remained comparatively unknown. Even in professional circles their names are seldom heard mentioned in the course of discussions on the Vedas and their translations. On the contrary, Max Muller, who came much later on the scene and even though neither Sanskrit nor English happened to be his mother tongue so quickly shot into pre-eminence so soon to become world-famous. Hidden behind this meteoric success were some strong reasons which we shall try to unfold as we proceed with our narrative of Max Muller's lifelong masquerade.

The European scholars had started taking active interest in the Vedas only after Sir William Jones had drawn their attention to Sanskrita—the wonderful mother of all other languages — and its literature. But before some European scholars actually translated the Vedas or the Brahamanas a number of Indian scholars had already written commentaries (lika, and bhashya) on the Vedas. Among them Deva Swami who lived sometime before the Christian era is said to have written the first commentary on the Veda. Thereafter, several Indians wrote their commentaries on the Vedas e.g.

n/a Name Year Veda   1. Skanda Swami A.D.630 Rigveda   2. Narayana A.D.630 Rigveda   3. Udgitha A.D.630 Rigveda   4. Hastamalaka A.D.700 Rigveda   5. Venkata Madhava A.D.1050 Rigveda   6, Lakshmana A.D.1100 Rigveda   7. Dhanushkyajwa 13th Cent A.D. Rigveda   8. Ananda Tiratha A.D.1198-1278 Rigveda   n/a (Madhavacharya) n/a n/a   9. Atmanand A.D.1250 Rigveda   10. Sayana A.D.1315-1387 Rigveda   a. Ravana A.D.1450 Rigveda   12. Mudgala A.D.1413 Rigveda   13. Chatur Veda Swami 15th Cent, end Rigveda   14. Deva Swami Before Era Vikram Rigy'eda   15. Bhatta Bhaskar llth Cent A.D. Rigveda   16. Uwata A.D.1050 Rigveda   17. Swami Dayanand Saraswati A.D.1824-1883 Rigveda   18. Shaunaka — Yajurveda   19, Hari Swami Uwata A.D.638 Yajurveda   20. Guaradhara A.D.1300 Yajurveda   21. Mahidhara A.D.1660 Yajurveda   22. Swami Dayanand Saraswati A.D.1824-1893 Yajurveda   23. Madhava 7th Cent Vik. Samaveda   24. Bharat Swami A.D.1303 Samaveda   25. Sayana A.D.1315-1387 Samaveda   26. Surya Devajnya A.D.1530 (sic) Samaveda   27. Maha Swami 14th Cent A.D. (BC) Samaveda   28. Shobhakar Bhatta Before A.D.1408 Samaveda   29. Guna Vishnu 13th Cent. A.D. Samaveda   30. Sayana A.D.1315-1387 Atharvaveda

None of the Indians except perhaps Sayana ever became widely known and whatever fame he gained he owed it to the fact that most European scholars, Muller in particular, made use of his commentary as their vade-mecum. Next is Mahidhara who too came to be better known for a similar reason. Swami Dayanand Saraswati became famous (19th cent.) for the reason that he wrote his bhashya based entirely on principles laid down in Nighantu, Nirukta, Ashtadhyayi and Mahabhasya and more so because he willingly chose to write, in spite of himself having been a great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita, in Hindi, the language of the people. Nevertheless, none of these three Sayana, Mahidhara and Dayananda ever achieved the fame which Max Muller enjoyed in his lifetime.

Most European scholars, other than Max Muller, did the work of translation more for the love of it and because they felt a scholarly urge to do this work for the good of posterity. They had no godfathers to support them or to finance and subsidize the result of their endeavours. Some of them hailed from Germany which till then was not much respected for the reason that like England and France it could boast of no colonies. Since they wrote in German, a language which was not at all then popular, whatever they happened to write remained confined only to a very selected small circle of intellectuals. A few others wrote in French which was hardly spoken in India, the land of the Vedas. It was spoken and understood in Pondicherry, a small Indian territory, but the number of French speaking people was too small and insignificant to lend any fame either to French translations or to their translators.

Max Muller's reputation rested on a plank altogether different. He wrote in English at that time when English had become the language of the elite throughout an empire on which it was very vaingloriously claimed that the sun did not set! He was, in addition, supported covertly by British imperialism which then held under its yoke a very large number of colonies including India. When Max Muller started translating the Rig he was assured of his bread and butter for the next eight years and insecurity and uncertainty did not haunt him nor want stared him in the face as it had been the case with some other translators of the Vedas who had to struggle in this difficult field on their own. Since in addition Max Muller had the clandestine support of important and influential Christian missions he had at his back the combined might of all those Christian missionaries who wanted to convert the Hindus of India to Christianity. These missionaries readily and willingly broadcast to the world whatever Max Muller said and wrote and this went a long way to enhance Max Muller's prestige as a scholar.

As India had been held at this time in political bondage it was not very difficult for Max Muller to collect around him willing stooges and sycophants from among Indians some of whom willingly agreed to toe the same line as did Max Muller himself. When in 1857 the three Universities of Calcutta, Bombay and Madras came into being a number of Indians vied with one another for college and university professorships. Then things used to be so manipulated that only those who happened to be in the good books of influential Christian missionaries or who for some unknown reasons were especially recommended by them were given the chance. And anyone who seemed inclined to oppose Max Muller's pet theories and his interpretations of the Vedas or of Hinduism readily became a taboo with Indian universities which used to have as their chancellors and vice-chancellors Englishmen who were, or had sometime been, Christian missionaries — secular or regular.

The establishment of universities in India and, consequently, university and college libraries provided ready custom and outlet for books written by Max Muller and published in London. These libraries, most of them controlled by English theo-politicians, were under constant official and unofficial pressure to buy all these books for which funds were provided mostly out of the Indian revenues.

All this silently operated like a big racket which the Englishman's ingenuity had invented with the sole purpose of disseminating Max Muller's pernicious ideas against Hinduism among the young student community of India at no cost to England. As the leading British booksellers and publishers with their roots in London had their fingers in the pie they freely encouraged the sale of Max Muller's books because these brought them regular fat profits.

All this worked like a big cartel in which the alien rulers, the Christian Church and her mission agencies, the Indian universities and libraries and the so-called Indian branches of British publishers willingly or at times obsequiously participated boosting in the process the name of Max Muller as a great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita and of the Vedas. All this apart, it just happened that at the same time Swami Dayananda came out with his forthright and acerbic criticism of Max Muller's Sanskrit scholarship and his knowledge of the Vedas. Though it was not intended it nevertheless added immensely to Max Muller's fame. In short it might safely be said that Max Muller owed his world-wide reputation as a great scholar of the Vedas to three great factors — the spirit of the times; the great importance and profundity of the subject (the Rigveda) which Max Muller happened to handle and the strong indictment of Max Muller

Simultaneously the country had been growing politically much more powerful which fact had gone to the head of some of the more influential and powerful politicians and statesmen of England most of whom happened to be religious bigots and Christian zealots. All this considerably helped Max Muller in the execution of the project on which he was engaged.

According to a study made by Dr. Hoffman (Weltwirschaftliches Archiv, September 1934) British industrial production between the years 1713 and 1913 registered the highest landmark of 3.4 per cent growth between the years 1813 and 1875.

"We can take 1815 as the year in which thai development began which was destined to make Great Britain what she was at the end of the nineteenth century, the richest industrial country in the world." (How Strong Is Britain?, pp. 15-16). After the Indian war of independence (1857) Lord Palmerston declared (1859) to a deputation headed by the Archbishop of Canterbury: "We seem to be all agreed as to the end. It is not only our duty, but it is our interest to promote the diffusion of Christianity as far as possible throughout the lengh and breadth of India." (The Conversion of India, p. 233, emphasis added). Palmerston was the Prime Minister of England from 1855 to 1862 with a brief interruption and he was a great patron of Max Muller.

By Swami Dayananda

It was the East India Company that had hired Max Muller's services to do a particular job but in 1858 Company's commercial empire was taken over by the British government as a going concern with all its liabilities and assets. Since Max Muller's contract with the East India Company formed an important part in a significant field the British government wanted to reward Max Mullet for a work well done. When Max Muller came to known of this he wrote (May 13, 1875) to Dean Stanley saying:

"... I should like the Queen to known that I have now fulfilled the task which brought me to England in 1846! On my return to England I found a letter that Lord Salisbury had proposed that a further grant should be paid to me in recognition of my services in editing the Rig-veda.')

The letter is an eloquent testimony in proof of the secret agreement that had been reached in 1847 between the Directors of the East India Company and Max Muller. Max Muller clearly admitted, though perhaps only unwittingly, while in state of over exuberance, that he fulfilled the task which brought him to England! Can there be any doubt left after th's candid and voluntary confession by Max Muller that he had been hired to do a particular job in a particular manner to achieve particular ends and that this was somehow to pronounce to the world that the religion of the Rig was polytheistic. This task had brought him to England and, as he admitted, he had fulfilled his past as a party to the contract.

A deep sense of gratitude in a man is an unmistakable sign of a noble soul and there is no doubt that Max Muller possessed one and that on that account he suffered to the end of his life. When he was young and saw no ray of light coming from anywhere the English people gave him not only bread and butter but also a home and a satisfying vocation. Very gratefully he remembered all his life what he owed to England. Max Muller had felt indebted to the English people and he was so weighed down by this sense of gratitude that all his life he was never able again to raise his head and think like a free intellectual.In 1875, he acknowledged the fact to his own satisfaction that he had fulfilled the task which had brought him to England and as an honourable man he felt deep sense of satisfaction on having honoured his part of the commitment. More than once he thanked the English people in no uncertain terms. For instance once he said:

"If some of the young candidates for the Indian Civil Service who listened to my Lectures, quietly made up their minds that such a reproach shall be wiped out, if a few of them at least determined to follow in the footsteps of Sir William Jones, and to show to the world that Englishman who have been able to achieve by pluck, by perseverance, and by real political genius the material conquest of India, do not mean to leave the laurels of its intellectual conquest entirely to other countries, then I shall indeed rejoice, and feel that I have paid back, in however small a degree, the large debt of gratitude which I owe to my adopted country and to some of its greatest statesmen, who have given me the opportunity which I could find nowhere else of realising the dreams of my life, - the publication of the text and commentary of the Rigveda, the most ancient book of Sanskrit, aye of Aryan literature and now the edition of the translation of the Sacred Books of the East."

These lines from Max Muller's pen express profusely the feelings of a man of great honour who considered sense of gratitude to be a heavenly virtue. Not wanting to be known and remembered by coming generations as ungrateful he gave all that he could afford and the best that he possessed in return for so little which he received as pelf. The Englishman had fed Max Muller's body; in return Max Muller had given away his soul.

## 3 - Max Muller Meets Macaulay

Max Muller did not go to Paris to learn or study Sanskrit; he had gone there in search of a loaf of bread to keep the wolf away from his door. There he went to Bournouf who happened to know that a search had been going on in London for someone who would undertake to translate the Rigveda from Vedic-Sanskrita to English. While in Paris Max Muller managed his affairs only with the greatest difficulty; he lived precariously on the meagre income which he received only irregularly by collating and copying old manuscripts for some clients. That is why when Bournouf put him wise and advised him to go to London Max Muller at once saw in this suggestion a chance of a lifetime. He reached London in 1846 and within less than a year landed with a job which brought him both fame and fortune. In London (1846) he lived the life of an indigent suffering all the while acutely from want never very sure even of his two square meals. In his own words:

"... had not a penny left, and that in spite of every effort to make a little money, I should have had to return to Germany, had not Bunsen stood by me and helped by word and deed."

Baron Christian Karl Josias von Bunsen was the Prussian Minister to England till the year 1854. He was an ardent Christian who basked in the childishly pleasant thought of converting the whole world to Christianity. He wrote some books the most important of which were his Die Verfassung der Kirche der Zukunft (The Constitution of the Church of the Future) and Bible werke fur die Germeinde (Bible Commentary for the Community). There is every reason to believe that the plans of Christian missions to try to convert the Hindus of India to Christianity by subverting Hinduism from within were known to Bunsen who as a result and in order to assist these Christian missions and also to help Max Muller brought both together. And it was Bunsen who recommended Max Muller to the Queen of England.

It was on April 14, 1847 that the directors of the East India Company took their final decision to engage Max Muller for translating the Rigveda. The very next day Max Muller wrote a letter to his mother which shows unmistakably the state of Max Muller's mind at that moment. He had informed her (in translation):

"April 15, 1847 — From 5, Newman's Row, Lincoln Inn, Fields — At last the long conflict is decided, and I have carried off, so to speak, the prize! I can yet hardly believe that I have at last got what I have struggled for so long, entire independence, and I am filled with the thought of how much more I have gained than I deserved. ... I am to hand over to the Company, ready for the press, fifty sheets each year — the same I had promised to Samter in Germany; for this I have asked £ 200 a year, £ 4 a sheet. They have been considering the matter since December, and it was only yesterday that it was officially settled. I have to read the corrections, and shall have plenty of time left to devote to my studies.. .. As the work will be above 400 sheets, I have a certain position for the next eight years, and the work is really so light I could take another post with it. This in fact has been already offered me, i.e. a place as Librarian at the British Museum, with £ 150 a year. But on Bunsen's advice I have refused this. . . . And now what do you say, dearest mother? Is it not more than I could have ever expected? ... But only think I had not a penny left, and that in spite of every effort to make a little money, I should have had to return to Germany had not Bunsen stood by me and helped by word and deed.."

In his letter to his mother Max Muller made more than one specific points viz. (1) he gained more than he had deserved and ever expected; (2) he had tried for similar assignment in Germany but his services were turned down for being considered too costly; (3) this assignment gave him a job for the next eight years; (4) he was to be paid £ 200 a year and in return he had to get ready for print only one sheet in a little over a week and (5) he considered the work for which he was to be paid so handsomely really so light that he could easily accept additional work. It shows how casually Max Muller treated the extremely important and difficult job which he had been called upon to do. He seemed to be convinced that he could not only trifle with it but also get away with it irrespective of the manner in which he chose to deliver the goods.

In the year 1847 Max Muller was about 25 years old and had to his credit a study of Sanskrit covering a short period of six years! Even though neither Sanskrit nor English was his mother tongue he thought the work of translating the Rig from the former to the latter really easy and lightl He was to be paid for this work an amount he had neither expected nor deserved. The handsome and heavy payment that had been agreed to be made to him for this work was, in fact, considered by some critics as a piece of sophisticated jobbery. This was not all. He pretended the job to be very light and easy despite the incapacitating migraine from which he suffered almost regularly and which it times made it nearly impossible for him to concentrate on any substantial work. Sometimes his headaches were so severe that these completely prevented him from attending to any work at all. In 1852, he actually fell ill and Bunsen who was his mentor in London advised him to engage an assistant for translating the Rig and Max Muller realizing his own plight had readily accepted Bunsen's suggestion. In consequence Muller wrote to Dr. Aufrecht, another German, whom Bunsen had in fact recommended, offering him the position. Dr. Aufrecht agreed and practically lent his hand at translating the Rigveda.

Max Muller had indeed been very fortunate to have arrived on the scene at a time when Christian missions had been nearly restive asking for somethings to be done and done quickly to restore the prestige of Jesus Christ in the eyes generally of Indians and particularly of the Hindus. Ultimately the choice had fallen on Max Muller. And what type of a scholar was this man, Max Muller?:

"Though undoubtedly a great scholar, Max Muller did not so much represent scholarship pure and simple as her hybrid types -the scholar-author and scholar-courtier. In the former capacity, though manifestingly little of the originality of genius, he endered vast service by popularizing high truths among high minds. In his public and social character he represented Oriental studies with a brilliancy, and conferred upon them a distinction, which they had not previously enjoyed in Great Britain. There were drawbacks in both respects: the author was too prone to build upon insecure foundations, and the man of the world incurred censure for failings which may perhaps be best indicated by the remark that he seemed too much of a diplomatist."

Starvation had been staring in Max Muller's face too long. The student-Orientalist in him had wanted, therefore, to lay his hands on something that would sustain him in life. The diplomatist in him would not, in consequence, let a chance of a lifetime to gain fame and earn fortune easily slip through his fingers no matter how fit or unfit he might have been for the job which he was asked to do. As nothing would appear unethical or wrong to a starving man if something gave him a chance to make a decent living Max Muller agreed to put in his contribution for uprooting Hinduism. He settled down in Oxford making it ultimately his home in England. The youthful scholar from Germany had now a specific and well defined assignment. He was somehow to prove to the world, or at least to the Hindus of India, that Hinduism was a polytheistic religious faith and, in that process, wherever possible, to project the image of Jesus Christ; of Christianity and of the Bible. The news that a scholar capable for achieving all these goals had been discovered was swiftly carried to all Christian capitals of the world. And as Max Muller soon realized that fame and fortune appeared to be well within his reach he started opening his mouth and weilding his pen in the service of the great mission he had undertaken as a mercenary.

At times, letters which a man writes in his lifetime reveal him to coming generations in such nakedness as nothing else would. This happens more often when he sits down to unload his mind during a period of his life when he happens to be suffering incorrigibly from the ecstatic feeling of having succeeded against and in spite of heavy odds. Max Muller who happened to be a prolific writer has been no exception. He exchanged in his lifetime with some important people of his time a large correspondence in some of which at times he really let himself go. As a result he now stands so fully damned on the strength of some of his own writings that no amount of gerrymandering would ever free him from the grave charge of having been a Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the secular mask of scholarship. Nevertheless, the fact that he was a scholar of unusually high calibre cannot be denied but being a mercenary his undoing lay in his abject inability to be mentally free either to accept truth or pronounce it to the world.

In the year 1851, Max Muller happened to meet Macaulay for the first time at a party in London. He mentioned this meeting to his mother in a letter dated "Oxford, 27 June, 1851" telling her (in translation):

"I went to London lately to a party at Bunsen's, most interesting, where I met Macaulay, the historian .. . ," He met Macaulay the second time in December 1855 when he had with him a long interview. In between these two years the Crimean war had begun involving England in it as an active and important belligerent political power. As the British armies had to be familiarized with the languages spoken in the theatre of war Sir Charles EdwardTrevelyan, sought Max Muller's assistance in collecting information about languages spoken by people in those areas. Responding readily and obsequiously Max Muller speedily produced a pamphlet, Suggestions for the Assistance of Officers in Learning the Languages of the Seat of War in the East, which quite surprisingly ran into its second edition within a year. "In his introductory letter to Sir Charles he first called attention to a subject that continued to occupy his thoughts almost to the end of his life." Max Muller had written:

"It is undoubtedly high time that something should be done to encourage the study of Oriental languages in England. At the very outset of this war, it has been felt how much this branch of studies - in emergencies like the present so requisite has been neglected in the system of our education. In all other countries which have any political, commercial, or religious connections with the East, provision has been made by Government, or otherwise, to encourage youngmen to devote themselves to this branch of studies. Russia has always been a most liberal patron of Oriental philology. In the Academy of St. Petersburg there is a Chair for every branch of Oriental literature. The French Government has founded a school, "L'ecole pour les langues orientales vivantes." At Vienna there is an Oriental seminary, Prussia finds it expedient to give encouragement to young Oriental scholars, employed afterwards with advantages as consuls and interpreters. In England alone, where the most vital interests are involved in a free intercourse with the East, hardly anything is done to foster Oriental studies."

More than one influential Englishmen had carried a feeling for some time that Max Muller had more than a soft corner in his heart for Oriental languages and in particular for Sanskrit. When Max Mullet's pamphlet appeared his love for Sanskrit no longer remained a secret. It created the impression that he had been trying first to ingratiate himself with politicians who wielded power and then to secure for Oriental languages a place of honour in the English educational system. Macaulay who seemed to have an in-built dislikefor Oriental learning, felt offended more strongly than anyone else. He nearly resented and abhorred the idea of someone, especially a fledgling from outside England, meddling with the English system of education. He was stubbornly opposed to anyone attempting to undo what he had done for English language. Very patiently and designedly he lay in ambush waiting for an opportunity to offer itself so that he might effectively act to destroy Max Muller's love for Oriental languages. The pamphlet gave Macaulay the necessary excuse to pounce upon the German scholar of Sanskrit and to maul and vanquish him.

Max Muller's Suggestions for the Assistance of Officers had already turned Macaulay inwardly very hostile toward the Sanskritist from Germany. When in June 1855 Max Muller was placed on the commission for the examination of the candidates for the Civil Service of India, and appointed examiner in Sanskrit" Macaulay felt highly incensed. Perhaps this happened to be the proverbial last straw on the camel's back which nettled Macaulay to a degree that he was able no longer to contain himself. He made up his mind to strike which he did when in December 1855, at his instance, Max Muller went to Macaulay's rooms in Albany to meet the great rhetorician.

Macaulay first pretended to discuss with Max Muller "the new regulations for Indian Civil Service, so far as they related to the teaching of Oriental subjects and languages." Max Muller who still suffered from his youthful zeal and carried at least within his heart a genuine love for Sanskrit had gone to meet Macaulay enthusiastically prepared and fully armed with all the arguments that he could command to plead the case for Oriental languages. But little did he realize that in Macaulay he was going to face a mulish Christian and a rabid enemy of Oriental languages and literature. Nor perhaps was he sufficiently conscious of the historical fact, and its Christian background, that it was Macaulay who had imposed on the Indian people English language with the covert purpose of language being used as a vehicle for converting people to Christianity.

The second interview which he had with Macaulay was described by Max Muller in his AuId Lang Syne (1898) recording that Macaulay not only did all the talking all the time but in the end he also dismissed Max Muller in a manner which was highly peremptory. And it was more than likely that Macaulay treated the German scholar and a lover of Sanskrit with his usual disdainful arrogance which was almost congenital with him. In a letter to his mother Max Muller described to her the outcome of this infamous interview. He said ruefully (in translation):

"... I made acquaintance this time in London with Macaulay, and had a long conversation with him on the teaching necessary for the young men who are sent out to India. He is very clear headed, and extraordinarily eloquent ... I went back to Oxford a sadder, and, I hope, a wiser man.

Max Muller's letter to his mother reveals the anguish of a young lad who had been slighted and humbled just for having been too open-mouthed. At the time of this interview Macaulay, a veteran politician of 55 years and the greatest rhetorician of his time, was already basking in the leisure and fortune he had earned in India while Max Muller still only about 32 years old was yet not very sure of his future. While Macaulay triumphantly gloated on the high reputation he enjoyed for having served Christianity and the English people Max Muller was still working hard on an alien soil to feel his way in the difficult field of Sanskrit scholarship.

Macaulay must have given to Max Muller, who was no match for the Englishman, such heavy and alternate doses of hope and despair that the scholar extraordinary would have felt utterly confused and flabbergasted. Max Muller would have nearly reeled under the strong impact of Macaulay's rhetorics and psychological onslaughts. Max Muller knew neither what to say nor how to put across his views to meet one argument after another which Macaulay must have hurled at him in quick volleys. In the end without ever giving Max Muller a chance to open his mouth Macaulay dismissed the scholar extraordinary after he had completely overwhelmed and vanquished him with his pet arguments against Oriental languages accompanied perhaps with mouthfuls of froth.

Macaulay would have assuredly pointed out to Max Muller the folly of his thinking and also of the possible harm his advocacy for Oriental languages might do to the theo-political interests of the Christian rulers of India. He would have thrown also a subtle hint to Max Muller how ungrateful in that case he would be to the English people who had given him a career and a home. Finding all exits sealed Max Muller fell an easy prey to the force of circumstances and capitulated. There is no doubt that Max Muller was made to foresee the possibly frightful consequences that might follow if he continued to persist in his foolish advocacy of Oriental languages. As Max Muller could hardly afford to be valorous he chose to be discreet keeping his mouth shut perhaps very much against his wish.

Max Muller had returned to Oxford sadder because he had lost the battle for a cause which he held so dear to his heart but wiser because he had learnt for the first time how best to protect his worldly interests. In consequence Max Muller sacrificed the scholar in him to salvage the worldly man engaged in Oxford in the work of uprooting Hinduism. In pocketing these unmerited insults which Macaulay heaped in plenty upon him Max Muller was able, nevertheless, to save for himself his English bread and butter to which both he must have by this time got addicted.

There is no reason to believe that Max Muller was either a naive or that he had no convictions of his own. Far from either. He was like that man who by placing himself under gratitude in time of his utter need had forfeited the right to speak the truth. He lay prostrate under pressure of circumstances which proved too strong for him to resist and which any other man too might have found equally difficult to withstand. He was helpless but his helplessness would in no measure absolve him from the reproach that he prostituted his scholarship for the English crumbs which the East India Company threw regularly in his lap.
