As a result he turned a more active member of the theo-political caucus which was then busy in planning to perpetrate a big literary fraud on the Hindus of the world.

Max Muller was a scholar of extraordinary ability but unfortunately he was a man who constantly suffered from want with only very little means of his own to fall back upon. He depended woefully on the hospitality of others for his weal which made him a near bondsman of all those who went to his n scue in times of his stress. All this stripped Max Muller, a scholar extraordinary of a very high calibre and of unusual brilliance, of manly independence of his views transferring him into a stooge of that clique to whom he stood obliged for favours he had accepted. That he had bartered away his freedom to think and act independently was a stigma which he carried deep down in his heart all his lifetime. Much before he came to be known either as a Sanskritist or as a philologist he had committed himself to Christian mission agencies and in the process had reduced himself to the position of that accursed gardener who, much against his wish, had to pledge his trees before they started bearing fruits of his labour.

Max Muller was fully conscious of the highly deplorable situation in which he had unwittingly landed himself and from which he now saw no hope of escaping unharmed. He had lost his freedom which is the only priceless possession of an intellectual. He realized it too well that in the bargain that he had struck with the East India Company he had lost something which he could not retrieve no matter how hard he afterwards tried. This made Max Muller really a very sad man deep down in his heart. He gave expression to his pent up feelings in a letter that he wrote to Keshub Chunder Sen. His words smack of an anguish greater than one would ever be able to see on the surface. He seemed very much distressed when he made the painful confession that he never had that independence which an average Englishman generally enjoyed. To quote him:

"Stanley had one great advantage in starting in life, an advantage which is not sufficiently appreciated by those who possess it, because it is rightly considered very honourable if those who do not possess it surmount the difficulties of life without ever forfeiting their self-respect. Stanley belonged to a good family, and w&s always peculiarly independent. He never knew the temptations of a poor struggling man, who has often to choose between sacrificing the chances of obtaining a position of usefulness and influence in the world, to which he has a good right, and making certain concessions which, howsoever small, colour his character through life. A man who has once been silent when he ought to have spoken, or who has ever spoken when he ought to have been silent, will never be the same again, not perhaps in the eyes of the world which calls speech silver and silence gold, but in his own eyes, if he has eyes to see. And these temptations are great, so great that we ought to be very lenient in judging a poor man who may see actual want, loss of influence, loss of usefulness on one side, while one small concession to the world would lead him to the bench of Judges or Bishops, or any other place for which he is the fittest man.

There is one expression in English which I have always liked very much. 'He has an independence,' that is, he has sufficient to live without caring for frowns and favours."

Max Muller had plenty of worldly wisdom and he chose his words with great care and preferred to be circumspect when he wrote his letters, At times he was not averse even to practising equivocation if the circumstances so demanded. However, when he wrote to Keshub Chunder Sen after Stanley's death in July 1881 he came nearly to showing up himself completely, after having suffered in silence for more than thirty years. He understood the situation full well and on that account he must have suffered in his heart a pain which at times he found unbearable. In return for what the East India Company gave him he had surrendered his independence which as a German he valued most.

Max Muller profited very little because while he gathered pelf in accepting the Company's assignment he had, in the bargain, lost his soul. He lamented in his later years that he had given away too much for too little. He was very certain that whatever he did, or howsoever he tried, it was not possible for him again to be the same free man and much less a free scholar. He resembled that unfortunate orphan girl who after having been rescued from starvation later, on blossoming out into full womanhood, has to allow herself to be ravished, much against her will, by her benefactor in order only to given satisfaction for the debt of gratitude she owed him. In fact' Max Muller once did complain that he was "only tolerated at Oxford" feeling that he had been "at Oxford on sufferance." This had indeed been too true because a hireling would seldom command respect beyond receiving the price he accepts for the services he contracts to render for the payment made to him.

In India very few people know much about Lord (Thomas Babington) Macaulay beyond perhaps the two facts that he gave India its Penal Code and that it was he who imposed English language on India. As the fascinating story of Max Muller might remain incomplete we are compelled here to make a digression to throw sidelight on Macaulay.

Thomas Babington Macaulay, to give him his full name, was born in 1800 and was educated at Cambridge. His father, Zachary Macaulay, was a colleague of William Wilberforce who had been the leader in the British House of Commons of the Evangelical Party known also &s the Clapham Sect for more than 30 years. Macaulay's mother, Selina Mills, was the daughter of a Quacker and his grandfather was Rev. John Macaulay, a Presbyterian minister. In his childhood Macaulay had been "severely educated in the rigid Galvanism of what was known as the Clapham Sect." This education appears to have given young Macaulay a strong bias in favour of Christianity to the exclusion of all other religions — a prejudice from which he was not able to free himself even when he had grown into a matured man of 55 years. In 1833, when the Charter of the East India Company was renewed the post of the fourth member of the Supreme Council for India had been especially created for him to fill it as its Law Member. In consequence he went to India in the year 1834 landing in Madras on the 10th June.

In his childhood Thomas Babington Macaulay came in frequent contact with Wilberforce who aj»ɱa friend of young Macaulay's father often used to visit them regularly. Wilberforce who had been ardently advocating for all efforts to be made to convert Indians to Christianity was also of the view that nothing would aid this conversion more than the introduction of English language as the medium of instruction in Indian schools. In March 1835, English was arbitrarily imposed on India by Macaulay and this continues to remain till this day an event of great historical importance.

At first Macaulay had threatened to resign if his recommendations on language were not accepted but it was only a polished spoof. A clever man who had been longing and manoeuvring to go to India to be able to put his finances in good shape would not ever seriously think of jettisoning his chances of making an easy fortune and return home travelling twice, in less then 250 days, over a long distance of several thousand miles which separated India from England. Before he left for India Macaulay had written a letter to his sister, Hannah, which tells very vividly how much he had been craving to go India for making money. He had said in his letter:

"The advantages are very great. It is a poSt of the highest dignity and consideration. The salary is ten thousand pounds a year. I am assured by persons who know Calcutta intimately, ... that I may live in splendour there for five thousand a year, and may save the rest of the salary with the accruing interest. I may therefore return to England at only thirty-nine, in the full vigour of life, with a fortune of thirty thousand pounds. "... I can live only by my pen; and it is absolutely impossible for any man to write enough to procure him a decent subsistence,.....

Now, in order to live like a gentleman, it would be necessary for me to write; not as I have done hitherto, but regularly, and even daily. I have never made more than two hundred a year by my pen. ... The prospects of our family are, if possible, darker than ever.

"In the meantime my political outlook is very gloomy. A schism in the ministry is approaching.... chance of keeping my present position for six months is so small, that I would willingly sell it for fifty pounds down .... In England I see nothing before me, for some time to come, but poverty, unpopularity; and the breaking up of old connections."

It requires no special wit to understand that when Macaulay was given a chance to go to India he was already on the brink of financial disaster and his political career was heading towards utter ruin. His threat to resign in February 1835, in case his recommendations on English language were not accepted, was, therefore, only a piece of clever chicanery. The appointment which he got with a salary of ten thousand pounds a year was in itself an exercise in political jobbery which had been adversely commented upon by a man like Professor H.H. Wilson who then occupied the Boden Chair of Sanskrit at Oxford. Wilson had commented: "The power of legislating for all persofas and for all courts of justice was advantageously vested in the supreme Government; but it might be doubted whether the association of the Chief Justice as a legal member of the council would not have more effectively and economically answered the purpose, than the special appointment of an individual from England unfamiliar with the law or the practice of the Indian courts and recommended by no remarkable forensic qualifications."

That Macaulay's appointment was only a piece of theo-political jobbery to satisfy ecclesiastical needs of Christian missions is abundantly clear from another letter which Macaulay wrote to Hannah, his sister, on October 21,1833. In this letter Macaulay made some very significant disclosures:

"My dear Sister, — Grant is here at least, and we have had a very long talk about matters both public and private. The Government wanted to support my appointment; but he expects violent opposition from the Company. He mentioned my name to the Chairs, and they were furious. . . . They put their opposition on the ground of my youth, - a very flattering objections to a man who this week completes his thirty-third year.

The question now is whether their opposition will be supported by the other Directors. If it should be so, I have advised Grant most strongly to withdraw my name, to put-up some other man, and then to fight the battle to the utmost. We shall be suspected of jobbing if we proceed to extremities on behalf of one of ourselves; but we can do what we like, if it is in favour of some person whom we cannot be suspected of supporting from interested motives."

The training which Macaulay had received in his more impressionable years had imparted to him a religious malice which in his later years he was able successfully to contrive to conceal only behind his English education and rhetorics. As he came to know that he had been selected for the new post Macaulay had felt so overjoyed that soon he felt compelled to address his constituents of Leeds who had elected him as their representative. The manner and the language in which he chose to do it completely exposed him as a Christian zealot who was getting ready to go to India as if for the only purpose of bringing about the conversion of the Hindus of India to Christianity.

In that moment of exuberance Macaulay had poured out his mind in that address like a megalomaniac. However, very surprisingly some very important portions of his address were kept out of Macaulay's biography for which there seems to be no valid explanation except that it had been done on purpose to prevent posterity from knowing some of Macaulay's ugly Christian proclivities. What he told his Leeds constituents is interestingly revealing arid we produce it all here:

"To the Electors of Leeds. Gentlemen, — It is well known to you that the great Corporation to which Parliament has entrusted the Government of our Indian Empire, has appointed me to one of the highest posts in its service; that His Majesty has been graciously pleased to confirm the appointment — that I have accepted it — and that in very short time I shall proceed to the scene of my new labours.

I have lately enjoyed the pleasure of conversing with several of my most respected constituents, and I have been truly gratified to learn that my conduct on this occasion has obtained their approbation. They justly consider it as honourable to themselves that their representative should be freely selected by a body, which wants neither the spirit nor the power to resist unreasonable dictation on the part of the Crown, to fill one of the most important offices of the Empire.

I trust that I shall carry with me the esteem of my constituents, and that in my new situation I shall not forfeit their esteem. In Asia, as in Europe, the principles which recommended me to your favour shall be constantly present to my mind. While legislating for a conquered race, to whom the blessings of our constitution cannot as yet be safely extended, and to whom the benignant influence of our religion is unknown, I shall never forget that I have been a legislator chosen by the unforced and uncorrupted voices of a free, an enlightened, and Christian people.

I this day return into your hands the high trust with which you have honoured me. It was obtained by no unworthy arts. It has been used for no unworthy ends. I owed it to your free and unsolicited choice. I have endeavoured to employ it for what appeared to me your real good. My conscious tells me that I have been honest servant, and I owe to you this attestation, that you have been most indulgent and reasonable masters. You will bear me witness that I have never shrunk from speaking the truth, and I can bear witness that you have been always willing to hear it. When we have differed, I have never evaded your questions, n6f have you clamoured down my answers. We have endeavoured to convince each other by a fair interchange of reasons, and if we still continue to differ, we have differed as friends.

I can form no better wish for your borough — and it is well entitled to the best wishes that I can form — than that it may maintain the honourable character which it has won. // now that I have ceased to be your servant, and am only your sincere and grateful friend, I may presume to offer you advice which must, at least, be allowed to be disinterested, I would say to you: Act towards your future representatives as you have acted towards me. Choose them, as you have chosen me, without canvassing and without expense.

Encourage them, as you encouraged me, always to speak to you fearlessly and plainly. Never suffer your great and independent town to be turned into an East Retford or Newark. Reject, as you have hitherto rejected, the wages of dishonour. Defy, as you have hitherto defied, the threats of petty tyrants. Never forget that the worst and most degrading species of corruption is the corruption which operates, not by hopes, but by fears. Cherish those noble and virtuous principles for which we have struggled and triumphed together — the principles of liberty and toleration, of justice and order. Support, as you have steadily supported, the cause of good governments; and may all the blessings which are the natural fruits of good government descend upon you and be multiplied to you an hundred fold! May your manufactures flourish; may your riches increase! May the works of your skill, and the signs of your prosperity, meet me in the furthest regions of the East, and give me fresh cause to be proud of the intelligence, the industry, and the spirit of my constituents.

And now, gentlemen, it remains for me only to bid you farewell — to wish to you all, to my supporters and to my opponents, health, prosperity, and happiness, and to assure you that to the latest day of my life I shall look back with pride and pleasure on the honourable connection which has subsisted between us."

I have the honour to be, Gentlemen,

Your faithful friend and Servant,

T.B. Macaulay,

London, February 4,1834."

In his address Macaulay had exulted in the knowledge that he had been elected by "a free, an enlightened, and Christian people." Bloated with messianic pride and in true fashion of a hard-boiled and incorrigible imperialist he bragged only as a Christian missionary would before hio select audience whom he expects to do nothing in approval but applaud. He had readily owned that while legislating for a conquered race, to whom the blessings of English Constitution could not yet be safely extended and to whom the benignant influence of Christian religion was unknown, he shall never forget that he had been a legislator chosen by Christian people. He made these claims on behalf of Christianity, Christian civilization and his own strict adherence to that religion which in his opinion was benignant. He, therefore, told them though only obliquely, that he shall never forget to legislate for the conquered race (Indians) in a manner that would abet their conversion to Christianity so that they too might, after conversion, benefit by becoming followers of the same benignant religion!

Soon after reaching Calcutta (September 25) Macaulay had got busy with his plans which he had been hatching in London in active collusion with some other members of the Clapham Sect and in particular with the connivance of Grant and Wilberforce. Macaulay had gone to India with preconceived ideas and a mind that had already been made up: he had somehow to introduce English language as the medium of instruction in Indian schools. It had been very much within his knowledge that the senior members of the Clapham Sect had since long been pleading and planning for the introduction of European education in combination with Christian doctrines so that Indians could more easily be made to accept the religion of Christ.

In February 1835, Macaulay took the fateful step and made English language the compulsory medium of instruction in all Indian schools. This needs no emphasis to prove that all this was premeditated. In a letter which Macaulay wrote from Calcutta to his father he had admitted, quite brusquely indeed, that the purpose of English language schools was in essence to bring about the conversion of high-caste Hindus. Very gleefully he told his father:

"Our English schools are flourishing wonderfully. We find it difficult, — indeed, in some places impossible, — to provide instructions for all who want it. At the single town of Hoogly fourteen hundred boys are learning English. The effect of this education on the Hindoos is prodigious. No Hindoo, who has received an English education, ever remains sincerely attached to his religion. Some continue to profess it as a matter of policy; but many profess themselves pure Deists, and some embrace Christianity. -" is my firm belief that if, our plans of education are followed up, there will not be a single idolater among the respectable classes in Bengal thirty years hence. And this will be effected without any efforts to proselytise; without the smallest interference with religious liberty; merely by the natural operation of knowledge and reflection. I heartily rejoice in the prospects-----

Within half a year after the time when you read this we shall be making arrangements for our return.... I feel as if I never could be unhappy in my own country; as if to exist on English ground and among English people, seeing the old familiar sights and hearing the sound of my mother tongue, would be enough for me.... Ever Yours most affectionately, T.B. Macaulay."

Though it is not very widely appreciated it remains a fact that Macaulay had influenced Max Muller's young mind and his thinking decisively during the second interview which Max Mullet had with him in December 1855. As a Lutheran perhaps Max Muller was as rigid as was Macaulay but being the member of the race who then ruled India Macaulay was unmistakably less accommodating and more rabid in matters of religion. Whoever studied with some care the working of Max Muller's mind would easily notice and understand that after the year 1855 when Max Muller received long briefing and chastisement from Macaulay, his writings took a different trend inasmuch as he became more active and less inhibited in praise of Christianity; of the Bible and of Jesus Christ. Though it remains very much a moot point it is very likely that if Max Muller had not met Macaulay he might have been less dogmatic and arbitrary in his writings and speeches.

## 4 - Max Muller Reveals Himself as a Christian Zealot

Macaulay believed, perhaps sincerely, that as a result of introduction of English language as the sole medium of instruction in Indian schools the entire high-caste Hindu population of Bengal will fall at the feet of Christ in the next 30 years i.e. by the year 1866. One of the chief characteristic of Christian missionaries "is the love of maximizing and belauding all their own efforts, in order to secure the advantages of their position" and Macaulay's thinking was only symbolic of this common missionary trait. Macaulay was naturally very eager to convey to the rulers and to the Christian missions in London an impression that by one stroke of his pen he had given Hinduism a grevious blow from which it would be difficult to recover. It was in 1855 that Max Muller met Macaulay the second time but little had he then realized that he had met a man who, though a near genius, had a warped mind. Nevertheless, the interview seems to have left an indelible impact on Max Muller whom Macaulay appeared to have won over completely to his side. Max Muller's translation of the Rig had appeared between 1848 and 1874 but most of his other writings were completed after 1855 which show clearly in which direction his mind had started moving after that fateful year. Though even before 1855 Max Muller had been on the side of Christian missions after 1855 his Christianish attitudes had become markedly more aggressive. Despite his constant endeavours to hide his moves behind the secular mask of scholarship he too often gave himself away as a Christian proselytizer and evangelist. The thin veneer and polish of philology which he painstakingly gave to his writings did not help him sufficiently to conceal his true identity of a secular Christian missionary. As early as 1850 (the letter bears the words; "9 Park Place, Oxford, Sunday, July 1850" but gives no exact date) he had written a letter to (Chevalier) Bunsen saying:

"Your Excellency, — With regard to Empson's and Dr. Wilson's letters, it is difficult to advise. I have no doubt whatever, that something can be written about the Veda which would reach even the dullest ears. Whether Dr. Wilson can undertake that task is another question. You know the dry hard shall in which the Veda is presented to us, and which seems still harder and more wooden in the English translation. Nevertheless I of course shall be glad if the Rigveda is dealt with in the Edinburg Review, and if Wilson would write from the standpoint of a missionary, and would show how the knowledge and bringing into light of the Veda would upset the whole existing system of Indian theology, it might become of real interest."

The youthful German scholar of Sanskrita had been at his job hardly for about 39 months when he chose to open his mouth to give this wily suggestion to Bunsen. What did he really advise? He would be glad if Wilson agreed to write something, anything, about the Veda from the standpoint of a (Christian) missionary. It is not difficult at all to understand a Christian missionary's standpoint towards other religions. Though there are and there must always be some worthy exceptions a Christian missionary, no matter to which brand of Christianity a missionary owed his allegiance, enjoys decrying, denigrating and denouncing all other religions to the exclusion of his own. He is friendly to no other religious faith except to his own brand and chop. If ever he finds himself out-numbered and in a not too hospitable surrounding he at once becomes circumspect to lie low to bide his time; whenever he finds himself on a surer ground he turns openly hostile.

From the very beginning of his career as a translator of the Rig Max Muller had manifested his Christian missionary characteristics. In his lifetime he exchanged with different people a large number of letters some of which, though he did not then realize, have laid him bare before posterity in his full ugliness as a committed lobbyist for Christianism. Max Muller almost ventured to foretell that if Wilson wrote something about the Veda from the standpoint of a missionary it will surely upset the entire existing system of Hindu religion. And in nothing else did Max Muller seem to find greater delight and solace than in being able to upset and uproot Hindu religion.

Perhaps all men can do any work but each one of them can do one particular job better than many others. When they find this work they usually put their heart and soul into it because they feel convinced to have found a vocation for which they find themselves to be suited most. And when Max Muller got the assignment to translate the Rig he guessed he had found the work for which he had been looking around since long. But it seems he soon discovered that the job was too big for him. It had started to bore him down mainly because he was incapable of understanding and comprehending correctly the meaning and substance of Veda mantras. As a missionary and more so as a bourgeois who loved very much to live by his tongue and on other people's money he soon grew so exasperated that he wrote to Bunsen a letter which reveals his mind. He said (in translation):

"55 St. John Street, Oxford, August 25, 1856 Whether shall be able to do this is doubtful, for without my love for antiquity and the past, my dreams for the future return again and again, and I feel somewhat drawn to India — a desire difficult to resist in the end. Only I do not know how to get there; but my life here seems so aimless and unfruitful that I shall not be able to bear it for very much longer. I thought the other day whether I could not manage to go to India with the Maharaja Dhulip Singh. He is very well spoken of, and he returns next year after having learnt in England what good things he would do some day for his fatherland in India. It seems to me it would form the natural nucleus of a small Indo-Christian colony, and it is only necessary to create such a centre in order to exercise one's power of attraction on all sides. After the last annexation the territorial conquest of India ceases — what follows next is the struggle in the realm of religion and of spirit, in which, of course, centre the interests of the nations. India is much ripe for Christianity than Rome or Greece were at the time of St. Paul. The rotten tree has for some time had artificial supports, because its fall would have been inconvenient for the government. But if the Englishmen comes to see that the tree must fall, sooner or later, then the thing is done, and he will mind no sacrifice either of blood or of land. For the good of this struggle I should like to lay down my life, or at least to lend my hand to bring about this struggle. Dhulip Singh is much at Court, and is evidently destined to play a political part in India. I wish I could get in touch with him in some quite natural way. Could it be managed with the help of Prince Albert or would you help me to it? I do not at all like to go to India as a missionary, that makes one dependent on the parsons; nor do I care to go as a Civil Servant, as that would make me dependent on the government. I should like to live for ten years quite quietly and learn the language, try to make friends, and then see whether I was fit to take part in the work, by means of which the old mischief of Indian priestcraft could be overthrown and the way opened for the entrance of simple Christian teaching, that entrance which this teaching finds into every human heart, which is freed from the enslaving powers of priests and from the obscuring influence of philosopfiers. Whatever finds root in India soon overshadows the whole of Asia, and nowhere could the vital power of Christianity more gloriously realize itself than if the world saw it spring up there for a second time, in a very different form than in the West, but still essentially the same.

Much more could be said about this; a wide world opens before one, for which it is well worth while to give one's life. And what is to be done here? here in England? here in Oxford? — nothing but to help polish up a few ornaments on a cathedral which is rotten at the base-----"

Max Muller was about 33 years old — exactly as old as was Macaulay when he went to India as the Law Member — when he wrote all this to Bunsen. In 1847 the German scholar extraordinary was so completely down and out that he was not sure even of his food and raiment. In 1856 — just in about ten years — after English bread and butter had provided the much needed nourishment for his body, he had started dreaming of bigger things. In his letter to Bunsen he had uncovered his inner thoughts to his confidant and fully exposed himself as a Christian proselytizer. He was prepared even to lay down his life to bring down the rotten tree of Hinduism. Like most Christian missionaries he too thought India was much ripe for Christianity and he had only to shake the tree to find the fruits (the Hindu converts) dropping into his lap in quick succession. He had perhaps started having hallucinations like St. Paul or it is likely that he had begun to suffer from a mild attack of calenture or paranoia.

There can be no doubt that Max Muller was a (secular) Christian missionary but he was a missionary with one great difference: he had received education and this made him potentially much more dangerous than Nobili, Dubois or Carey. In his days and before them very few of the missionaries had really had any education as we understand it today. In his letter to Bunsen he had told him prophetically: "Whatever finds root in India soon overshadows the whole of Asia." Many missionaries who came on the scene after him took this saying of Max Muller to heart and whenever they wished to appeal to religious sentiments of their co-religionists they have often given them Max Muller's message in slightly different language. In his Foreword Dr. E. Stanley Jones said: "For whichever way India goes the whole of non-Communist Asia goes. India has risen to leadership in Asia in a very remarkable way. Hundreds of millions will follow her lead."

Max Muller had wanted to go to India; live there for ten years quite quietly; learn the language; try to make friends and then see if he was fit to take part in the work of pulling down the rotten tree of Hinduism with all its fruits and blossoms. Nobili had tried to do exactly likewise in the seventeenth century (A.D. 1611) and it seems that Nobili's great imposture had fired Max Muller's young imagination immensely. He wanted, therefore, to emulate Nobili and go to India to perpetrate a similar pious fraud after having made some friends and won their confidence. In the course of his babble he made an astounding confession that he had wanted to go to India to learn the Sanskrit language. His confession made so voluntarily provides us with the clue behind the mystery as to why in spite of Max Muller not knowing the language well numerous degrees and memberships were bestowed upon him recognizing him as a great Sanskritist!

As a result of Professor (H.H.) Wilson's death in 1860 the Boden Chair of Sanskrit at Oxford had fallen vacant. Since a new Boden Professor had to be elected in Wilson's place Max Muller at once decided to offer himself as a candidate. The fight was between Max Muller and Monier Monier-Williams who had been a teacher at Haileybury where students used to be prepared and groomed for the Indian Civil Service. The battle for the Boden Chair was fought with the usual bitterness which often accompanies a political election. The sectarian manner in which Max Muller and his supporters acted to win this election clearly marked him out more as a Christian missionary than as a pure and simple scholar ofSanskrita.

At different times in the past more than one Christian missionaries have assumed varying roles and postures to gather converts for Christ. In the sixteenth century, Francis Xavier "bought his converts with money." Then came Robert Nobili who practised fraud to achieve the same goal. He was followed by Dubois who nearly walked in Nobili's footsteps. Then came on the Indian scene (William) Carey the Baptist who practised deception and told the simple Indian villagers of Bengal that his god (the crucifix made of bronze) was superior and more powerful than their god (idol usually made of wood) because Carey's god would not perish in fire! These four great missionaries were followed by Friedrich Max Muller who operated from a different and a much higher intellectual plane masquerading successfully for more than half a century as a philologist.

Max Muller's job was to put in the hands of regular and secular Christian missionaries such written material which they would be able to use with advantage while engaged in religious disputations with the Hindus. The work was very important. It aimed at convincing, in a subtle manner, the followers of Hinduism that their sacred books (Dharam Shashtras), especially the Rig, sanctioned and preached a religious faith that was not monotheistic and that the Rig-veda was childish, primitive and barbaric. It was to achieve this objective that Max Muller lent all his energies while engaged in the work of translating the /J/g. He often gave to the Rig mantras a twist, at times on purpose, to give them a meaning to suit his convenience and to create in the hearts of the Hindus a disaffection for their own religion.

The Church hierarchy had felt highly gratified with the manner in which Max Muller had been strengthening their hands in the task of uprooting Hinduism. They very much appreciated the part which Max Muller had been playing toward achieving this end. The useful and solid contribution which Max Muller had made in the service of Christian missions had been generally and widely recognized and openly appreciated. They were, therefore, by and large, on his side and wanted him to succeed Wilson as the next Boden Professor of Sanskrit at Oxford. This apart, there were some others who sincerely believed him to be the more eligible candidate and this argument seemed to have much weight behind it.

While some influential missionaries and ecclesiastics wanted to support Max Muller in this contest there were some others who had yet to make up their minds. In this context "two letters from the Bishop of Calcutta and Dr. Pusey are of interest as showing the good they expected from Max Muller's election to the cause of Christian missions though Mr. Jowett wrote much about the same time that the could not make up his mind whether Max Muller or his opponent would do more for missions." Inwardly there was very little difference of opinion between these two groups. Jowett too had wanted only that man to succeed Wilson who, by his writings, would do most for Christian missions to further their cause. In the initial stages Jowett had been undecided but seemingly only because while the other candidate, Monier-Williams, happened to be perhaps a demonstrably much bigger Christian fanatic he was not very sure of Max Muller. However, when he viewed Max Muller as a scholar he considered him as the more deserving.

The Bishop of Calcutta was far more clear in his mind. In an extremely forthright manner free from all inhibition he went all out of his way to support Max Muller. In a letter to Max Muller the Bishop had advised him:

"Ravenswood, Simla, July 13, 1860, my dear sir, - When I heard of the great loss which Sanskrit literature had sustained by the death of Processor Wilson, my thoughts .naturally turned to you as his obvious successor, and it will give me great pleasure to hear that the University make an election which is certainly expected and will be approved by every one to whom I have spoken on the subject in this country.

"I feel considerable interest in the matter, because I am sure that it is of the greatest importance for our missionaries to understand Sanskrit, to study the philosophy and sacred books of the Hindus, and to be able to meet the Pundits on their own ground.

"Among the means to this great end, none can be more important than your edition and Professor Wilson's translation of the Rigveda. It would be most fitting in my opinion for a great Christian University to place in its Sanskrit Chair the scholar who has made the Sanskrit scriptures accessible to the Christian missionary.

".... You are at liberty to make any use that you please of this letter.

"With every wish for your success, I remain, my dear Sir, Yours very sincerely. G.E.L. Calcutta."

The Bishop who had been canvassing for Max Muller had made the position transparently clear. The Bishop had told Max Muller with an undisguised sense of triumph that every other missionary in India to whom he had spoken had upheld the Bishop in his resolve to support Max Muller. The Bishop thought and he seemed to have earnestly believed that since the great job of converting the Hindus of India to Christianity had received great push as a result of the translation of the Rig as made by Max Muller the Christian University would indeed do well to elect Max Muller to the Boden Chair of Sanskrit. The Bishop had felt so much concerned that of his own free will and without any solicitation from Max Muller he willingly and readily permitted Max Muller to make any use of the Bishop's letter to influence other voters if Max Muller felt it would in any manner improve his chances of success in that contest.

In the early years De. Pusey had not taken kindly to Max Muller whom he regarded as an alien on English soil and perhaps as an heretic. "In the beginning Max Muller speaks of having had little intercourse with Dr. Pusey. When he was made an MA., of Christ Church, and attended chapel regularly, Dr. Pusey at once announced that he would never administer the Holy Communion to Max Muller, as he had only been confirmed in the Lutheran Church - not by a Bishop." An average Christian missionary usually carries deep down in his heart an inbuilt obsession to convert every one else to his own particular religious brand and in this respect Dr. Pusey was no exception. However, later as Dr. Pusey came to realize that Max Muller had been doing much for Christian missions he found no difficulty in switching over his loyalties to Max Muller and in coming out openly in support of his candidature. In consequence Pusey turned a staunch supporter of the German scholar. Making his position clear in a letter that he addressed to Max Muller Dr. Pusey said:

"Christ Church, June 2, 1860, My Dear Professor, - On the first election to the Sanskrit Chair, you will have heard that we were divided before two great names, Professor Wilson, whose first-rate Sanskrit knowledge was in the mouth of every one, and Dr. Mill, who, many of us thought, might fulfil the object of the founder better by giving to the Professorship a direct missionary turn. The same thought would naturally recur to us now, and I have kept myself in suspense since our sudden loss of Professor Wilson. My first impression, however, is my abiding conviction, that we should be best promoting the intentions of the founder, by electing yourself, who have already done so much to make us fully acquainted with the religious system of those whom we wish to win to the Gospel.... I cannot but think that your lectures on the Vedas . . . are the greatest gifts which have been bestowed on those who would win to Christianity the subtle and thoughtful minds of the cultivated Indians. We owe you very much for the past, and we shall ourselves gain greatly by placing you in a position in which you can give your undivided attention to those labours by which we have already so much profited.

". . . . your work will form a new era in the efforts for the conversion of India, and Oxford will have reason to be thankful that, by giving you a home, it will have facilitated a work of such primary and lasting importance for the conversion of India, and which, by enabling us to compare that early false religion with the true, illustrates the more than blessedness of what we enjoy -Your very faithfully, E.B. Pusey."

Had Dr. Pusey known that with the passage of time his words will become public property he might have been more choosy with the language he chose to give expression to his Christian feelings. But since Max Muller seemed to value this testimonial from Dr. Pusey as another feather in his missionary cap he handed down Dr. Pusey's letter to future generations. Dr. Pusey's letter unmistakably stamps Max Muller as a lackey of the Christian Church who worked in close collusion with Christian missions in their insidious efforts to destroy Hinduism. In his letter Dr. Pusey had confirmed of which he was fully convinced: by electing Max Muller it would be possible to promote more effectively the intentions of Col. Boden.

While confirming that Christian missions owed much to Max Muller for the past Dr. Pusey expressed the fond hope that the missions shall gain greatly in future if they placed Max Muller in a position in which he could give his undivided attention to those labours by which Christianism had already much benefited. Dr. Pusey did, as a result, whatever lay in his power to assist Max Muller to win the contest for the Boden Chair. The manner in which he toiled relentlessly to lend his full support to Max Muller's candidature would leave no one in doubt that he really wanted the German to win. Dr. Pusey wrote a note on each notice in his own hand when he sent final notices of the forthcoming election. And his note categorically exposed once again to the world at large Max Muller's camouflaged Christian missionary character. Pusey's note read: "Max Muller has already done more for the Gospel in India than -any other Sanskrit scholar, by opening to our missionaries their sacred books. His election would enable him to devote himself to that work. He is first Sanskrit scholar living"?

In the end and in spite of all efforts put into the contest by Max Muller and his supporters, in England and in India, Monier-Williams won. He was no liberal in matters of religion. He too was committed to the missionary cause as much as Dr. Pusey and Max Muller. Monier-Williams has also left for future generations some writings which equally condemn him as a rabid Christian. It seems in that era when Macaulay, Pusey, Max Muller and Monier-Williams lived and laboured they all observed only one moral code: they fervently believed that "every British Christian, every one who speaks the English language, has a solemn mission from God for the conversion of India" and deriving their strength from this belief in which they were equally supported, aided and abbetted by the State and the Church alike they always found some valid excuse to gang up against that section of the Indian people who happened to profess Hinduism.

It seems very likely that in those days both the Christian missionaries and the Christian people felt convinced, though only naively but unabashedly, that they had a divine mission to fulfil which was somehow to convert the Hindus of India to Christ. Their aims were the aims of paranoids who neither would see reason nor would ever find anything wrong with the means they so often employed to achieve their Christian ends. The Christian clergy who so often resorted to undesirable and unethical means to achieve their goals always had some ready excuse and explanation to offer for their every action. The missionaries were in a profession and like anyone else they tried their best somehow to keep it going. However, when the thoughts of great men of letters and of scholars too moved in the same groove they truly made themselves reprehensible. Monier-Williams who had become (1860) the second Boden Professor of Sanskrit at Oxford happened to be one such scholar who revelled more in being a proselytizer than a scholar of Sanskrit. In his Preface to A Sanskrit-English Dictionary which he compiled he boastfully gave free vent to his Christianish zeal and aspirations in these words:

"In explanation I must draw attention to the fact that I am only the second occupant of the Boden Chair, and that its founder, Col. Boden, stated most explicitly in his will (dated August 15, 1811) that the special object of his munificient bequest was to promote the translation of the Scriptures into Sanskrit so as to enable his countrymen to proceed in the conversion of the natives of India to the Christian religion.

"It was on this account that, when my distinguished predecessor and teacher, Professor H.H. Wilson, was a candidate for the Chair in 1832, his lexicographical labours were put forward as his principal claim to election.

"Surely it need not be thought surprising, if following in the footsteps of my venerated master, I have made it the chief aim of my professional life to provide facilities for the translation of our sacred Scriptures into Sanskrit and for the promotion of a better knowledge of the religions and customs of India, as the best key to a knowledge of the religious needs of our great Eastern Dependency. My very first public lecture delivered after my election in 1860 was on "The Study of Sanskrit in Relation to Missionary Work in India' (published in 1861).

"For the rest, I have already alluded to the advantage which this Dictionary has derived from the support of the Government of India, and I ought here to acknowledge with gratitude that, without the subsidy granted by successive Secretaries of State in Council, the present volume could not have been sold to the public at the price at which it is now offered — Monier Monier-Williams, Indian Institute, Oxford, 1899."

It ought to be clear from confessions so freely and vaingloriously made by Monier-Williams that he found greater satisfaction in his being an instrument for converting the Hindus of India to Christianity than in following his scholarly pursuits. Sir Monier Monier-Williams and his predecessor, Professor Wilson, both had equally abused their official position as the Boden Professors of Sanskrit to promote purely parochial interests of Christianism. They used scholarship only as a cover for their missionary activities aiming all the while to advance the interests of a religion which damned all those who refused to believe in the divinity of Jesus and to accept him as their personal saviour.

No one but only those equally committed and subsidized like Max Muller would ever be able successfully to hold a brief on behalf of the missionary-philologist. Again and again he uncovered his mind and what he revealed fully established his camouflaged identity of a secular Christian missionary. Though it seems probable that his scholarship lost its objectivity in much greater measure only after he received strong, and perhaps vituperative, briefing from Macaulay (1855) it is certain that after this interview he turned palpably bold coming out more often in the open barefaced to censure and denigrate Hinduism. What he said in 1866 in a letter to his wife proves the point to the hilt.

"I hope I shall finish that work, and I feel convinced, though I shall not live to see it, that this edition of mine and the translation of the Veda will hereafter tell to a great extent on the fate of India, and on the growth of millions of souls in that country. It is the root of their religion, and to show them what that root is, I feel sure, is the only way of uprooting all that has sprung up from it during the last 3000 years.'

At the time when Max Muller had been busy in devising ways to uproot Hinduism with the help of his edition and translation of the Veda another Christian bigot, the Eighth Duke of Argyll, was the Secretary of State for India. In another letter which Max Muller wrote to him Max Muller blatantly and quite brazen-facedly admitted having played behind the scene the part of a proselytizer. His letter to the Duke shows Max Muller in his true missionary colours. In this letter, dated December 16, 1868, Max Muller made an unadulterated confession saying:

"... . As for more than twenty years my principal work has been devoted to the ancient literature of India, I cannot but feel a deep and real sympathy for all that concerns the higher interests of the people of that country. Though I have never been in India, I have many friends there, both among the civilians and among the natives, and I believe I am not mistaken in supposing that the publication in England of the ancient sacred writings of the Brahmans, which had never been published in India, and other contributions from different European scholars towards a better knowledge of the ancient literature and religion of India, have not been without some effect on the intellectual and religious movement that is going on among the more thoughtful members of Indian society. I have sometimes regretted that I am not an Englishmen, and able to help more actively in the great work of educating and improving the natives. But I do rejoice that this great task of governing and benefiting India should have fallen to one who knows the greatness of the task and all its opportunities and responsibilities, who thinks not only of its political and financial bearings, but has a heart to feel for the moral welfare of those millions of human beings that are, more or less, directly, committed to his charge.

"India has been conquered once, but India must be conquered again, and this second conquest should be a conquest of education. Much has been done for education of late, but if the funds were tripled and quadrupled, that would hardly be enough.

The results of the educational work carried on during the last twenty years are palpable everywhere. They are good and bad, as was to be expected. It is easy to find fault with what is called young Bengal, the product of English ideas grafted on the Indian mind. But young Bengal, with all its faults, is full of promise. Its bad features are apparent everywhere, its good qualities are naturally hidden from the eyes of careless observers. . . India can never be anglicized, but it can be reinvigorated....

". . . The missionaries have done far more than they themselves seem to be aware of, nay, much of the work which is theirs they would probably disclaim. The Christianity of our nineteenth century will hardly be the Christianity of India. But the ancient religion of India is doomed - and if Christianity does not step in, whose fault will it be?"

As time passed Max Muller gradually became more intimately concerned with the missionary cause and in consequence less inhibited in his protestations. In his letter to the Duke he eulogized the work that had already been done by Christian missionaries in the educational field during the last twenty years While paying tribute to the work of missionaries in the field of education he was in disguise belauding Macaulay who had boastfully told his father that if their plans of education were followed up, there will not be a single idolater among the respectable classes in Bengal in another thirty years.

Max Muller had not stopped at that. He was growing old and perhaps impatient for having been ignored and not recognized. After paying him a homage which would have been legitimately due from a liegeman he first humbly reminded the Duke that the work done by him (Max Muller) had been responsible for bringing about a change in the intellectual and religious outlook of the Hindus of Bengal virtually putting it on record that he (and the missionaries) had done their job as a result of which Hinduism was now doomed. In his next breath he bewailed and lamented that if Christianity did not come forward to fill this spiritual void so created by their efforts the responsibility for this failure would then rest elsewhere and not on him.

On December 2, 1873 Max Muller delivered at the Deannery of Westminister Abbey a Lay Sermon. Though this Sermon did not find a place in his first biography a reference to it did appear in these words:

"The next day both the congratulations and vituperations began; the latter made little impression. On his way home Max Muller met Dr. Liddon, who wrung his hand, saying 'I rejoice from my heart, that you have been helping us.' The author of The Childhood of Religion wrote: - 'I must thank you for your noble words in the Abbey last week, to which it was my delight to listen. I am sure they will do great good in directing attention to the place each faith bas had in the order of this divinely governed world."

Dr. Liddon had in fact made no secret that Max Muller had been helping Christian missionaries in their pursuits. The only manner, more covertly, in which Max Muller could have possibly helped and abetted Christian missionaries was to provide them which leads by putting in their hands literature which presented Hinduism in unfavourable colours and this he did by giving, at times intentionally, wrong and misleading meanings and interpretations to some of the Rig. mantras.

The publishing firm of Longmans who had wanted their publishing business to be much more closely in touch with the fast expanding large Indian market once sought Max Mullet's advice. His reply to them will show once again how much concerned he had felt and to what extent he was involved with Christian missions and their schools which had been opened to bring to fruition the educational plans of Grant, Macaulay & Company. He had advised Longmans:

"I think your plan is excellent, if you can find a very intelligent traveller. The persons responsible for every kind of school in India are the Directors of Education in each Presidency. . . . There is publishing department under each Director of Education, and you can see from published reports how much they spend, and what books they patronize .... I ought to tell you that I have long urged the Clarendon Press to establish depots in India. I tell you this that you may not suspect me of serving two masters. ... I know the Registrar of Bombay, Mrs. Peterson, and at Lahore, Dr. Leitner, and at Benares, Dr. Thibaut. The missionary schools might also be approached through the missionary Societies in London."

While he lived for more than fifty years in Oxford Max Muller sincerely tried to identify himself with the English people. He tried more than once to ingratiate himself with those who wielded power and influence and he seemed to have succeeded in this direction remarkably well. As he had always wanted to play safe he was, usually, punctiliously careful in the use and choice of his words. But after losing the contest for the Boden Chair he became, at times, more outspoken. Max Muller seemed to have anticipated the election results to go against him because he had indicated his fears in a letter (June 1860) which he had written to his mother. He had confided in her: "My time is quite taken up with the election business, and I sometimes wish I had not thought of it.'It will absorb my time till December, and if I don't win I shall be very cross! Only think of 4000 electors, scattered all over England, and each must be written to."

In spite of having prepared himself mentally to face this humiliation at the hands of Monier-Williams "it was the one great disappointment of Max Muller's life, and made lasting impression on him." His defeat turned Max Muller sour and thereafter he appeared to have become less guarded in his utterances. One such occasion came when he was requested by the Vicar of St. Giles to deliver a speech which he did (1887) in the hall of St. John's College. In the course of this speech Max Muller made an open admission of the truth which he had so long kept buried in his bosom. He told his .udience that one of his objects while undertaking to translate the Rig. was to assist Christian missionaries who wanted to convert the Hindus to Christianity!

". . . . When I undertook to publish for the University Press a series of translations of the most important of these sacred books, one of my objects was to assist missionaries. What shall we think if a missionary who came to convert us, and who had never read our Bible? .... But, it will be said, you cannot deny that the Hindus are polytheists, that they worship idols. But let us look at their own Bible, at the Veda, older than any other book in India. No doubt we find there many names for the Divine, many gods, as we are accustomed to say. But there are also passages in which he oneness of the Deity is clearly asserted."

Even though Max Muller made an important disclosure there is reason to believe that he had not told the whole truth. His first and foremost object when he accepted (1847) the job was somehow to fill his stomach and while there was nothing questionable about this object no amount of gerrymandering by Max Muller and his votaries and abettors would ever absolve the German scholar of Sanskrita of the indictment that in exchange for worldly comforts he had prostituted his pen and scholarship.

Max Muller was too canny for average band of Christian missionaries very few among whom then had either the education or a family background of which a nineteenth century Englishman felt proud. Max Muller was an amphibologist and loved to talk to his audiences in a language which each one of the listeners could conveniently interpret as it suited his mood, temperament and prejudices. In the course of the speech he delivered in the hall of St. John's College to ecclesiastics he glibly told them in one breath that in the Rig the Oneness of the Deity was clearly asserted but in spite of this he described to them the Hindus as polytheists. Max Muller merrily indulged in intellectual acrobatics as often as he found it convenient or possible to toy with his missionary listeners. He knew it too well that they, most of them, had neither the necessary intelligence nor that objectivity of mind which alone enabled a man to separate grain from chaff both of which he served them mixed up in full measure. At times he would appear to be fooling them on purpose in order perhaps to amuse them and when they exuded satisfaction he felt mightily delighted in his capacity to handle with such dexterous ease this section of the 19th-century English society. And if ever Max Muller found it helpful he went all his way to humour his audiences to pamper their whims, prejudices and caprices. For the diplomatist Max Muller the end was, as it always is for the missionary, singularly much more important than the means he employed.

Max Muller not only passionately believed in the desirability of converting the Hindus of India to Christianity but its possibility too had since long been a pressing obsession with him. But to his great dismay he realized that despite his having written and spoken so much to extol Christianity and to denigrate Hinduism all his efforts in this direction had been of not much avail and this made him necessarily feel ill at ease. Since writing letters in which he habitually expatiated on this pet topic had become an addiction with him from which he derived vicarious pleasure he wrote a letter from his sick bed (November 23,1898) to Sir Henry Acland in which he said:

". . . . They must be made to feel that they are in the same boat with ourselves. They are a cleanly race, more so than we were a hundred years ago; they look upon digging wells, building bathing places, planting trees, as part of their religion. Even their religion is not as bad as it looks, as I hope to show in a book just finished. I have not much faith in missionaries, medical or otherwise. If we get such men again in India as Rammohun Roy, or Keshub Chunder Sen, and if we get an archbishop at Calcutta who knows what Christianity is, India will be Christianised in all that is essential in the twinkling of an eye. On this too, we must be hopeful, but not too sanguine."

Max Muller belonged to that special specie of men who not only think themselves to be the wisest but they also seem to believe everyone else to be a fool. He believed that if Bishop Middleton had not made a wrong approach to Rammohun Roy the Bengali bhadrlok would have renounced his ancestral religion to become a Christian! The sentiments expressed by him in his letter to Sir Acland prove yet another point: he took Sir Acland for a naive feeling he will readily swallow whatever he doled out to him though in the last sentence he himself had made a clear attempt to get out of all that he had promised in the earlier parts. Nonetheless, Max Muller's letter unmistakably brands him as a Christian proselytizer; to call him a scholar in the light of all that he told Sir Acland, would amount only to be an insult to independent scholarship of any kind and whatever merit.

The East India Company and the Christian mission societies in London had wanted to keep secret the true identity of the German .scholar. None of them wished the world, especially the Hindus, to know that as a secular Christian missionary he was hands in gloves with Christian missions who wanted to convert the Hindus of India to Christianity. But since no one has ever been able to plan a perfect murder they too failed in their attempts and the truth soon came out after Max Muller's death. He left, inadvertently perhaps, such incontrovertible evidence in some of the letters he wrote in his lifetime that \*«\>, now stands fully unmasked. These letters have so fully exposed »i \\w that he can now be seen and studied in his full nakedness. The world at large now knows it too well that this scholar extraordinary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the secular mask of philology was more a Christian missionary than a scholar of the Veda.

## 5 - Max Muller as a Scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita

It was 145 years ago (1847) that Max Muller had been launched upon a career for which he was far from being fit - he was to translate the Rigveda from Vedic-Sanskrita into English. He was then only 25 years young and in addition neither of the two languages happened to be his mother tongue. While his study of Sanskrit had hardly covered till then a short period of about six years being a German he had his own difficulties with the English language. It is a known fact admitted by Max Muller, that while he was in Paris (1846) he regularly used to go to meet Dwarkanath Tagore in his hotel suite to brush up his English and that when he took up residence in London he often experienced difficulty whenever he wished to make himself understood by his English landlady. But all this notwithstanding he was given the amplitudinous job of translating the Rigveda from Vedic-Sanskrita into English!

Max Muller had matriculated from Leipzig in 1841 and by 1843 he had left the university with a doctorate! In 1844 he translated Hitopdesha. In 1846 he went to Paris migrating in the same year to London. In 1847 his services were commissioned by the East India Company for translating the Rigveda. In the year 1851 he was made an honorary MA. of Christ Church which was then considered to be the most exclusive of English colleges. The news announcing the discovery of someone who would translate the Rig. into English from the standpoint of a Christian missionary had travelled fast to all Christian capitals of Europe where, as a result, both ecclesiastics and the laity legitimately felt more than tickled. They somehow believed that the new messiah who had the capacity and the ability to bring the Hindus of India to Christ had miraculously surfaced. To have such a person on its side was indeed a matter of great joy for Christendom. Everyone considered it his duty to encourage that man and accord him recognition. One by one Christian political states and Societies started conferring on Max Muller degrees and memberships, mostly honorary, till he had so many of them that it became almost difficult for him to keep a count.

The honorary recognitions heaped on Max Muller for his scholarship by Christian countries and organisations is a proof, if one were needed, of his involvement in the work of missionising. He was not a priest nor did he belong to any religious Order and was under no vows. But there is no doubt that he was to do the same work which a Christian missionary has to do in mission lands. He was to be a Christian missionary but only in the secular garb of a philologist.

It is a commonly held belief that the language of the Veda is Sanskrit. It is not true. Sanskrit, as it is now understood by this name, has evolved itself mainly and essentially from the language in which the Vedas have come down to us. The language of the Vedas has some peculiarities of its own which mark it out as a language distinctly different from Sanskrit as we know it today and which for this reason can only be called Vedic language. The fact that Max Muller had thus to translate the Rig from Vedic-Sanskrita which was only akin to Sanskrit, into English made the task still much more difficult for him. Yet he was asked to do the job and, quite surprisingly, he considered it so light and easy that, he thought, he could take up along with it some other work to be done in spare time.

Like all men of letters Max Muller too was maturing as he advanced in age. In the year 1878, at the time when he delivered his fifth Hibbert Lecture he admitted: "All we can do is to find, if possible, the original focus of thought, and then follow the various directions taken by the rays that proceeded from it. This is what I have endeavoured to do, and if in so doing, I may seem to have put a new garment upon old, all I can say is that I see no other way, unless we all agree to speak not only Sanskrit, but Vedic-Sanskrit." Max Muller had clearly admitted (thirty one years after he began translating the Rig) that the language of the Rigveda was Vedic-Sanskrita which was different and distinct from modern Sanskrit, and that, on that account, the job had been rendered hard and difficult. In this he had agreed (only in 1878) with Swami Dayananda that "it is not an easy matter to interpret the Vedas. Their language is different from the classical as well as the colloqual Sanskrit. The Vedic texts cannot be explained by grammatical rules that apply to common Sanskrit speech."

This was not all. Max Muller once again admitted to the world at large that his work of translating the Rig consisted in fact only of "deciphering". But the world, or at least the entire Christendom, which was then politically dominated by the English people who had hired Max Muller, seemed to be under the impression that the translation as it had been done by Max Muller was nothing but genuine, flawless and correct. Again, Max Muller, when he revised his first edition, re-affirmed that he believed that the translators (of the Rig-veda)" ought to be decipherers." He was clever and full of guile and cunning. First, he admitted he had done the work only of deciphering. Later, he wanted all other translators also to be known no better than decipherers because if every other translator made the same or similar mistakes as he had made he would have a clear alibi and no one would ever be in a position to single him out to be blamed.

In his heart Max Muller was so fully convinced of his utter failure as a translator of the Rig that once he had to confess it to his great chagrin: "No one who knows anything of the Veda would think of attempting a translation of it at present. A translation of the Rigveda is a task for the next century." He did not, however, stop there. He went a step further and made a clean breast of the whole thing when he owned his failings saying: "If by translation we mean a complete and satisfactory and final translation of the whole of the Rigveda I shall feel inclined to go even further than Prof, von Roth. Not only shall we have to wait till the next century for such a work, but I doubt whether we shall ever obtain it."

Max Muller had to admit though he did it only in his sophistic style that the translation of the Rig that he had somehow managed and for which he had been so handsomely paid and acclaimed was neither complete nor satisfactory. To put it a little more bluntly he owned it, though only equivocally, that what he had given to the world of scholarship in a highly specialised field was only a little short of trash. The East India Company and Christian missions had jointly foisted Max Muller on the gullible world as a scholar extraordinary of the Vedas (Vedic-Sanskrita) and many an Indian had willingly accepted Max Muller in this capacity. It must also be conceded that Max Muller made full and effective use of the position in which Providence had placed him and succeeded in fooling most of the people for more than half a century, with his translations.

It would seem hardly fair to accuse and reproach Max Muller about a hundred years after his death for the spurious translations which he gave to the world. He had in fact been repeatedly telling the world that what he had offered could easily be, and in all probability was, not genuine. He implied that any translation of the Rig could even be mendacious but some scholars appeared to laugh it away as a mark perhaps of condescension and humility on the part of a scholar extraordinary of unprecedented merit ! It looked as if no one ever understood his joke. No one ever cared to lift a finger even when Max Muller ventured to hurl yet another explanation in their face for having put into circulation a coin which was only slightly better than a counterfeit. He said: "When we come to really difficult passages the Vedic hymns often require for greater effort of divination than the hymns addressed to Egyptian or Babylonian deities," adding that "the Veda, I feel convinced will occupy scholars for centuries to come and maintain its position as the most ancient book in the library of mankind." He admitted his inability as a translator but continued, in spite of it, to flood the world of scholarship with such translations and interpretations of Vedic texts which in his own estimation were far from satisfactory and genuine.

Though some interpretations put on some Veda mantras by Max Muller were atrocious and, on the surface, untenable he seemed to have realised this fact only when perhaps it was too late. He had already gone too far. He had even admitted that his "attempt to translate the Veda is a mere contribution toward the better understanding of the Vedic hymns" and that "there are, as all Vedic scholars know, whole verses which as yet yield no sense whatever; there are words the meaning of which we can but guess." In addition he confessed that his translations of the Vedic hymns were conjectural. Yet he continued to work according to a master plan. First, he himself made non-sensical translations of the Rig mantras and then deposed that these mantras yielded no sense!

It was very generous indeed of Max Muller to have made all these confessions but since he did not desist from committing similar sacrilege again and again in the name of scholarship these confessions so piously made carry with them the foulsome odour of Jesuitical simulations. Perhaps, he chose to stoop down in order only to conquer. But in truth Max Muller seemed to be helpless. He knew that unless he was willingly prepared once again to starve and suffer from want he could not go back upon the promise he had given in exchange for the English pelf he had received. In consequence he succumbed to circumstances over which he had no control and in which he found himself fully submerged much against his wish. But all this does in no way alter the painful truth that he had willingly worked in collusion with Christian agencies with the clandestine purpose of wrecking Hinduism from within as a means to bring about the conversion of the Hindus of India to Christianity.

Did Max Muller understand and realise that he was unfit for the job for which he had been chosen? There is ample internal evidence to support such a supposition. He was woefully conscious of the fact that he did not fully and sufficiently understand and appreciate the intricacies of Sanskrita grammar without which it was impossible for anyone, much less a German and a fledgling of 24 years, to understand and grasp the true sense of Veda mantras. In 1845 or 1846 Max Muller had received an offer from Boehtlingk, who had then been working with the Imperial Russian Academy, to join him but for some sound reasons Max Muller had declined the offer. Explaining his unwillingness to go and join Boehtlingk he had this to say among some other things: "Boehtlingk could have done the whole work himself, in some respect better than I, because he was my senior, and besides, he knew Panini, the old Indian grammarian who is constantly referred to in Sayana's Commentary, better than I did."

The true significance of what Max Muller said and the great importance which Panini holds for all those wanting ever to understand and comprehend the true meanings of Veda mantras we shall soon explain at some length. Here it is sufficient for our purpose to know that, as Max Muller admitted, he did not know and understand Panini the old grammarian who was constantly referred to by Sayana in his Commentary of the Rigveda on which (Sayana's Commentary) Max Muller had depended almost exclusively and heavily for his guidance.

The youthful Sanskritist had many admirers in India but very few critics. While some of these admirers more often considered it their privilege and, at times, perhaps an honour, to applaud Max Muller in chorus the critics, by and large, either kept discreetly quiet or chose to open their mouths only when they found themselves in a small select gathering. Nevertheless, one such critic, B.V. Kameshwara Aiyar, who happened to be more outspoken once wrote to Max Muller to tell him that his translation of the Purushasukta was incorrect and inappropriate. Swami Dayananda was the other Indian who was ruthlessly forthright in censuring Max Muller and his Sanskrita scholarship. It might be of some interest to mention here en passant that Max Muller came to be more widely known in India mainly as a direct result of the strongly adverse criticism which his work and Max Muller as a translator received from Swami Dayananda. The Swami had likened Max Muller, so far as his knowledge of Vedic-Sanskrita was concerned, to that child who was still learning to walk. In his Satyarth Prakash he made such biting reference to Max Muller and his capacity and learning as a Sanskritist that it must have severely nettled the German scholar. In a language which some people might have considered unsavoury and acerbic Swami Dayananda had remarked:

"The impression that the Germans are the best Sanskrita scholars, and that no one has read so much of Sanskrita as Prof. Max Muller, is altogether unfounded. Yes, in a land where lofty trees never grow, even recinus communis or the castor oil plant may be called an oak. ... I came to learn from a letter of a Principal of some German University, that even men learned enough to interpret a Sanskrita letter are rare in Germany. I have also learnt from the study of Max Muller's History of Sanskrit Literature and his comments on some mantras of the Veda, that Prof. Max Muller has been able only to scribble out something by the help of the so-called tikas or paraphrases of the Vedas current in India."

The fact that there existed a commentary on the Rigveda, by Swami Dayananda was well within Max Muller's knowledge but he wilfully refused to take note of il. Perhaps he considered it infra dig to heed to the opinion of a Hindu sanyasin. Or, perhaps he might have felt slighted that a heathen from Indian should have dared to question his position and authority as the official translator of the Rig. In a letter which he wrote (January 22, 1882) to Byramjee M. Malabari he referred to Dayananda in not-too-complimentary terms. He advised Malabari that he had "wished to warn against two dangers, that of undervaluing or despising the ancient natural interpretations of a rik; why should not western scholars be allowed the same privilege?"

Sandhyavandanam, Foreword, pp. i-ii. See also The Life and Letters of Max Muller.

religion, as is done so often by your half-Europenaized youths, and that of overvaluing it, and interpreting it as it was never meant to be interpreted, of which you may see a painful instance in Dayananda Sarasvati's labours on the Veda."

As a scholar Max Muller had as much right to put to test, and question, the validity of the translation made by Dayananda as the

Swami had to check upon the veracity of translations made by Max Muller. But instead of adopting this honourable course Max Muller came out throwing aspersions on Dayananda's commentary of the Rig. That was not all. Though at that time a few other commentaries on the Rigveda were very much extant Max Muller arbitrarily chose and stuck tenaciously only to Sayana's commentary for his guidance for the only reason which Max Muller himself explained:

"And now let me tell you, what will again sound like a fairy-tale, but is nevertheless a simple fact. The Rigveda which, for more than three, or it may be four thousand years, has formed the foundation of the religious and moral life of untold millions of human beings, had never been published; and by a combination of the most fortunate circumstances, it fell to my lot to bring out the first complete edition of that sacred text, together with the most authoritative commentary of Hindu theologians, the commentary of Sayana Akarya.

How did Max Muller decide that Sayana's was the most authoritative commentary on the Rigvedal Was he really qualified to judge? No, he had no means to ascertain the authoritative character or otherwise of any one commentary against another. As Sayana's commentary served his missionary purpose admirably well he conveniently opted in favour of Sayana. He regarded, or at least he so pretended, Sayana's commentary of the Rig as the most authoritative commentary of Hindu theologians but he conveniently ignored the advice tendered by Sayana himself to the effect that "of the three kinds of interpretations of the Rigveda, the spiritual interpretation is the most important." Sayana's advice notwithstanding Max Muller made use. of Sayana's commentary as an excuse for eliminating all other commentaries for the specious reason that Sayana's commentary suited his parochial conveniences. Sayana himself had admitted to having translated more than once a Rig mantra wrongly in a manner that later appeared to him to be not correct or regarding which he was in serious doubt.
