However, since Max Muller found it much more easy and possible to twist and 'distort Sayana's commentary he stuck to it wilfully ignoring all other commentaries including the one by Swami Dayananda.

As we said earlier Vedic-Sanskrita has some peculiarities of its own which need very careful and devoted attention and study if someone really wished to master the Vedic ((language) as a means to understanding correctly the Veda texts. Since the Vedic Rishis were fully conscious of this problem they had laid down certain important and basic guidelines for all those who felt inclined to study and understand the Vedas. These guidelines and principles which govern the correct interpretation of Vedic texts had first been laid down by Yaska and by Patanjali. In his Nirukta its able author, Yaska, says (M5):

without the aid or Nirukta the Vedas cannot be understood. Again, Patanjali asserts in his Mahabhashya that in order to safeguard (and protect the meanings and the true sense of) the Vedas grammar must be studied. Yaska had made yet another observation of great importance in his Nirukta that all the words used in the Vedas are Yaugika and must only be understood and interpreted in that sense. The same view was expressed by Pandit Guru Datt Vidyarthi, a great Sanskrita scholar. He had re-affirmed: 'The fourth section of the first chapter of Nirukta opens with a discussion of this very subject in which Yasaka, Gargya, Shakatyana and other grammarians and etymologists mention that Vedic terms are all Yaugika.""

Patanjali's Mahabhashya lays down the same great principle in extremely clear terms:

"all the rishis and munis, ancient authors and commentators without exception regard all Vedic terms to be Yaugika."

Yasaka's Nirukta and Panini's Ashtadhyyayi have been considered, and accepted, throughout the ages and throughout the world, as indispensable for correct comprehension of the Vedic texts. "The Nirukta of Yasaka Muni explains about seven hundred Vedic mantras." Panini's Ashtadhyyayi is the surviving authoritative grammar based on which Patanjali wrote his commentary called Mahabhashya. That Panini's Ashtadhyyayi has always been held in the greatest esteem by all notable and serious scholars of Vedic-Sanskrita is a fact which had very well been known to Max Muller. While delivering his third Hibbert Lecture he had told his listeners:

"The science of phonetics rose in India at a time when writing was unknown, ... I believe I shall not be contradicted by Hemlboltz, or Ellis, or other representatives of phonetic science, if I say that, to the present day, the phoneticians of India of the 5th century B.C. are unsurpassed in their analysis of the elements of language.

In grammar, I challenge any scholar to produce from any language a more comprehensive collection and classification of all the facts of a language than what we find in Panini's Sutras."

Despite this high opinion of Panini held by Max Muller he ignored Panini the great grammarian for the simple reason that the scholar extraordinary did not understand Panini!

No one seriously and honestly interested in the study of Vedic texts and of Vedic-Sanskrita can, under any pretext, ever afford to ignore Panini or bypass his Ashtadhyyayi. Panini was the foremost grammarian of India who gave to the world the science of philology and whom Prof. A.H. Sayce paid the loftiest tribute. In his opinion among all grammarians of all ages and of all lands Panini stands atop:

"The native grammarians of India had at an early period analyzed both the phonetic sounds and vocabulary of Sanskrit with astonishing precision, and drawn up a far more scientific system of grammar than the philologists of Alexandria or Rome had been able to attain. The Devnagri alphabet is a splendid monument of phonological accuracy, and long before the time of Saadia and Khayyug, the Hindu "Vaiyakaranas" or grammarians, had not only discovered that roots are the ultimate elements of language but had traced all the words of Sanskrit to a limited number of roots. Their grammatical system and nomenclature of inductive reasoning, and though based on the phenomena of a single language, show a scientific insight into the nature of speech which has never been surpassed."

The paramount importance of Panini for diligent study of Vedic texts was too well known to Max Muller but he schemingly ignored him. He had acted like that unscrupulous lawyer who would hesitate neither to hide truth nor to introduce falsehood in his defence if it would somehow secure a verdict of "guilty" against his opponent. Panini's position as a great grammarian has always been unassailable. According to Goldstucker.

". . . when I see that the very forms of the language are falsified, and that it is made a principle to slur the grammar of Panini, and to ridicule those who lay stress on it; — when I see (hat one of the highest grammatical authorities of India is schooled for a "want of practice and still" while this censure is passed without even an understanding of the work to which it refers; . . . and when I consider that the method of studying Sanskrit philology is pursued by those whose words apparently derive weight and influence from the professional position they hold; . . . then I hold that it would be a want of courage and a dereliction of duty, if I did not make a stand against these Saturnalia of Sanskrit Philology.

On this ground I have raised my voice, ... to examine the competence of those who set themselves up as our masters and authorities. On this ground I have endeavoured to vindicate for

Panini the position he holds in Sanskrit literature, and the position he ought to hold amongst honest Sanskrit philologist."

In his Panini Goldstucker made a very strong plea for a careful study of Panini's Ashtadhyyayi by all honest Sanskrita scholars if they really wanted to understand ancient Sanskrita literature generally and the Vedic texts in particular. Apparently his advice and appeal was directed to them who were honest and sincere in their professions. He did not have in his mind those who were either insincere or dishonest because Goldstucker knew it too well that those who had an axe to grind would usually find easy excuses to ignore Panini in furtherance of their parochial interests.

In the past, before Max Muller attempted a translation of the Rig, several other Occidental scholars had translated or attempted to decipher the Rig mantras and, by and large, these scholars had depended, like Max Muller, for their guidance either on Sayana or on Mahidhara. Some of them believed, perhaps honestly, that Sayana's was the most authoritative commentary. But even while depending on Sayana they conveniently ignored an important observation made by Sayana that Sanskrita words in the Veda mantras had multiple meanings by which he clearly meant that it was absolutely necessary to give only the appropriate and correct (Yaugika) meaning to each and every word in each particular context.

The Occidental scholars, nearly all of them, however, stuck to another incongruous theory, advanced first perhaps by Roth, that by means of German "science of philology Vedic mantras could be interpreted much better than with the help of Nirukta." W.D. of Nirukta, Roth gave a wrong interpretation of a passage of Artareya Brahmana which had attracted derisive comments from Goldstucker in his Panini. Commenting on Roth's scholarship Dr. Haug wrote (1863) : "Roth's explanations of Vedic terms and words were nothing but guesses, having no foundation than the individual opinion of a scholar who appeared to have thought his own conjectures to be superior to the opinions of the greatest divines of India." (Sandhyavandanam, p. iv).

Whitney supported Roth: "The principles of the 'German School' are the only ones which can ever guide us to a true understanding of the Veda." But now such statements can easily be dismissed as ravings in the wilderness of scholars who in the past too often condescendingly scratched one another's back.

Roth was not the only German scholar who thought unduly very high of himself of his scholarship and of the German science of philology. Macdonnel was of the opinion that "the sole aim here being the attainment of truth, it is a positive advantage that the translators of ancient sacred books should be outsiders rather than the native custodians of such writing. The later could not escape religious bias, and orthodox Brahmans could not possibly do so."

Roth was more outspoken and he gave expression to his scholarly feelings in an uninhibited language: "It would be a disgrace to the criticism and ingenuity of our century which has deciphered the stone inscriptions of the Persian Kings and the Book of Zoroster, if it did not succeed in reading in this enormous literature the intellectual history of the Hindu race." And he continues: "A European Sanskritist was better qualified to arrive at the true meaning of the Rigveda than a Brahmin interpreter, for unlike the latter, he had no theological bias, he possessed the historical faculty and had also a fair, wider intellectual horizon and all the resources of modern scientific knowledge."

It seems very likely that the 19th century Sanskritists (scholars of Vedic-Sanskritd) hailing mostly from Germany operated more like those unscrupulous members who had insidiously formed themselves into a cartel with the dishonest purpose of preventing all outsiders, the Hindus in particular, from entering the field of Vedic scholarship. Since they considered the true interpretation of Veda mantras as a great threat to the Christian cultus of the Trinity they seemed eager not only to give misleading meanings to Vedic text but appeared also to be always manoeuvring for their garbled interpretations and versions to be accepted by everyone else as true and correct. Several members of this cartel happened mysteriously to be working upon identical hypotheses. They propounded the theories that the Germans were in a better position, and more able, to interpret the Vedic texts; that the work of translation meant only deciphering; and, after they themselves had made a mess of the work of translation, they asserted that this work was so difficult that the work of translating the Rigveda may have to wait for another century or perhaps, which they thought it more likely, indefinitely!

This was not the end of it, however. After each one of them had contrived to dupe the world with his clever concoctions he endeavoured to cover up the results of his ineptitude and delinquency with another scholarly gloss. He looked around for an alibi and soon piously pretended that his interest in the Rigveda had been only historical. To quote Max Muller in this context: "My interest in all religions is chiefly historical; I want to see what has been, in order to understand what is." While writing to Byramjee Malabari he had told him: "Accept the Veda as an ancient historical document, thoughts in accordance with he character of an ancient and simple-minded race of men. . . :M Max Muller had preconceived notions about the Vedas as a result of which he looked for history in them. It is for this reason perhaps that he translated many Vedic words to mean proper nouns!

Though Max Muller had acknowledged the great truth that Vedic-Sanskrita texts had multiple meanings he chose only those which served his ends most. His aim was to translate the Veda from the standpoint of a (Christian) missionary and this objective could not possibly be achieved if he translated the Veda mantras in the Yaugika sense. Therefore, he chose mostly rudhi meanings because they served his purpose more admirably. What great havoc can possibly be done to the meanings of Vedic texts by use of rudhi meanings would be clear from an examination of rudhi and Yaugika meanings of a few words in the following list.

Words Yaugika meaning is Common place meaning

given in Nighantu (rudhi)

(ahi) cloud (I-10) snake

(adi) cloud (I-10) stone or hill

(varah) cloud (1-10) Bear

(gauri) speech (1-10) wife of Mahadeva

(shachi) works (II-1) wife of Indira

Intelligence (III-9)

(pureesh) water (1-12) excreta

It is not difficult to imagine how easily a translator might go astray if instead of taking Yaugika meaning of gauri and shachi he were to translate these words into proper nouns. Max Mullcr had acknowledged: "every word retains something of its radical meaning and further, names are to be found in the Vedas as it were in a still fluid state. They never appear as appelatives, nor yet as proper names." But despite this admission he did not see reason and refused to translate the Vedic texts according to guidelines that had been laid down for this purpose long, long ago.

Like most other European scholars Max Muller conveniently ignored the fact that Nirukta of Yasaka being confessedly a book of much anterior date as compared to the Veda commentaries made by Madhavacharya; Sayana and Mahidhara it {Nirukta) must inevitably take precedence over all these commentaries. Nirukta lays down in the very beginning guidelines saying that the terms used in the Vedas are Yaugika which means they possess derived meanings as against rudhi meanings which represent only the conventional. This principle as enunciated by Yasaka in his Nirukta is further supported by Panini in hisAshtadhyyayi and by Patanjali in his Mahabhashya.

In the 19th century while Max Muller was alive this important matter had received the attention of Pandit Guru Datta Vidyarthi, a great scholar and exponent of Vedic-Sanskrita. He commented: "Speaking broadly, three classes of words are used in the Sanskrita language; the yaugik, the rudhi and the yoga-nidhi words. A yaugika word is one that has a derivative meaning, that is, one that only signifies the meaning of its root together with the modifications effected by the affixes. In fact, the structural elements, out of which the word is compounded, afford the whole and the only clue to the true signification of the word. These being known, no other element is needed to complete its sense. ... A rudhi word is the name of a definite concrete object, or answers to a definite concrete technical sense not by virtue of any of its connotations but by virtue merely of an arbitrary principle.... A third class of words, yoga-rudhi, is one in which two words are synthetically combined into a compound, denoting a third object by virtue of the combination of these two words. Such words express any relation or interaction of phenomena."

As European scholars ignored the guidelines laid down for the translation of Vedic texts they too easily went palpably wrong. While most of them made silly and laughable mistakes some others being ignorant novices chose to dabble in great subjects with the insensibility and unconcern of an amateur making in consequence inexcusable blunders. As a result they too often made a laughing stock of themselves.

That the work of translating the Vedic texts (into any language) was not an easy one was very well known to Swami Dayananda. He had considered this question carefully and then had laid down the qualifications for an interpreter of the Veda mantras. According to him: "The mantras cannot be interpreted in an off-hand manner on hearing them or with the help of reasoning alone. They ought to be explained with due regard to their context i.e. with reference to what precedes as well as to what follows. But a man who is not a rishi, who has not performed the austerities (tapas), whose mind is not pure and who does not possess learning cannot eratise meanings of mantras."

But all this advice fell on deaf ears and European scholars of Vedic-Sanskrita merrily went about their job of translating the Rigveda as it suited the whims and fancies of their bigoted minds.

It is neither very rare nor at all difficult for a translator to go wrong when engaged on a difficult work of translation. Even an easy job would become understandably much more difficult if neither of the two languages involved is, as was the case with Max Muller, the mother tongue of the translator. But in all such cases a sincere translator would be painfully conscious of his shortcomings and would refrain from going about his work in a cavalier manner. He would be humble and would not jauntily disregard and ignore established norms and canons laid down for such work by people who have gone before him.

Though Max Muller had all the means to correct himself within his easy reach he designedly shjjt his eyes and mind to prevent light from entering his heart and this made his high-sounding claims to profound scholarship all the more suspect. In consequence, at times he appeared in some of his translations so phoney and stupid that within a short time after his death independent scholars throughout the world were obliged to put him aside despite all the efforts of Christian missions to keep his name and memory alive.

Max Muller was too astute and leery for the common man. He was punctilious in the choice of his words which he used often with the double purpose of humouring Christian demagogues and simultaneously to disparage those toward whom he did not want to be openly hostile. At times he would tell a damaging truth but in a manner that no one would care to take much notice of it. At the time when he delivered his fifth Hibbert Lecture he openly made a noteworthy remark which was sufficient to damn him as a biased and committed translator. While dilating on the difficulties which anyone engaged on the work of translating the Rigveda would face he told his listeners: "The great difficulty in all discussions of this kind arises from the fact that we have to transfuse thought from ancient into modern forms. In that process some violence is inevitable."

Some violence is inevitable? And without doubt he did do violence to many a Rig mantra and lamentably while most of it was the result of Max Muller's shallow and inadequate knowledge of Vedic-Sanskrita grammar (Nirukta and Ashtadhyyayi) some of it was deliberate and intentional. Some violence was inevitable because all the while Max Muller had been pre-resolved to translate the Rig from the standpoint of a missionary. And, a Christian missionary's standpoint is, nearly always, only to denounce and denigrate every other religious faith except his own brand of Christianity.

In his anxiety to appear honest the German philologist meticulously acted in a manner which would be expected only of a scholar of great honour and of unimpeachable integrity. Simulating praiseworthy condescension and fearing ultimately to be disrobed by independent scholarship he voluntarily chose to drop the veil and show up himself. He repeatedly attempted to establish his own innocence and bona fides in the matter all the while skillfully making use of more than one artifices and subterfuges to cover up his misdeeds and failings as a translator. He had to invent for himself alibis and look around for escape routes where none really existed.

He began by offering pleas and explanations which at times looked really plausible in his mouth. In order to impart to his arguments the force of truth he continued to assert whenever a chance offered itself to him that (i) the Vedic hymns were very difficult; (ii) the hymns were childish and (to him) made no sense; (iii) he had only tried to he-a decipherer of the Vedas and not a translator; (iv) the correct translation of the Rig will have to wait for another century or perhaps till eternity and above all (v) that in trying to translate the Rigveda (as done by him) some violence (to Rig. mantras) was inevitable.

Violence is always a deliberate and, often, a premeditated act on the part of the doer and it invariably involves an intention and will to commit it; it just does not happen to be committed by itself or by someone just wishing it. It is never inevitable in the manner as Max Muller suggested. It might, and did, become inevitable only because Max Muller had already set for himself a particular goal which, he very well knew, he could not reach unless he committed violence. To achieve his objective, therefore, he gave wrong meanings to Rig mantras at times by first twisting them out of shape into an unintelligible mess and then complaining that several hymns yielded no sense. That is how perhaps whenever a Veda mantra made no sense to him he used violence to make nonsense out of it!

It was more than once that Max Muller admitted his shortcomings as a scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita. He freely owned that while he was busy with the work of translating the Rigveda he took help from Sayana but: "over and over again was I stopped by some short enigmatical reference to Panini's grammar or Yasaka's glossary, which I could not identify. . . . How often I was in perfect despair, because there was some allusion in Sayana which I could not make out, and which no other Sanskrit scholar, not even Bournouf or Wilson could help me to clear up. It often took me whole days, nay weeks, before I saw light."

In spite of all the admissions made by Max Muller so voluntarily about his Vedic scholarship he continued, nevertheless, to hold the stage, nearly exclusively to himself, for about half a century. Max Muller could not, as he admitted, understand Sayana although Sayana wrote Sanskrit which belonged only to the fourteenth century. It can easily be imagined how far more difficult Max Muller must have found the Vedic (language) of the Vedas which Max Muller accepted as the "oldest books in the library of mankind." The scholar extraordinary of the Rigveda deserved all the pity of the world but instead he was hailed, and received recognition, as a Sanskrita scholar of unparalleled merit!

Max Muller had been interested more in furthering Christian mission interests than in rendering a faithful translation of the Rig-veda. Therefore, when Swami Dayananda censured him Max Muller reacted in the only manner that could be expected of an average Christian missionary. He tried to ridicule the Swami in a letter which he wrote to Byramjee Malabari. Max Muller who held, in the early years of his career, very-high opihion of his Sanskrita scholarship seemed to have mellowed and grown more sober as he advanced in years. As he saw death approaching him he thought of making a confession. In a letter which he wrote (September 28,1898) to Pandit Chavilal of Kathmandu (Nepal) only about two years before his death he frankly admitted the inadequacy of his knowledge of Sanskrita. Since this letter had been kept out of Max Muller's first biography (The Life and Letters) and found no space in Scholar Extraordinary we are taking liberty of producing the Letter here in full.

"7 Norham Cards, Oxford, 28th September, 1898. Pandit Chhavilal, Dear Sir, Accept my best thanks for your Natakas, Sundara Charita and Kushalavodaya, the Vrithalankara, and the Sanskrit verses addressed to me. As soon as I find time I hope to read your two plays, but I am getting so old (75) and have still so much to do, that I have but little leisure left to me. I am surprised at your familiarity with Sanskrit. We, in Europe, shall never be able to rival you in that. We have to read but never to write Sanskrit. To you it seems as easy as English or Latin is to us. You see, we chiefly want to know what India is and has been — we care for its literature, its philosophy, etc., and that takes up so much time, that we never think of practising composition, that we ca , Admire all tb? -i.jnv because we cannot rival, and I certainly was tilled with auu-uation when I read but a few pages of your Sundara Charita.

"And now a question. Mr. Bahramji Malabari is publishing at Bombay (India Spectator) translation of my Hibbert Lectures in Marathi, Bengali, Gujarati, Tamil, etc., he is very anxious to find a scholar to translate them into Sanskrit. One translation was made, but it was too imperfect.

Would you undertake that work? Of course, you would be paid for you trouble. Or could you recommend any friend of yours, who is competent to undertake such a work? And would you write direct to B.M. Malabari, India Spectator Office, Bombay, inform him of what could be done in Nepal.

"Thanking you once more for your valuable presents, I remain, Yours faithfully, F. Max Muller."

It was perhaps the first time that Max Muller had so candidly confessed to anyone that he was no scholar of Sanskrita and much less of Vedic-Sanskrita? Very plainly he had admitted that he was not capable of writing flawless Sanskrit. What he could do was only to read (perhaps that too incorrectly!) what some others had written and that too in all probability without being sure whether all that he was reading had been written correctly or incorrectly! Yet Max Muller had been put into world-wide circulation as the greatest and as an extraordinary scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita like a counterfeit coin among the innocent public who neither had the capacity to judge its genuineness nor the ability to discard it even if they wished. In his letter to Pandit Chhavilal Max Muller actually confirmed what Schopenhauer had said about the ability and the capacity of European scholars of Sanskrita. In his opinion: "I add to this the impression which the translation of Sanskrit words by European scholars, with very few exceptions, produce on my mind. I cannot resist a certain suspicion that our Sanskrit scholars do not understand their text much better than the higher class of school boys their Greek and Latin." It seems Schopenhauer had in his mind Sanskrit scholars like Max Muller.

We have now covered sufficient ground to prove that Max Muller was no great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita as he is often made out to be. At best he was only a diligent and promising student of the Vedas who had still to learn his vaiyakarana (grammar). " In his Panini Goldstucker called to question Max Muller's Sanskrita scholarship by pointing to a grossly misconceived translation of a very simple Sanskrita word. We shall let Goldstucker speak for himself:

"I doubt, however, whether his rendering of "those also who defile the Vedas" is quite correct. It seems to me that it means "those who corrupt the texts of the Veda.:," and that it is synonymous with the expression \* which occurs in the second act of the Prabodha-Chandrodaya (ed. Brockhaus, p. 20, 1.41; ed. Calc. p. 12a, 1.5)."

Here may be stated what another Gentian scholar admitted of his scholarship. Abrecht Weber confessed:

"... in I. St., i.10, I have translated, doubtless incorrectly, the expression Chatuhshasti-kala-sastra (cited in the Piasthana-bheda as part of the Artha-sastra by 'treatise on chess,' referring the 64 kalas to the 64 squares of the chess-board; whereas, according to the As. Res. 1.341 (Schlegal Reflex, sur 1'Etude des Langues Asiat, p. 112), it signifies 'treatise on the 64 wits?" (The History of Indian Literature by Abrecht Weber, p. 275, footnote, London 1882).

## 6 - Max Muller's Translations

It would be unjust and unfair to censor a man on insufficient grounds. We know that Max Muller flouted all established norms for translating the vedic-Sanskrita texts. Now, we shall show what great mess he made, as a result, of the work of translation that had been entrusted to him. We shall examine, though necessarily only briefly, Max Muller's translations of a few of the Veda mantras.

The first mantra of the Rigveda of Mandala I and Sukta 162 is:

and Max Muller translated this to mean: May Mitra, Varuna, Aryaman, Ayu, Indira, the lord the Ribhus, and the Maruts not rebuke us, because we shall proclaim at the sacrifice the virtues of the swift horse sprung from the gods.

It seems that Max Muller laboured hard under the impression that Mitra meant 'the) god of the sky"; Varuna, the 'god of the investing sky"; Aryaman, the 'god of death'; Ayu, the 'god of the winds'; Indira, the 'god of the watery atmosphere' and he carried this impression because in his Occidental wisdom he thought that Mitra; Varuna; Aryaman; Ayu and Indira all were proper names. Being under this fallacious impression he refused to give these vedic words appearing in the mantra their yaugika meanings. In consequence more than once he looked grossly stupid in some of his translations.

"This Sukta is an exposition of ashva vidya which means the art of training horses and the science of heat which pervades everywhere in the shape of electricity."

Literally speaking, Mitra means a friend; Varuna a man of noble qualities; Aryaman a judge or an administrator of justice; Ayu a learned man and Indira, a governor; ribhuksha, a wise man and Maruts those who practically observe the law of seasons. The word ashva was translated by Max Muller to mean a horse as is commonly understood by a man in the street in Tndia without realizing that its yaugika meaning is the group of three forces of heat, electricity and magnetism. It in fact means anything that would carry one soon and speedily though distance and space.

That ashva means heat would be clear from the Rigveda itself:

The words ashvam agnim mean agni (heat). The Rig again tells us . I:XXVII: 1):

which means agni, the ashva, carries, like an animal of conveyance, the learned (men) who thus recognize its distance-carrying properties.

Thus the correct meaning of the mantra would be: we shall describe the power-generating virtues of the energetic horses endowed with brilliant properties, or the virtues of the vigorous force of heat which the learned or the men of science can evoke to work for purposes of appliances (not sacrifice). Let not philanthropists, noble men, judges, learned men, rulers, wise men and practical men (mechanics) ever disregard these properties.

Max Muller translated the second mantra (Rig I: 162:2):

to mean: "when they lead before the horse, which is decked with pure gold ornaments, the offering firmly grasped, the spotted goat bleats while walking onwards; it goes the path beloved by Indira and Pushan."

Max Muller's translation makes no sense. The bleating of the spotted goat has no connection with the leading of the offering before the horse, nor any with its walking onward. And there is no relationship between the first mantra that preceded this one. Max Muller's translation betrays his childish perversity and in addition not only his utter lack of knowledge of true (yaugika) meanings of some of these words but also dismal absence of common sense. He translated reknasas into 'pure gold ornaments' whereas according to Nighantu (ii:10) it only means 'wealth'. He translated 'Rati' which signifies the mere act of 'giving' into 'offering'; vishvarupa which means 'one having an idea of all forms' into 'spotted'! Max Muller's rendering of 'memayat' from root mi meaning to injure, into 'bleating' is highly risible. He must have picked up this meaning from some jolly Indian jester who wanting perhaps to pull Max Muller's leg might have advised him that memyana was a verb (in an Indian dialect) meaning to bleat and this seems to have gone straight home with the German scholar of Sanskrita! Max Muller had another big fall when he translated pathah which means 'drink or food' into path (road) on which people walk. In this instance too he must have been fooled by some uninformed from India telling him lhaipathah means (path), road etc! And as usual, and this appeared to be an addiction with him, Max Muller translated Indira and Pushan into proper names! Had Max Muller been honest and sincere about the work he had been doing he would surely have known that Yasaka had written about the word, pathah (VI:7)

Mukhato nayanti in truth means, 'they bring out of the organ of speech' or 'they explain or preach' but hi his great scholarly wisdom Max Muller translated these words to mean "they lead before". In one mantra alone there are at least nine words which Max Muller translated grossly wrong and in spite of all this he was acclaimed as the greatest Sanskrita scholar!

The second mantra would correctly mean: They who preach that only wealth earned by righteous means should be appropriated and spent, and those born in wisdom, who are well versed in questioning others elegantly, in the science of forms and in correcting the unwise, these and such along drink the potion of strength and of power to govern.

We shall now take up the third maqtra (Rig: 1:162:3):

and -append below the translation which Max Muller gave to the world: "This goat destined for all the gods, is led first with the quick horse, as Pushan's share; for Tvashtri himself raises to glory this pleasant offering which is brought with the horse."

Max Muller had been hired and paid to translate the Rigveda into English but it did not necessarily mean that he was paid to translate it correctly! This apart, the people who hired him and paid him £ 200 a year for this job had no means whatever to ascertain whether the muck and non-sensical literary rubbish which he shoved on to them was really faithful translation of the Rig. Max Muller was able to make a big fool of the Englishman who as a result of having been intoxicated with political power had lost his capacity to discriminate between truth and falsehood.

The translation, or whatever one might choose to call it, of the third mantra is yet one more startling example of Max Muller's so called extraordinary learning and Sanskrita scholarship! He did not hesitate for a while to ask himself as to how (one) goat could be 'destined' for all gods' and yet also be 'Pushan's share' alone? The word vishvadevyas translated into 'destined for all gods' can grammatically perhaps mean something like this but at best it would mean 'for a}l the devas' but the word 'destined' is a wanton ungrammatical addition. Vishvadevya is formed from vishvadevya by addition of the suffix yat in the sense of tatra sadhu (ref. Ashtadhyyayi, IV:4:98). Max Muller again translated Pushan into proper noun. He translated tvashtri also into a proper noun. He had to do this only because he had first made the goat to be led as Pushan's share! He did not care to explain who was Tvashtri and what was the relationship with Pushanl

A particular part of the translation of the third mantra as rendered by Max Muller deserves special attention. The words 'which is brought with' is Max Muller's own invention concocted to put some iota of sense into something which without these four words would have been the height of nonsense. Saushravasaya Jinvati which means 'obtains for purpose of good food' (shravas in Vedic-Sanskrita means food or anna, ( 3F=r ) was translated by Max Muller into 'raises to glory"!

The correct meaning which will have sensible relationship with the first two mantras which preceded it would be like this: The goat possessed of useful properties yields milk as a strengthening food for horses. The best cereal is useful when made into pleasant food well-prepared by an apt cook according to the modes dictated by specific knowledge of the properties of foods.

Max Muller translated the remaining 19 mantras of this hymn of the Rig. in similarly atrocious manner. For instance, in the fifth mantra (". . . may you fill the streams round the altar with a sacrifice which is well-prepared and well accomplished.") the words 'round the altar' were interpolated by Max Muller without any reason perhaps because he thought that a sacrifice must always be conducted at an altar! The word yajna which originally indicated any action requiring association of men or objects and productive of beneficial results is always translated by European scholars to mean sacrifice! The notion of sacrifice is Judaic or Christian in its concept but has no place in Vedic philosophy.

In the ninth mantra Max Muller did not understand the true significance of the words ashvasya kravishah which he translated to mean the flesh of the horse! Kravishah is an adjective qualifying ashvasya giving to the whole the meaning: 'of the pacing horse'. Kravishah does not mean 'of the flesh' but 'pacing' from the root kram - to pace.

In the tenth mantra, Amasya kravisho meaning 'raw food yet undigested and not disposed to come out' was translated by the great German scholar in a cavalier manner to mean 'raw flesh'. Ama is the state of the undigested food in the stomach (belley). Max Muller was unable to follow even the structure of the mantra.

In the eleventh mantra he wrongly translated Ayain Pachyamauad (forced by the heat of anger) into 'roasted'. In the same mantra Max Muller translated hatasya which means 'propelled' into 'killed'.

Max Muller's translation of the twelfth mantra is especially noteworthy. The word vajinam from vaja meaning cereals was taken by him to mean 'horse'! The great Sanskritist was so anxious to bring forth the sense of the sacrifice of the horse that not content with this he interpreted mansa bhiksham uapste which means 'he serves the absence of meat' into 'serves the meat'. There can perhaps be no greater proof of his perverted mind and worm-eaten scholarship.

While translating the 18th mantra he said: "The axe approaches the 34 ribs of the quick horse. . ." Max Muller's translation of vankri which means a 'vig zag motion' into rib is risible.

We come last to the 19th mantra. Once again Max Muller gave ample proof of his utter incompetence. He translated Twashtu vashvasya to mean 'brilliant horse' (he seemed to be in love with the horse!) as if ashva were the noun and tvashta its qualifying adjective. Actually the reverse is the truth. As Max Muller must have felt great difficulty with his own translation he interpolated the words 'offered to the gods' en his own volition without any reason whatever.

In the 21st mantra he once again gave Had its rudhi meaning: 'two horses of Indira' and prishati: 'two deer of Maruts'. The shaft of the ass' ("Indeed thou diest not thus, thou sufferest not; thou goest to the gods on easy paths. The two horses of Indira, the two deer of the Maruts have been yoked, and the horse come to the shaft of the ass (of the asvins") makes Max Muller himself look like an ass of a Sanskrit scholar in his translations. Max Muller's 'shaft of the ass' still continues to remain, more than 90 years after Max Muller's death, an unsolved mystery created by the genius of Max Muller — the scholar extraordinary whom the East India Company had discovered with great difficulty and whom the Company had hired at greater expense.

It was Sayana whom Max Muller had chosen and accepted as his guide and mentor. As the guide himself happened to be ill informed and not sufficiently groomed in the great language he pulled Max Muller also into the same abyss from which at times he had found it difficult to extricate himself. Max Muller had known all this too well but since he had an axe to grind he feigned ignorance.

Sayana was no scholar of the Vedas or of Vedic-Sonskrita. He was well aware of his shortcomings which, as an honest man, he readily admitted more than once. He had no ulterior motives; he translated the Veda more to satisfy his religious urge or his curiosity. It was altogether a different matter with Max Muller. Since Max Muller's intention and purpose was to uproot Hinduism he translated the Rig. from the standpoint of a Christian missionary which fact completely vitiated his efforts as a translator.

In spite of his honest intentions Sayana had failed to do justice to the Rigveda. Nevertheless, Sayana's commentary of the Rigveda came to be more widely known than any other commentary by any other commentator-living or dead. This does not, however, mean that Sayana's commentary has been, or ever was, accepted as the only or as the most authoritative commentary as Max Muller tried to give the impression. Aurobindo Ghosh was one who did not agree with Max

Mullet. According to him: "It is not possible to follow either Sayana's method or his results without the largest reservations ... if is the central defect of Sayana's system that he is obsessed always by the ritualistic formula and seeks continually to force the sense of the Veda into the narrow mould."

What Sri Aurobindo said about Sayana may not in all fairness apply to Max Muller although Sayana was the model for the German scholar. Being a mercenary Max Muller was not free to express his views without seeking favours or without being under the fear of losing his lucrative assignment. And all this resulted into translations which were not faithful translations and which happened to be not only absurd and wrong but which also presented the sense and the meanings of the Veda mantras in a wilfully and deliberately distorted fashion.

## 7 - The Ugly Evangelist

The German scholar was a crusading evangelist who loved to fight his battles from behind fortifications especially rigged up for his protection. Always itching to arm themselves with arguments against Hinduism the missionaries constantly looked toward Max Muller for him to tell them some things new about the Vedas and their religion. He instructed and briefed the missions in a manner that mightily pleased them. Though it was not unusual for him to tell them, in the course of his lectures, some camouflaged untruths they willingly accepted them as gospel truths only because they came from Max Muller.

Max Muller was a small-hearted man whom providence had placed in a position from where he could conveniently talk of big things. He seldom liked anyone else to be given credit for anything big or important. He was a jealous scholar who often quipped, though only discreetly, over the accomplishments of others with the calculated purpose of making himself look more important in comparison. He knew it too well all that Raja Rammohun Roy had done, and achieved, in his lifetime to thwart the advance of Christianity in India. The Raja and been dead for 50 years when in 18-33 the elite of London, Oxford and Bristol had collected to pay their tribute to Rammohun Roy at his fiftieth death anniversary. Choosing carefully this special occasion to pay his tribute Max Muller found fault with this great son of India more than once in the course of his address. He said:

"No doubt he admitted Christianity more than any other religion; I think we may truly say he admitted it more than his own; yet for all that, he remained a Brahman, and was therefore in the eyes of most of the people who received him in England, a non-Christian, or a heathen."

Whenever Max Muller chose to talk of religious matters concerning either Hinduism or the Vedas he talked like a master dissembler in a language which can only be described as mischievously diabolic. He made free and careful use of suggestio falsi and suppressio veri to register desired impact on the virgin mind of his listeners. Nevertheless, it goes to his great credit, however, that he was able to do it so smoothly that too often he remained undiscovered. While delivering his address he told his listeners that he (MM) had no doubt (i.e. MM was absolutely sure) that Raja Rammohun Roy admitted Christianity more than any other religion and yet he only thought ("I think") that the Raja perhaps admitted Christianity more than his own religion (Vedic Hinduism because Rammohun Roy did not subscribe to popular Hinduism as practised in his days). And as the Serampore missionaries had done he called Raja Rammohun Roy a heathen knowing too well how strongly Rammohun Roy had reacted to this description in 1820. Since Max Muller, knew that Rammohun Roy could not have come back to defend himself against this charge he chivalrously chose to call Rammohun Roy a heathen at his back!

At the time when Max Muller delivered his address, the Raja had been dead for 50 years. Knowing that there was then no one among his listeners who would venture to contradict him or protest Max Muller called the Raja a heathen with no qualms. It was truly this word which had given Rammohun Roy an umbrage that had caused him to lash out at Serampore missionaries and, later, to write his three Appeals. Max Muller conveniently ignored all those arguments which Rammohun Roy had offered to prove that he could not be named a heathen. Max Muller did not seem to believe that one should speak nothing but good of the dead. He thought instead that there was nothing wrong in talking ill and blasphemously of those who had been dead provided it profited a clever living man and advanced his fake reputation as a great scholar.

Max Muller was scholar of an unusually high calibre but he seemed to suffer from a constantly biased mind. At times he would not hesitate even to insinuate if it suited his convenience and for example he did this, in the course of his address; ". . . but the statement that, at the age of sixteen, he composed in Persian a treatise against the idolatry of all religions - a bold subject for a man of sixty, much more for a boy of sixteen - rests on authority that may be doubted."

Max Muller was a master quibbler who took great delight in talking with mental reservations. He made subtle use of every intellectual artifice to record a favourable impression on the receptive minds of his audiences. After finding fault with Rammohun Roy he said something about him which did not go well with what he had said about him earlier: "And yet I like to call Rammohun Roy a great man, using that word, not as a cheap, unmeaning title, but as conveying three essential elements of manly greatness, namely unselfishness, honesty, and boldness."

In the earlier part of his address Max Muller had doubted if Rammohun Roy had really written his Tuhfat-ul-Muhaviddin (which Max Muller called Gift to Monotheists) when Rammohun Roy was only about sixteen years young. Max Muller virtually accused Rammohun Roy in absentia of having told a lie. He threw aspersions on all those biographers of Raja Rammohun Roy who in the past had confirmed the fact that Rammohun Roy did write that treatise in Persian when he was just a lad of about sixteen years. In his great wisdom (Max Muller was then 60 years old) the great philologist thought it as something impossible for a heathen so young as that to have performed such a literary feat!

Max Muller was a big courtier but a small man. As a small man he was not free of spite when he delivered his address at the fiftieth death anniversary of the man whom the world had recognised of its own accord and free will as a grea. cholar - greater than any who then lived. One of them, a follower of the same religion which Max Muller professed, had paid tribute to Rammohun Roy in words which must have given the German scholar some heart burns. And the thought that even 50 years after his death the Englishman should have wanted to pay the Raja a tribute must have painfully rankled in Max Muller's heart. This man, while commenting on Rammohun Roy's writings, had made the observation:

"It is a masterly exposure, by a native, of the absurdities of the present Hindu system. While the work is argumentative in high degree, it is interspersed with observations which for keenness of satire would scarcely have disgraced the pen of Lucian.

What European could have written a work equally delicate and equally severe in its application, and who that had read these extracts will not say that our author, though unassisted by the advantage which we possess, has not exhibited such force of reasoning, such strength of intellect, as with them would have a placed him in the foremost rank of defenders of truth! We appeal to those who have accompanied us through this work, whether a native equal to such a production would find himself unequal to the comprehension of Bacon or Locke or Butler or Howel"

It was within Max Muller's knowledge that, according to the biographer of Lord Macaulay with whom Max Muller had a long interview in 1855, Macaulay had shown unusual capabilities even when he was only a child of 3 or 4 years. But Max Muller did not disbelieve the statements made by Sir George Otto Trevelyan in the manner he disbelieved Raja Rammohun Roy. He would have neither the courage nor the manliness to open his mouth even half as wide. He was aware that Macaulay belonged to the ruling race while Raja Rammohun Roy came from a country then held in political bondage by Max Muller's masters. And in addition Rammohun Roy being a heathen and a Hindu Max Muller did not expect him to have unusual talents as to enable him to write a small tract in Persian even though this language happened to be so common among that class of aristocracy to which Rammohun Roy's family belonged.

In the People of India its learned author says: "These ideas are not the monopoly of the learned, they are shared in great measure by the man in the street. If you talk to a fairly intelligent Hindu peasant about the Paramatma, Karma, Maya, Mukti, and so forth, you will find as soon as he has got over his surprise at your interest in such matters that the terms are familiar to him, and that he has formed a rough working theory of their bearing on his own future."

Had Max Muller been alive when Sir Risley wrote these lines he would have surely suspected the truth of Sir Risley's statement. He might have exclaimed how was it possible for a peasant and a man in the street in India to have a fair knowledge of subjects like Mukti; Maya and Karma since these were bold subjects even for a man who had spent about 50 years in Oxford translating the Vedal It would have been difficult to convince him that talent was not hidden only under a white skin!

It seems Max Muller had completely and very conveniently forgotten that he had been given the assignment to translate the Rig-veda from Vedic-Sanskrita into English when he was only about 24 years young. This was in spite of the fact that neither of the two languages happened to be his mother tongue and that he was not fully conversant with Panini without whose aid it has never been possible to comprehend the correct meanings of Veda mantras. All this notwithstanding he had thought the job to be so easy that he imagined he could do it well. He had tried to hold Rammohun Roy and his profound scholarship to ridicule merely because in the face of it he himself felt too small. Though Raja Rammohun Roy had been dead the philosophizing proselytizer still appeared to be suffering from the impact which Rammohun Roy's arguments had produced on entire Christendom more than fifty years ago. But all this did not stop Max Muller. He spitted more venom:

"He arrived at Liverpool on the 8th of April, 1831, and, after a short stay, proceeded by Manchester to London. You may read, in most of the biographical accounts of the Rajah, how he was received, how he was the lion of the season, how he was presented to the king, called on by dukes and duchesses, feasted by aldermen and the directors of the East India Company; how he went to Paris, and dined twice with the king, Louis Phillip, and elsewhere,.... We have hardly time to do more than to point out the few really important events during his stay in England, - how, when at Liverpool, he was invited by William Roscoe to shake hands with him on his death bed; how William Bentham, the utilitarian philosopher, who had secluded himself from all the world, was the first to call on the Rajah, of whom he used to say, 'He has cast off three hundreds and thirty millions of gods, and has learnt from us to embrace reason in the all important field of religion;' how he knew Henry Brougham not yet banished to the House of Lords; how he gave important evidence before several Parliamentary Committees at the time of renewal of the Charter of the East India Company; how lastly, as soon as he could free himself, he carried out his long cherished wish of going to Bristol, a city famous at that time as the home of Dr. Carpenter, John Foster, Dr. Jerrard, Dr. Symonds, Mr. Estlin, Dr. Pritchard, and others - men known not only for their learning, but for their liberal spirit."

When Raja Rammohun Roy visited England and Paris people had warmly welcomed him and held numerous more-than-fricndly receptions in his honour. Max Muller who had come to know of all these accounts did not approve of all this deference having been shown to a heathen. He felt annoyed and furiously jealous that so much attention should have been showered upon and importance given to a Hindu who had done more than anyone else to denounce and denigrate biblical Tr.nitarianism. His anger was understandable. Inside it made him feel miserable when he realized that despite his having done so much for Christianity he had received not even a part of that honour and affection which had so generously been bestowed upon Raja Rammohun Roy - the man who had stood like a solid unconquerable wall between Christian missions and the conversion of the Hindus of India to Christianity. He was right but he appeared to have completely forgotten that Raja Rammohun Roy had been honoured because though he went there from a country held in political bondage his soul was free; Max Muller had been ignored and neglected for having pawned his soul to feed his body. He was not able even perhaps then to understand the national trait of the Englishman. The Englishman never respected a man whose friendship he had to purchase. But then it was perhaps too late for Max Muller to make any amends.

Max Muller held very high opinion of himself, his scholarship and his knowledge of the Rigyeda. He would have, he so thought, beaten Rammohun Roy hollow at the game of Unitarianism versus Trinitarianism if only he had been Rammohun Roy's contemporary. He made this plain when he spoke on September 27, 1883. He was full of sarcasm when he said:

"This was a bold assertion, half true, half false, as we know now, but an assertion which at that time no one could venture to criticise or contradict.

Now it may sound strange, but I feel convinced that Rammohun Roy himself, when, in his controversies with his English friends, he fortified himself behind ramparts of the Veda, had no idea of what the Veda really was. Vedic learning was then at a low ebb in Bengal, and Rammohun Roy had never passed through a Regular training in Sanskrit".

In his address the great philologist had made another significant and remarkable assertion though he did it more perhaps to humour his Christian listeners than with any intention of offering a counter argument to any which Rammohun Roy had used in his lifetime to demolish the cult of the Trinity. Max Muller had said: ". . . on no account be led to believe that Trinitarianism was part of Christ's own teaching. As then taught by many of the missionaries in India, doctrine of the three Persons, that is the three aspects or manifestations of the Godhead, had been hardened into mere Tritheism ...."

He had offered this advice as a sop to his fellow religionists who, sitting before him in Bristol Museum, must have been pondering afresh on the arguments Rammohun Roy had successfully employed to knock out Trinitarianism. We only hope they went home fully re-assured. Max Muller had the audacity to question Rammohun Roy's knowledge of Sanskrit even though he knew that Rammohun Roy came from a family which had long traditions of Sanskrit learning and also that Rammohun Roy had diligently studied the language in Benares for a number of years. This notwithstanding we already know how well versed Max Muller himself had been in the subtleties of this great and sacred language.

The unmatchable reputation which Raja Rammohun Roy enjoyed in England and the high esteem in which he was held even 50 years after his death had nearly baffled and nauseated Max Muller. He understood it too well to his great discomfiture that it was Rammohun Roy who not only had fought the battle for Vedic monotheism but who had also won it single-handed against all the combined might of all Christian missions which then operated in India. When Max Muller proposed a monstrously farcical name (henotheism) for the religion of the Veda he had tried his very best to condemn the Vedic Dhartna as polytheistic but to his great dismay he had failed to carry conviction with independent scholarship. The few who believed and applauded him belonged mostly to the one or the other Christian group with an axe to grind. A large number of those who either remained indifferent or looked inclined to believe him were ignorant of the truth. Professor A.H. Sayace, who was (1876-1890) the Deputy Professor of Comparative Philology at Oxford looked with scholarly disfavour at the abortive attempt Max Muller had made to differentiate between henotheism and polytheism. Sayace was of the opinion: "Polytheism and henotheism are but two phases of the same form of religious faith, the two sides, as it were, of the same prism. It matters little whether a multitude of gods were worshipped together, or whether the worshipper addresses but one of them at the time, making him for the moment the supreme and single object of his religious reverence. In either case we have a plurality of deities, confessed explicitly in polytheism, implied in henotheism."

Professor Sayace was clear in his verdict that there was hardly any difference between henotheism as defined by Max Muller and polytheism as generally understood. Nevertheless, since Max Muller wanted to carry his point he deliberately manoeuvred his words in a manner that suited his convenience and the occasion. As there was absolutely no justification for him to brand the religion of the Veda as polytheistic he did the next possible thing. He "christened" it henotheism.

The Vedas declare in a language which is more than clear that there is Only One Supreme Being though the wise call Him by more than one Names. There is such irrefutable evidence available from the Vedas in support of monotheism that Max Muller really found it impossible, in spite of his very much wishing to be able to do it, to brand the Vedic religion as polytheistic. In order, therefore, to prove that he was true to his world which he had given to his employers he invented a Satanic definition for the religion of the Rigveda and called it henotheism.

In the past, during the years between 1820 and 1824, Christian missions had received strong rebuff from Rammohun Roy when he had effectively silenced them with his arguments in support of Vedic monotheism. Rammohun Roy traced his monotheism directly to the Vedas and to the Upanishads. While delivering his address in the Bristol Museum (September 27,1883) Max Muller had bragged that the assertion which Rammohun Roy had then made was half true and half false. He had also bewailed the fact that when Rammohun Roy made this assertion there was no one like him (MM) who could venture to criticise or contradict him. He seemingly wished to have been there, in 1820, to undo the harm which Rammohun Roy had done to the cause of Christianity and Christian missions. It was astonishing, however, that in spite of this pious wish he was not able to offer, in 1883, any tangible counter argument to contradict what Rammohun Roy had said more than 60 years ago in his three Appeals. He only threw aspersions and imputed motives to make himself look like the cLj'verer of Christianity and of Christian missions. But in spite of his best efforts the religion of the Vedas as followed by the Hindus continued to remain, as before, pure Theism — Monotheism.

When Max Muller asked his listeners not to be led to believe that Trinitarianism was part of Christ's teaching he was trying only to deceive them since Trinitarianism had in fact been introduced by Jesus himself. To begin with it was Jesus who said: "blasphemy against the Holy Ghost shall not be forgiven unto men" (Mt. XII:31). It is evident that Jesus was speaking of a third person distinct from himself. Then again Jesus is supposed to have said very explicitly: "Go ye therefore, and teach all nations, baptising them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost: teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you. . . ." (Mt. XXVIII: 19-20). The four Gospels are so full with the Holy Ghost that only some crazy listeners whose minds might have been demented and stupefied with some dope could have accepted what Max Muller doled out to them as factual. His listeners would not have forgotten Luke who had told them: "And the angel answered and said unto her, The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee . . ." (Lk. 1:35). It is clear that a different being, apart from baby Jesus who was yet to be conceived in the womb of her mother, was to come upon Mary. This proves to the hilt the existence of two different and separate beings. Then, Jesus himself admitted: My Father is greater than I. All this completes Christian Trinitarianism. What Max Muller told his listeners was just a missionary fib passed on as theological truth.

Another statement made by Max Muller needs attention. He claimed: ". . . the doctrine of the three persons, that is the three aspects or manifestations of the Godhead ..." He again had made yet another attempt to bamboozle his listeners. He seemed in fact to advise them that there was only One God (or Godhead) and what he meant by Christian Trinity was only three aspects or manifestations of One Being. This was not borne out by Christian scriptures.

Christian theologians call Christian Trinitarianism a great mystery which human mind can neither define nor solve! It is truly so. If it were possible then the entire gamut of sciences will have to be changed and overhauled. This argument notwithstanding we might examine this Christian claim from another angle. The Christians claim that the three persons (God, the Son and the Holy Ghost) are three distinct beings but are united in One. Conceding this point it must be agreed that these three co-existed in the beginning. If they really did then there must be a fourth one who was in the beginning of the Beginning before these three began to co-exist. If there was no fourth one then they must have created one another but one who is not cannot create another.

The argument advanced by Max Muller of three aspects or manifestations was unchristian in the extreme. Jesus is known by different titles but they represent only one being but God the Father; God, the Son and God, the Holy Ghost are three different beings known by three different names. They cannot thus be, and are not, one. Max Muller knew it all but was not prepared to admit it. He wanted people to believe that father and the son are not two beings! A more honest admission of Christian Trinitarianism (the cultus of three gods) was however made by Read, another Christian missionary-writer:

"There is no question about this emphasis. Not only is the Trinity expressed in creed and catechism, but it is a constantly -recurring note in Christian prayers, hymns and blessings. We baptise 'in the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Ghost,' we sing 'glory be to the Father and to the Son, and to the Holy Ghost,' we pray 'God the Father, God and Son, God the Holy Ghost.' All the Churches that are members of the World Council, as well as the Roman Catholic Church, are avowedly and explicitly Trinitarian. We find another clue to the importance of this doctrine in a curious feature of Church life and history. "It seems, to be generally true that the Christian community in every age has been nourished by a belief in the Trinity so that when this belief has waned the strength of the Christian communities that abandoned the doctrine of the Trinity have nearly always tended to die-away."

Is not the religion of the Rigveda monotheistic? Is not Christianity polytheistic? Both these points had been thrashed out (1820-1824) at considerable length between Rammohun Roy and the Baptist missionaries of Serampore. In the course of these disputations Rammohun Roy had made such merry sport of Trinitarianism that his incisive arguments had left the entire team of Baptist missionaries then headed by the Serampore trio (William Carey, Joshua Marshman and William Ward) nearly flabbergasted. They had to concede the point which Rammohun Roy repeatedly made not only in favour of Vedic monotheism but also against biblical Trinitarianism.

All that had gone between Rammohun Roy and the Baptists of Serampore had been well within Max Mullet's knowledge but as a mercenary hired to abuse and denigrate the religion of the Vedas he was not prepared to admit the truth. On the contrary he made a crude attempt to ridicule Rammohun Roy when he spoke in Bristol Museum and tried to jeer at the Vedic religion in the course of his lecture which he delivered at the University of Cambridge. He had said: "But by the side of such passages, which are few in number, there are thousands in which even so many divine beings are praised and prayed to. Even their number is sometimes given as 'thrice eleven' or thirty-three, and one poet assigns eleven gods to the sky, eleven to the earth, and eleven to the waters, the waters here intended being those of the atmosphere and the clouds."

The argument which Max Muller used was neither new nor novel. The missionaries of Scrampore had used similar arguments and Rammohun Roy had met and beaten the missionaries on their ground with their own weapons. Rammohun Roy had retaliated saying:

"Lastly, I tender by humble thanks for the Editor's (of Friend of India) kind suggestion inviting me to adopt the doctrine of the Holy Trinity; but I am sorry to find that I am unable to benefit by this advice. After I have long relinquished every idea of plurality of Gods, or of the persons of the Godhead, taught under different systems of modern Hinduism, I cannot conscientiously and consistently embrace one of a similar nature, . . . since whatever arguments can be adduced against a plurality of Gods, strike with equal force against the doctrine of a plurality of persons of the Godhead; and, on the other hand, whatever excuse may be pleaded in favour of a plurality of persons of the Deity can be offered with equal propriety in defence of Polytheism".

Before he offered this argument Rammohun Roy had made this point crystal clear in yet another way. He had advised the Baptist missionaries:

"I answer, because common sense tells us that a son, as well as a servant, must be acknowledged to be inferior to his father or master. Again we find David called the son of God, Solomon the son of God, Adam the son of God and in short the whole children of Israel denominated sons of God; yet represented in scriptures as inferior to God — their father; nay, moreover, Jesus the son of God positively declares himself to be inferior to his father, - "My father is greater thanI... .tl

Max Muller was a scholar who had been extremely fortunate in more than one ways. He was supported, both overtly and covertly, by Christian missions and the British government and he could always speak from behind fortifications which used to be seemingly especially rigged up to provide him protection. In addition he happened to come (1847) on the scene only after Raja Rammohun Roy had been dead for about 15 years.

Max Muller could hardly stand an adversary, a rival or a competitor. He had been nearly scared of Swami Dayananda but dared not to open his mouth against him fearing that the Swami would not take it lying down. Nevertheless, he did write once criticizing Swami Dayananda and his commentary on the Rigveda. He had advised Byramjee Malabari in a patronizing way. "I wished to warn against two dangers, that of undervaluing or despising the ancient natural religion, ... and that of overvaluing it, and interpreting it as-it was never meant to be interpreted, of which you may see a painful instance in Dayananda Saraswati's labours on the Veda."

Max Muller had been confiding in one of his admirers who believed, without ever having either the means or the ability to ascertain the truth, that being a great scholar of the Veda and of Sanskrita, whatever he said about Dayananda's interpretations of the Veda must necessarily be true. All this notwithstanding what Max Muller wrote in January 1882 to Malabari remained nearly a secret till Max Muller's first biography became available (1902) to the public.

When his first biography appeared in 1902 the Indians came to know for the first time the game which Max Muller had been playing. Sri Aurobindo was one of them who knew also of Dayananda's Vedic interpretations and the new name which Max Muller had invented for Hinduism. In Aurobindo's opinion:

"We are aware how modern European scholars twist away from the evidence.... One deity is addressed by the names of others as well as his own, or, most commonly, he is given as lord and king of the Universe, attributes only appropriate to the Supreme Deity. Ah, but that cannot mean, ought not to mean, must not mean the worship of one; let us invent a new word, call it henotheism. . . .a There is then nothing fantastic in Dayananda's idea that the Veda contains truth of science as well as truth of religion. I will even add my own conviction that Veda contains other truths of a science the modern world does not at all possess, and in that case, Dayananda has rather understated than overstated the depth and range of the Vedic wisdom".

Whatever Sri Aurobindo thought of Dayananda's interpretations did not fit in well with the thinking of the great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita from Germany. He thought Dayananda had interpreted the Veda as it was never meant to be interpreted. Meant to be interpreted? What did he really mean? That he knew before hand how the Veda ought to have been interpreted. That the interpretation had to be from the standpoint of a missionary so that the translation would be of help in uprooting Hinduism and in the conversion of the Hindus to Christianity!

Max Muller had clearly censured Dayananda for having interpreted the Veda differently from how it should, and ought to have been interpreted. But how did he, a child who was still learning how to walk, know how exactly the Veda ought to be interpreted? Perhaps, by some Christian-missionary instinct? Therefore, Max Muller translated and interpreted the Veda as he already knew before he started to translate how it should have been interpreted and not as the rishis, the seers of Vedic texts, had meant. Seemingly Max Muller knew what the Vedic mantras meant even before he knew their texts! And so he largely got away with his literary bunkum which he called translation for the simple reason that he had the support of powerful theo-political institutions.

Dayananda did not interpret the Veda to suit either his personal whims and fancies or to satisfy any pre-conceived religious notions. He interpreted the Veda according to the norms and canons which some others who had preceded him in time (Nighantu, Nirukta, Ashtadhyyayi and Mahabhashya) had laid down long, long ago before either Dayananda or Max Muller came on the scene. Then at whatever meanings Dayananda arrived, after diligently following these canons, he faithfully passed them on to the world. No one with an unprejudiced mind and who cared to judge the issue impartially would ever deny that Dayananda relied on, and was guided by, only those authorities who have always been, and continue to be, accepted by all Sanskrita scholars as final for the translation of the Vedas. And this was not all: Dayananda never claimed his interpretations to be infallible or the last word.

It might here be mentioned en passant that at the famous Kashi shastrath\* (Benares, 1869) Swami Dayananda was asked by Pandit Jaishri to quote an authority from the Veda against idol worship. When Dayananda recited the mantra beginning with ^T cfw ulcHiRti and interpreted it to mean that there can be no image or idol of Ishwara Pandit Jaishri objected interpreting the mantra differently. At this Dayananda had cited at once Vedic authorities (Yasaka, Panini and Patanjali) to correct Pandit Jaishri who in the face of these authorities had nothing more to say but to keep silent and accept Dayananda's interpretation as supported by Yasaka, Panini and Patanjali, to be correct.

When Swami Dayananda took a clear stand against Max Muller's Sanskrita scholarship, he had minced no words. He had nothing personal against Max Muller but when he came to judge his claims to profound scholarship the Swami was unequivocal to the point of being hurtfully blunt. He compared the German scholar, so far as his Sanskrita knowledge was concerned, to that toddler who was still learning to walk. Dayananda criticised Max Muller's renderings of Veda mantras as meaningless and self-opinionatedly absurd. He had sound reasons to condemn Max Muller's scholarship (not him) in the manner he did but Max Muller never tried or wanted to ascertain, because he dared not, the grounds on which Dayananda had formed his opinion of his learning. As the scholar in him feared to be discovered and disrobed in public to his great shame Max Muller shrewdly deferred his opinion of Dayananda till after the Swami's death!

A Christian missionary, whether regular or secular, usually lives a dual life. On the exterior he would mask his face with a thin layer of cultivated cheerfulness, a spectre of peace around him and, an all-pervading desire to help, serve and guide. Deep down in his heart he would hide behind his missionary smile, among some other things, an inbuilt rancour for all other religious faiths except his own brand of Christianity. As a secular Christian missionary Max Muller was no exception. He bore his missionary grudge against Swami Dayananda for two reasons. First, Dayananda had thwarted his plans to convert the Hindus of Indian to Christianity and secondly because by his forthright criticism of Max Muller's scholarship Dayananda had been able to demolish completely the great myth that was Max Muller the Sanskritist. Max Muller would neither forgive Dayananda nor forget the humiliation he had suffered at the hands of the Swami and the consequences flowing from Swami's criticism. Therefore, to avenge himself he chose, like a true coward, to smear Dayananda's fair name only after Dayananda had been dead.

The missionaries of the 18th and the 19th centuries were, by and large, an uncouth, unlearned and a pretentious lot devoid even of some ordinary good manners. In the presence of persons who stood in some way obliged to them they assumed the arrogantly patronizing postures of a master. Those whom they could neither subdue nor bribe into submission and those whom they could afford neither to browbeat nor treat with derision they usually ignored or abused at their backs. Max Muller did not act any differently. He expressed his missionary opinion of Swami Dayananda after the Swami had died in these words: ". . . but he indulged for a time in the use of bhang, hemp, which put him into a state of reverie from which he found it difficult to rouse himself."

In a postscript (1894) Max Muller added; "From what has come to light after Dayananda Saraswati's death, I am afraid that he was not simple-minded and straightforward in his work as a reformer as 1 imagined." All this unfolds Max Muller's Christian missionary mind at work. His information that Swami Dayananda indulged for a time in bhang, hemp was apparently based on intelligence that he regularly received from missionaries who then worked in Uttar Pradesh and Rajasthan and who were used to telling polished lies with the sole purpose of running down an adversary whom they could neither subdue nor conquer.

Max Muller was a scholar with constant rancour in his heart for which reason he always tried somehow to put himself above everyone else. He was seldom willingly ready to recognize merit and if ever he could not help doing it he did it in a manner that it smacked of sarcasm. Once while commenting on the great power of erudition of Swami Dayananda he had grudgingly remarked: "At a large convocation at Calcutta of about 300 Pandits from Gonda, Navadipa, and Kasi discussed the orthodoxy of his opinions. But though the decision was adverse, the writer of the report adds: "the mass of young Hindus are not Sanskrit scholars, and it is no wonder that they should be won over by hundreds to Dayanand's views, enforced as they are by an oratorial power of the highest order, and a determined will force that breaks down all opposition." Yet Max Muller believed, or at least he wished the world to believe him, that Dayananda indulged for a time in the use of bhang, hemp which put him into such a state of reverie out of which Dayananda found it difficult to rouse himself. And Max Muller, the profound scholar that he was made to be, also believed that despite the fact that Dayananda found it difficult to rouse himself from the reverie into which bhang, hemp used to put him, Dayananda was nevertheless able successfully not only to carry his highly learned disputations single-handed against large groups of Pandits it was possible also for him, while he used to be in that state of reverie, to win over people in hundreds\*. Who would dare to make such a wild and irresponsible statement? Only a scholar extraordinary like Max Muller or someone who himself must have been in an utter state of reverie brought about either by an excessive use of ganja or LSD. Who would believe such a trash? Only those hirelings who have either a warped mind or have an axe to grind.

While trying to malign Dayananda in this bizarre fashion Max Muller in fact attributed to Dayananda the power to perform a feat which looks far more wonderful in its effect than some of the miracles described in the Bible!

Though this might look like a digression it seems necessary here to throw some sidelight on this Christian missionary trait. The temptation to malign and indulge in character assassination appears to have always been too strong for Christian missionaries to resist and by no stretch of imagination Max Muller can be singled out to be the only one to have practised it. Another missionary, Dr. John Murdoch, indulged heavily and regularly in this missionary pursuit when he wrote about Swami Vivekananda after Vivekananda had been dead. Vivekananda's Chicago address had nearly unnerved Christian missions throughout the world. Murdoch especially was commissioned to write to counteract this effect He said: "The Swami availed himself of the accommodation of "first class hotels". Did he eschew their delicacies and remain a vegetarian? Chicago is noted for its pork: did he leave the city without once tasting it? Was he not tempted by the savoury roast beef? Did he abstain from wine? What does the Swami think of the quality of Havannah cigars?"

Murdoch wrote more than one tracts on similar lines to revile Hinduism and its leaders and reformers. In his other tracts he indulged freely in suppresio veri and suggestio falsi — the two missionary characteristics he shared with Max Muller from whom he borrowed most of his ideas. A great and indirect tribute was paid to Murdoch for his Christian literary efforts by another missionary saying: "A second organisation of more recent origin than the foregoing is the group of Christian Literature Societies, under the powerful impulse of Mutiny of 1857, Christian circles in England determined, under the leadership of Lord Shaftesburry, to take a noble "Christian revenge" on India by founding a great society for the promotion of education on Christian lines, particularly by means of elementary schools, the training of native teachers, and the dissemination of wholesome Christian literature".

Murdoch like Max Muller was a secular Christian missionary who made literature his main field of Christian evangelizing activity.
