What he wrote, compiled and edited, most of it, could only be described by an uncommitted and unattached outsider as vile and plebeian which very justifiably had attracted the attention of Mahatma Gandhi, the Father of the Indian Nation. In his answer to some questions put to him by a Christian missionary Gandhi told him thus: "The outward condition has perhaps changed, but the inward mostly remains. Vilification of Hindu religion, though subdued, is there. If there was a radical change in the missionaries' outlook would Murdoch's books be allowed to be sold in mission depots? Are these books prohibited by missionary societies? There is nothing but vilification of Hinduism in these books."

As birds of the same flock fly together so do Christian missionaries think and act like one another. One more Christian missionary, Richter, (there are many more belonging to this tribe) threw similar aspersions when he wrote about Vivekananda and of his influence upon the Hindus. He tried to caricature like Murdoch:

"He was known to have been for years under the influence of one of the most adventurous Sannyasis with whom public rumour busied itself in Calcutta, Ram Krishna Paramhansa, whom Professor Max Muller has raised to unmerited repute by the publication of his biography. His (of Vivekananda) conduct was out of all agreement with the rules of the sacred books . . . the Swami had frequented American hotels, partaken food prepared by white men, and smoked innumerable cigarettes. Further, it is forbidden in the Shastras to make proselytes of any who are not Hindus. But the conduct of the Swami in appearing before the public in this way at all was an infringement of all custom; according to orthodox teaching, a man could only become a Swami after completing the six steps (tapa) of the Yoga by long years of ascetism. Such a Swami ought to go about entirely, or at any rate half naked, in order to demonstrate his complete superiority to all conventions of men and his renunciation of the world. Above all, he should be a Brahman; for even to read the sacred Vedas within the hearing of a Sudra is grevious sin. And here was a Sudra appearing as the apostle of Hinduism;-----"

Richter, Murdoch, Smith and Farquhar were chips of the same missionary block who derived their inspiration from Max Muller. Since Murdoch had been chosen and openly abetted and helped by the then Government of India he stands out among his contemporaries as a special henchman in India of Christian missions. "Though the Christian Literature Society, the fruit of the Sepoy war of 1857-58, Dr. Murdoch, encouraged by Lord Northbrook when Governor-General, has produced and published schools and reading books in most of the languages of India.. . . No man has done more during a long lifetime for pure literature in India, Ceylon and China than Dr. Murdoch." Never a greater tribute has ever been paid to foul and mischevious religious writing than the present one, to Murdoch's Christian tracts.

Max Muller had thrown wild aspersions on Dayananda when the Swami had been dead. It is possible, nevertheless, to look into these allegations as made by Max Muller in more than one ways. First, that Max Muller had really thought Dayananda to be simple-minded but later Max Muller somehow discovered to his great dismay that he had been wrong in his assessment and that in fact Dayananda had not been simple-minded as Max Muller had imagined. We have necessarily to accept the corollary that Max Muller was a naive. He seemed to have been disappointed only because Dayananda was able to see through the wicked game which Max Muller had been playing in the same of religious reform. No leaders of men and no world teachers who were ever able to bring about revolutions in any field were ever, or could afford to be, so simple-minded as Max Muller had wished Dayananda to have been. Dayananda was a genious who understood and judged men and matters truly and correctly. Had he been simple-minded, like some other Hindu saints, it would have been impossible for him too to protect and save Indians and the Hindus in particular from the determined onslaughts which Christendom had launched against them. But it can be stated without any fear of contradiction that without being simple-minded Swami Dayananda was scrupulously honest and above board in his dealings both with his friends and foes.

When he passed his judgement Max Muller had emphasized the fact that Dayananda was not simple-minded as he had imagined. He was being transparently ostentatious in trying to assert his own importance which he owed entirely to being the hireling of the Christian rulers of India. He considered himself and his opinions to be so indispensable that he expected these to be obsequiously respected forgetting very conveniently that great world teachers as was Swami Dayananda bowed down to nothing but Truth. As such Max Muller's vainglorious bombast held no meaning for Swami Dayananda who was in no way beholden to Max Muller as had been perhaps some other Indians. Nor did Dayananda crave for Max Muller's good opinions of his scholarship or of himself.

Deserved and undeserved favours which the English people bestowed upon Max Muller had given him a feeling of great self importance. Since no one was in a position really to judge the true worth of the work that he had been turning out he considered himself to be infallible and worthy to be idolized. In consequence he became so self-conceited and self-conscious that he began to treat every other scholar with an indifference which bordered on near disdain. He talked disparagingly of Swami Dayananda, his Vedic scholarship and his conduct and character not realizing the painful reality that far greater and loftier men than he thought very much differently of this giant among Sanskrit scholars and a great rishi among men. Romain Rolland paid Swami Dayananda tribute in words which must have piqued Max Muller:

"Indian religious thought raised a purely Indian Samaj...., and at its head was a personality of the highest order, Dayananda Sarasvati (1824-1883). This man with the nature of a lion is one of those whom Europe is too apt to forget when she judges India, but whom she will probably be forced to remember to her cost; for he was that rare combination, a thinker of action with a genious for leadership, like Vivekananda after him." "It was impossible to get the better of him; for he possessed an unrivalled knowledge of Sanskrit and the Vedas, while the burning vehemence of his words brought his adversaries to naught. They likened him to a flood. Never since Shankara has such a prophet of Vedism appeared. The orthodox Brahmins, completely overwhelmed, appealed from him to Benares, their Rome. Dayananda went there fearlessly, and undertook in November 1869 a Homeric contest. Before millions of assailants, all eager to bring him to his knees, he argued for hours together along agairist three hundred pandits - the whole front line and the reserves of Hindu orthodoxy".

These were prophetic words spoken by Romain Rolland. During his lifetime the Christian government of India and its stooges purposefully ignored the Swami but it did not deter the lion-hearted Dayananda. He went ahead with his mission of "reviving the Vedic Dharma which had disappeared". At times his life had been attempted but he remained undaunted. What Dayananda could not achieve in his lifetime he achieved it after his death. The Christian missionary lost his sting and the Englishman his empire.

## 8 - Max Muller Invents Henotheism

In 1849, Robert Hibbert died bequeathing "a sum of money" for the establishment of a trust giving full freedom to the Trustees to adopt such schemes as they" 'in their uncontrolled discretion from time to time' should deem 'most conducive to the spread of Christianity in its most simple and intelligible form, and to the unfettered exercise of private judgement in matters of religion!"

While the Trustees had been legitimately seized of the problem of how best to provide effective means for the spread of Christianity they' received a memorandum signed by eighteen persons requesting the Trustees for founding of Hibbert Lectures for that purpose. The memorialists included Max Muller along with Arthur P. Stanley, J. Muir, and A.H. Sayce. They had pleaded inter alia with the Trustees that a Lecture "under the name of 'Hibbert Lecture' or any other designation" may be established so that it should be possible to exhibit "clearly from time to time some of the most important results of recent study in the great fields of philosophy, of Biblical criticism, and comparative theology." The memorialists had, in addition, availed of this opportunity to draw the attention of the Trustees to the rich literature that had already "poured in from the liberal schools of Germany and Holland," and which had "more or less trained and quickened the mind of the present generation... ."

The memorialists who had felt gravely concerned about the fresh ground that had been broken by liberal schools in Germany and Holland solemnly wished something to be done to counteract the baneful effect which this rich but adverse criticism of the Bible had been having on the minds of younger laity and intelligentsia in England. As there seemed to be none other than Max Mullet more qualified who could possibly put things right in this field the first invitation naturally went out to him. to start these Lectures. This gave Max Muller the golden opportunity for which he had been patiently waiting and preparing himself for a number of years. It was only very apt indeed that he was asked "to take as his subject the religions of India."

As no better opportunity could have offered itself to Max Muller he used these Lectures to tell his audiences, most of whom were only as bigoted as he himself and Col. Boden and Hibbert, in his usual diabolical style, that the religion of India (of the Hindus) as preached by the Rig. was a polytheistic faith. And the manner in which he chose to do it was a clever piece of a perfect Jesuitical exercise. Max Muller was now ready to sting and the manner in which he decided to achieve his goal was ingenious.

The great Vedic scholar from Germany told his listeners that the Hindus were in essence followers of only a polytheistic religious faith which, he authoritatively confirmed, was in accordance with the teachings of the Rigveda. Since it suited their preconceived notions and prejudices his listeners found it not at all difficult to believe him. By a subtle display of Maxmullerism at its best he was able successfully to convince his listeners that he had handed over to them a talisman which would help them to convert the Hindus of India to Christ. The ease and flourish with which Max Muller was able to invent for Hinduism (the religion of the Rig) a new name and a definition convinced his audiences that he was much more than a scholar; he was a great theologist and a messiah.

When he delivered his first Lecture Max Muller started by defining religion. In his endeavour to find an acceptable definition of religion he first found fault, one after another, with the definition of religion as given in the past by Kant, Fichte, Schleiermache, Hegel,

Comte and Feuerbech. Then in the end he came to his own definition according to which religion was something which resembled a "mental faculty" and which enabled a man to hear "a groaning of the spirit, a struggle to conceive the inconceivable, to utter the unutterable, a longing after the Infinite, a love of God." But the definition which Max Muller gave, as we shall soon find out, is neither conclusive nor universally acceptable in this age when man seems to be fast outgrowing the conventional boundaries of religion.

Max Muller believed that "a longing after the Infinite, a love of God" was the criterion of religion. He clearly accepted the existence of the Infinite which meant that instead of defining religion he was, in fact and in substance, defining only Theism. Much water has flown under the bridge of religion since the day when Max Muller gave the world his definition of religion. He did not then realize and it seems astonishing that he should have so easily tripped over the definition of religion — a subject which had been so near to his heart and to which he had devoted his lifelong attention.

It appears to have been beyond Max Muller's imagination and comprehension that Atheism too could be, and was very much, a religion though for an atheist the Infinite just did not exist nor did he have any longing for Him. An atheist need have no love of God because God for him was a non-entity and had no existence. And quite obviously one could not have love of something which was not. All this notwithstanding there could easily be, somewhere, a theist who in place of love of God might have in his heart strong disliking and an aversion for Him. All this will easily make the definition of religion as given by Max Muller completely untenable rendering the need necessary for a more readily and widely acceptable definition.

What is religion? As Max Muller admitted it was not easy to give a flawless definition of religion. Today, there are some religions (e.g. Communism, and Marxism) which are atheistic in their concept. Despite the fact that Marxism is vehementally opposed to the idea and concept of His Being it has not been accepted by the world as yet another (new) religion and this is in spite of Communism and Marxism having neither any love of God nor any use for Him. The admission of Communism and Marxism to the Religions Club makes it necessary to have a new definition of religion so that it might more readily and easily be acceptable to theists, atheists and secularists alike. Though the search for a flawless definition must continue in the future for long time to come we shall attempt here to offer a new definition of religion without, however, in any way claiming it to be the last word in this difficult field.

To make matters easy it must be emphatically said in the very beginning that truly speaking there are in this world only two Religions: one is Theism and the other is Atheism. As both have to do either with being or not being of the Infinite it will have to be conceded that Religion (still remaining to be defined) is a thing which has something to do in one or the other way with Him (the Infinite). In the normal course it would perhaps be natural both for Theism and Atheism to have further divisions and bifurcations which would ordinarily not at all be difficult to distinguish from one another. Hinduism, Judaism, Christianism, and Mohemmadanism are in fact only different labels and names to distinguish one brand and type of theism (theistic religion) from another. Likewise, Marxism, Maoism, Secularism and Buddhism are the smaller compartments ir-to which Atheisms, the other parent Religion, has got itself divided and sub-divided over a number of years. In the final analysis, however, all these smaller isms (prognies of Theism and Atheism — legitimate or illegitimate) and beliefs and many more yet to be spawned in future shall surely belong to either of the two parent Religions of the world viz. Theism or Atheism.

Though there may be, and there always have been and are, millions of people claiming to be followers of Theism in essence they just happen to be adherents of particular theistic creeds having dogmas acceptable only to a small section of the entire population of Theists of the world. In the common language of the millions they are followers of a theistic religion having its own creeds, beliefs, rituals, dogmas, martyrs, saints and perhaps their own miracles which would easily be cognizable by those who owe their allegiance to that particular theistic belief (religion).

What is then theistic religion apart from Theism? Many attempts have been made in the past and there is no doubt that some more will be made in future to define religion but in the present age which belongs to Secularism (or Rationalism) none of these definitions will be found to be either satisfying or acceptable. In the mundane sense a religion is nothing but a theistic or an atheistic belief to which a group of people (there is no bar to a solitary individual professing a separate religion — theistic or atheistic) collectively subscribe. Such adherence to a chosen set of beliefs gives birth to an ism though a great part of their beliefs may have behind them no rationale or evidence whatever to support them. They mysteriously believe something, anything, just on faith without assigning any reasons and without ever expecting or giving an explanation for the beliefs they hold. Thus a particular set of people having a common belief founded just on faith in some dogmas slowly develop, at times unconsciously or subconsciously, for themselves a theistic or an atheistic creed either to serve their common interests (social, economic, political or ethnic) or to satisfy their abstract longings and aspirations. With the passage of time a small number of persons from among this group who happen to be more intelligent and clever than the rest gradually manage to appear and emerge as the leaders (priests) of this ism who having to safeguard their secular interests begin, and in course of time, strive hard to keep this ism alive as a separate religion — theistic or atheistic. The process keeps on repeating itself till the two parent Religions of the world (Theism and Atheism) gradually and unnoticeably branch themselves out into hundreds of different beliefs and faiths varying sometimes from the parents only in slight and imperceptible details.

Any people, whether Theists or Atheists, will and may follow particular religion — a theistic or an atheistic ism — if they have similar beliefs founded on common (similar) faith. Marxism has now come to be recognized as yet another religion (atheistic ism) because all Marxists held, at least till some time ago, a set of beliefs as common denominator. As time passed Marxism like Theism got itself sub-divided into more than one atheistic sects and creeds for the reason that some splinter groups began to hold beliefs which differed though only slightly from those of the parent Marxism. While one group of Marxists believed in making use of violence the other set believed that the world could be converted to their ism by less violent or some other means.

Nevertheless, both continued to have no love of God. For this reason both types continue to believe, exactly like the Christian missionaries belonging to multifarious Christian denominations, that the human race would be much more happy if the entire world was converted to their atheistic creed which on faith alone they claimed alone to be true and superior to all others.

The Christians have always found it near to impossible to define a Christian. After great deliberations and protracted discussions and consultations among different sects of Christians it was agreed only in 1948 "on the occasion of the formation of World Council of Churches" that members would be admitted to the Council on the "recognition of Jesus Christ as God and Saviour." None the less there are some others who though calling themselves Christians recognize Jesus neither as God nor as divine (Saviour). Yet they continue to be as much followers of Christianity (a theistic religion) as those who accept just on faith Jesus both as God and as divine. Likewise the Communists of the world find it difficult to define a Communist and as no suitable definition could be given Communists of all types and hues agreed, willy-nilly, to co-exist though not very comfortably with one another provided they all shared among themselves the common belief held on faith alone that there was and is not God. The fact that the same is equally true of Hinduism, Mohemmadanism and of all other theistic religions proves conclusively that a slightly different set of beliefs founded just on slightly different faith and held by a group of people will consign its members to a different (religious) ism which the group will call by a new name — a different and distinguishing name of a new religion as it might suit their fancy and convenience.

When a people came to believe something, theistic or atheistic, just on faith alone they form themselves, consciously, subconsciously or unconsciously, into a group adhering to a separate religion. As there are in reality only two (parent) Religions — Theism and Atheism — we can easily discover that both these Religions yield equally well to our definition of religion though it still will need some further explanation.

A Theist believes there is a Supreme Being which statement is denied by the Atheist with equal force and vehemence though neither of them could perhaps offer any conclusive proof to support his respective contentions. At best both will have equally plausible or perhaps equally flimsy arguments to support themselves and their pet theories and dogmas. Nevertheless each continues to believe, firmly and perhaps sincerely, that there is or there is not a Supreme Being. The extent to which their arguments might convince an uncommitted outsider with an open mind would depend, nearly always, more upon and in direct proportion to their power of rhetorics and the disparity between the knowledge and learning in this field of the outsider and each contender than on anything else. Their success in propounding and propagating their own Religion will depend more often upon such prejudices and preconceived ideas in which the minds of their listeners and followers might have been steeped and saturated during their more impressionable years. The painful truth would, nevertheless, remain that while one can prove the existence of the Supreme Being no more conclusively and convincingly than the other can prove and establish His non-existence both continue to believe just on faith as merrily as ever in these two of the greatest superstitions. Both will continue to assert with all the force at their command, and often with great vehemence, their own particular Religion though all the while each has for its foundation nothing more tangible and solid than a belief founded just on faith alone — some sort of belief on some sort of faith — without any evidence whatever to sustain and support their respective religious contentions.

Before Christianity came on the scene Judaism was the religion followed by the people of Israel. They had their own dogmas and their own rituals, their festivals and their martyrs and their heresies and their miracles. In addition they had their own beliefs (e.g. that they were the chosen people of God) which were theistic. Yet Judaism was not Theism — one of the parent Religions of the world. Judaism was an offshoot of Theism with certain variations and limitations. It was thus only a theistic religion without being Theism. Christianity brought with it the cultus of three Gods (not gods!) — God, the Father, God, the Son Jesus Christ and God, the Holy Ghost. In addition it had is own sacraments and creed e.g. "he that believeth (in Christ — Br.D.Bh.) and is baptized shall be saved; and he that believeth not shall be damned." This made Christianity just anther religion and like Judaism it too was a theistic religion (belief) without being Theism — Monotheism.

The position of Hinduism as popularly practised these days, or as it was practised in the days of Rammohun Roy, is not very much different from Judaism and Christianity. The present-day Hindus, or at least the less informed among them, recognize like the Jews and the Christians more than one creeds. Likewise, popular Hinduism has its rituals, dogmas and tenets which make it (popular Hinduism) as distinct from the religious beliefs of the Veda) — like Judaism and Christianism, another theistic religion without raising it to the high level of Theism — Monotheism — one of the two parent Religions of the world.

The Hinduism of today, as generally practised and understood by the Hindu laity, is very much different from Theism which the Rigyeda proclaims. Nowhere in the Rig. theism is mentioned by a name. Nor is there a mention anywhere in the Rig. of the two words — Hinduism and the Hindu. The theistic belief of the Rigveda is, therefore, known, for this very reason, as Vedic-Dharma which truly is universal and timeless in its concept and application and is applicable equally to all without any distinctions whatever. The only thing that matter is a belief in His One Being; all the rest was secondary. Thus the Vedic-Dharma was Theism — one of the two (parent) Religions of the world which later bifurcated into present-day theistic creeds known as Hinduism, Jainism, Buddhism and Sikhism as its more important offshoots.

We shall now make an attempt to define religion. In short it is a superstition which may be either theistic or atheistic. It is a belief in something founded just on faith. As the number of believers in something just on faith increases they tend to become, and desire to be known, as the followers of an ism and if that ism happens to have something to do with Him (His Being or not Being) it is given the nomenclature of a religion e.g. Hinduism, Judaism, Jainism, Christianism, Mohemmadanism, Sikhism, Monism and Deism. However, if an ism has nothing to do with Him (the Infinite) but concerns itself instead only with some economic, political, social or industrial programme it cannot, and may not, then be called a religion. At best it would only be a political, social, economic programme or a way of thinking to which a number of people collectively happen to subscribe. As outstanding examples of such isms (without being religions — any offshoot of cither of the two Religions of the world i.e. Theism and Atheism) may be mentioned Secularism, Socialism, Capitalism, Imperialism, Unionism, Liberalism, Conservatism, Toryism, and perhaps Hedonism.

What is Dharma! As in the case of religion several attempts have been made in the remote and recent past to define Dhanna and this matter has constantly caught the attention and imagination of those who were intimately interested in the subject. When Moncir-Williams was in India he happened to meet Swami Dayananda in Bombay. As Moneir-Williams himself did not, and perhaps was not capable fully to, comprehend the difference between religion and Dharina he put this all-important question to Swami Dayananda. In his own words: "Dayananda Saraswati, .... I made his acquaintance at Bombay in 1876, and was much struck by his fine countenance and figure. There I heard him preach an eloquent discourse on the religious development of the Aryan race. He began by repeating a hymn to Varuna (IV. 16) preceded by the'syllabic Om, prolatii.g the vowel in deep sonorous tones. In one of my interviews will, him, 1 asked him for his definition of religion, he replied in Sanskiit:-'Religion MO is a true and just view (^ora) and the abandonment of all prejudice and partiality (qflMid-itt^H) - tl.at is to say, it is an impartial inquiry into the truth by means of knowledge - (wrm) reason and revelation."

The definition of Dharma as given by Swami Dayananda was masterly. If and whenever a man, any man, inquired impartially into the actuality of anything he was bound to reach at the truth. And nothing but God is truth. On the other hand as religion has always some room for prejudice, superstition and partisanship it can never reach at the ultimate Truth. Dhanna as Swami Dayananda defined it has no room for any prejudice and does not have to lean on any creed formulated by anyone whosoever for its existence.

Max Muller was somehow to prqve to the world or at least to the satisfaction of Christian missions that the Dhanna proclaimed by the Rigveda was polytheism and not monotheism. Neither Max Muller nor the Christian missions were concerned and bothered about popular Hinduism because both knew it too well that polytheism as practised in popular Hinduism had no sanction behind it of the Rig. Nor were they bothered and concerned about present-day Hinduism. They agreed among themselves that if Max Muller's researches and his findings were to carry any conviction with the world of scholarship it was then incumbent upon him to establish the fact that the religion of the Hindus as proclaimed by the Rigveda was polytheistic. The world of scholarship knew it too well, the Christian missionaries more than, anyone else, that if such a finding by Max Muller did not have behind it (he sanction of the Rigveda the result would only be highly suspect.

A hard task had been set for Max Muller but he was not a man who would easily give up. For a number of years his mind had been working consistently on the problem for a solution and ultimately when a suitable opportunity came his way his ingenuity invented a Jesuitical definition for the Vedic-Dharma. He called it Henotheism.

Max Muller lived greater part of his life in Oxford with two consciences. One impelled him to be loyal to those who had given him a start in life. He was constantly weighed down by this conscience under a sense of gratitude compelling him to do things which, at times, he knew he would not have stooped down to do if he had been a free man. And whenever he did or said a thing which was neither fair nor true his other conscious, the conscious of a scholar and an intellectual, rebelled and looked down upon him with great disapproval. He was constantly torn between these two consciences constantly fighting each other, and like an ordinary man of the world he would struggle to maintain a balance betw°.n the demands made on him. This is exactly what he did when he denned Henotheism.

What is Henotheism? We shall go to Max Muller to learn from the horse's mouth what he meant by Henotheism. In History of Ancient Sanskrit Literature Max Muller had drawn "attention to the henotheistic phase of religion" of the Veda saying: "When these individual gods are invoked they are conceived as limited by the power of others, as superior or inferior in rank. Each god is to the mind of the suppliant as good as all the gods."

While delivering his sixth Hibbert Lecture Max Muller again spoke of Henotheism: "If we must have a general name for the earliest form of religion among the Vedic Indians, it can be neither, monotheism nor polytheism, but only henotheism, that is, a belief and worship of those single objects, whether semi-tangible or intangible, in which man first suspected the presence of the invisible and the infinite... .'

Max Muller had no way out and had to admit that the Vedic religion could not be termed polytheism but since he was not prepared to call it monotheism he. forged a new name for it and called it Henotheism. He made, thus, a concession both to the followers of the Veda who objected to the religion of the Veda being classified as polytheism and to those Christian missions and missionaries who did not wish to accord to the Vedic Dharma the status of monotheism. Therefore, he tried in his typical Maxmullerian manner to kill two birds with one stone and in his endeavour to succeed invented for the Vedic Dharma a new nomenclature which meant neither polytheism nor monotheism.

The German scholar of the Vedas was adept in exploiting religious sentiments and susceptibilities of those who in their ignorance looked forward hopefully to him for guidance in matters of religion. Since most of the time he hawked spurious wares he could ill offered to be honest to give them what they expected from him. He played and quibbled with his words so well that despite the fact that he seldom gave to his listeners more than what he chose to dole out they felt elated and gratified with whatever little they received. While explaining to them how he viewed the religion of the Rigveda he told them in one breath that even though he could not even answer whether the religion of the Vedas was polytheistic or monotheistic he was sure that in the usual sense of the word it was not monotheistic adding a rider that at first sight polytheism would be the only right name for the religion of the Veda. To quote him: "I could not even answer the question if you were to ask it, whether the religion of the Veda was polytheistic or monotheistic, in the usual sense of the word, it is definitely not, though there are hymns that assert the unity of the Divine as fearlessly as any passage of the Old Testament, or the New Testament, or the Koran. Thus one poet says (Rigveda, 1:164:46) 'That which is one, sages name it in various ways — they call it Agni, Yama, Matrisvan.' Another poet says: The wise poets represent by their words Him who is one with beautiful wings, in many ways.'"

In the course of the same Lecture he gave his reasons for choosing the new name for the religion of the Rigveda He said:

"In the Veda, however, the gods worshipped as supreme by each sept stand side by side. No one is first always, no one is last always. Even gods of a decidedly inferior and limited character assume occasionally in the eyes of a devoted poet a supreme place above all other gods. It was, necessary, therefore, for the purpose of accurate reasoning to have a name, different from polytheism, to signify this worship of single gods, each occupying for a time a supreme position, and I propose for it the name of Kathenotheism, that is a worship of one god after another, or of Henotheism, the worship of single gods. The shorter name of Henotheism has found more general acceptance, as conveying more definitely the opposition between Monotheism, the worship of one only God, and Henotheism, the worship of single gods; and, if but properly defined, it will answer its purpose very well. However, in researches of this kind we cannot be too much on our guard against technical terms. They are inevitable, I know; but they are almost always misleading."

Max Muller knew it too well in his heart that he was palming off a clever forgery when he told his listeners, most of whom were young and without much experience and were at the same time aspiring to go to India as civil servants, that the religion of the Rigveda was not monotheism. He also knew it very well that it was neither polytheism. Therefore he called it Henotheism.

It was first in 1859 that Max Muller had given a new name (Henotheism) to the Vedic Dharma. Whatever he then said was essentially either for the consumption of gullible public or he himself did not really know what he had been talking. His first biography has a letter (significantly missing in the second biography of Max Muller by Nirad Chaudhuri) which the inventor of Henotheism had written to the Duke of Argyll. In this letter the ingenious Inventor had clearly, «jluntarily and unequivocally admitted that the religion of the Veda ("of the Aryan race") was nothing but monotheism. Fearing to tell a lie to his patron he had to admit the truth. Max Muller had confessed:

"The earliest known religious form of the Aryan race is, as nearly as possible, a pure monotheism — yes, that is perfectly true. But it was an undoubting monotheism, in one sense perhaps the happiest monotheism — yet not safe against doubts and negation. Doubt and negation followed, it may be by necessity, and the unconscious defenceless monotheism gave way to polytheism."

His letter (February 4, 1875) to the duke of Argyll is a masterpiece in casuistry. He explained to the Duke that the religion (of the Veda) of the Aryan race was pure monotheism which by necessity had given way to polytheism. Like so many other people the Duke seemed to believe him. Or perhaps he did not care to correct or refute Max Muller because it suited Duke's own convenience. The ease with which Max Muller continued to indulge in Maxmullerian sophistry and the readiness with which the English people, the statesmen and scholars not excepted, allowed themselves to be duped with his Maxmullerism was amazing indeed. But Max Muller merrily continued to go on with his predetermined rampage because he knew in full depth the weakness of the 19th-century Englishman. Like a professional clairvoyant Max Muller knew what the colonizing Englishman wanted and to keep him happy and satisfied the diplomatist-from-Germany-turned philologist freely fed him on the stuff the Englishman so much relished.

## 9 - Max Muller's Epistles to the Brahmos

It was in 1847 that Max Muller had been given the assignment to translate the Rig. In less than 20 years (1866) we find him bragging to his wife that his edition of the Rig. will uproot Hinduism lock, stock and barrel. He boasted in his letter (1866) to the Duke of Argyll that the ancient religion of India was doomed. He had vaunted in similar vein when he wrote to Bunsen. He never failed to crow over his achievement as a translator of the Rig. superciliously claiming to have invented for the religion of the Rigveda a new name and a definition. Macaulay too when he wrote to his father in 1836 had claimed that if their plans of education were followed up there would be no idolater left among the high-caste Hindus of Bengal by 1866 ("thirty years hence").

When Max Muller wrote to the Eighth Duke of Argyll in 1866 he told him nearly the same thing but as time passed and as Max Muller grew old he saw no sign of the Hindus of India or the high-caste Hindus of Bengal willing to renounce their ancestral religion. Since the Hindus did not seem to be interested in Christ or the Christian cultus of three Gods (the Trinity) it gave Max Muller a feeling of great despair. He started looking around for likely victims and for some opportunity whereby he could prove, at least symbolically, that his translation had the effect on someone which he had claimed it would have on entire Hindudom. Since Max Muller guessed that the Brahmos would be more easily susceptible to his evangelizing endeavours and influences he devoted his special attention to those among them who in his judgement he thought to be more important.

In spite of having been too unwell he took the trouble of writing to Protap Chunder Mozoomdar of the Brahmo Somaj more than one letters which were more like St. Paul's epistles to the Romans, to the Corinthians, to the Galatians and to the Ephesians. In these epistles Max Muller entreated Mozoomdar with all the power and arguments that he could possibly muster and command to persuade him to accept Christ as his personal saviour. He tried to cajole, bluff and bluster Mozoomdar to gain him as a convert and made use of more than one artifices to hook him. He threw bait to entice him but to his great discomfiture and disappointment Mozoomdar spurned all his advances with calculated disdain.

Max Muller begged, implored and beseeched Mozoomdar to gain him as a proselyte — an important proselyte from among the members of the Brahmo Somaj. He wanted to show to the world that while all other Christian missionaries who went before him (Nobili, Abbe Dubois, Carey and Middleton) had failed in their attempts to convert the Hindus to Christianity and make a dent in Hinduism he had achieved the remarkable distinction of having successfully persuaded Mozoomdar to accept Christ. In his letter he admitted that he had been watching for many years the efforts the Brahmos had been making to purify the rotten religion of Hinduism and to bring it near to Christianity. He asked Mozoomdar, quite presumtuously, like that lad who did not know his place, how much of his ancient religion he was prepared to give up as if Mozoomdar had been under some obligation to him. In his next breath Max Muller had told Mozoomdar more confidently that if he accepted the teachings of Christ he was for all intents and purposes a Christian which perhaps was a wickedly clever auto-suggestion knowingly made to leave a psychological impact on Mozoomdar's mind.

In this endeavour and anxiety really to convince he extolled, as was the habit with him, the Bible, its teachings and its divine character. In the last resort Max Muller threw temptation to Mozoomdar and asked him to let him know his (Mozoomdar's) difficulties assuring him that when, and if, he crossed over to Christianity he (Max Muller) along with others will be waiting to receive him with open arms.

Max Muller was a master equivocator. Like most hypocrites he excelled in the art of simulation. He knew how and when to mount psychological attack on his adversary and how to buttress untruth. He told Mozoomdar that Christ came to him (Mozoomdar) as Christ comes to Christians in the only trustworthy records preserved of Him in the Gospels. But while affirming the trustworthiness of the Gospels he conveniently ignored the opinions which some other biblical scholars, more enlightened and learned than he, have held about the trustworthiness or otherwise of the Christian scriptures.

Whatever else might be said of him Max Muller could not possibly feign ignorance about the authenticity or otherwise of the Gospels. It is certain that when Max Muller recommended the Bible as an historically authentic document to Mozoomdar he was very much in possession of the truth regarding its specious trustworthiness. There had been such wide and formidably adverse criticism of the New Testament, especially of the four Gospels of which Max Muller had talked in adulation, that no student of the subject, much less a scholar, would have been without this intelligence. But Max Muller pretended to be blissfully uninformed in the matter.

In London itself, very close to Oxford, had appeared two outstanding criticisms from the pen of James Anthony Froude — one in 1853 in Westminister Review and the other in 1864 in Fraser's Magazine. In yet another incisive criticism Froude had told his readers: "Submissiveness, humility, obedience, produce if uncorrected, in politics a nation of slaves, whose baseness becomes an incentive to tyranny; in religion, they produce the consecration of falsehood, poperies, immaculate conceptions, winking images and the confessional." But ignoring all these criticisms Max Muller told Mozoomdar that no one had the right ever to consider "how differently" some people interpret the Christian Gospels.

When Max Muller wrote to Mozoomdar he had made another significant statement: "Christ comes to everyone in the only trustworthy records preserved of Him in the four Gospels." How trustworthy were the four Gospels is a matter only of conjecture and depends more upon the gullibility of each person concerned and how much and to what extent he or she has allowed himself or herself to remain uninformed.

In 1776 when the first volume of The Decline And Fall of The Roman Empire made its appearance perhaps the first fatal blow was dealt by it to the so called trustworthiness of the story of the four Gospels. But Max Muller as a true Christian missionary seemed to have completely insulated himself against all outside influences if they happened to interfere with the cause he had been espousing and for the furtherance of which he had been getting paid.

In 1794 had appeared another remarkable book, Age Of Reason, which blasted Christian religion and the Bible so heavily that it created unprecedented commotion throughout Christendom. Its author, Thomas Paine, said among other things:

"the presumption is that the books called the Evangelists, and ascribed to Matthew, Mark, Luke and John, were not written by Matthew, Mark, Luke and John, and that they are impositions ... that they have been manufactured, as the books of the Old Testaments have been, by other persons than those whose names they bear."

Thomas Paine pointedly questioned the trustworthiness of the Gospels to which Max Muller had so piously drawn Mozoomdar's attention. Paine had asked:

"The history of Jesus Christ is contained in the four books ascribed to Matthew, Mark, Luke and John. The first chapter of Matthews begins with giving a geneology of Jesus Christ; and in the third chapter of Luke, there is also given a geneology of Jesus Christ. Did these two agree, it would not prove the geneology to be true, because it might, nevertheless be a fabrication; but as they contradict each other in every particular, it proves falsehood absolutely. If Matthew speaks truth, Luke speaks falsehood, and if Luke speaks truth, Matthew speaks falsehood... ,"

Before Thomas Paine came to these conclusions he had reflected upon some other more important aspects of the life of Jesus. In his opinion:

"Jesus Christ wrote no account of himself, of his birth, parentage, or anything else; not a line of what is called the New Testament is of his own writing. The history of him is altogether the work of other people; and as to the account given of his resurrection and ascension, it was the necessary counterpart to the story of his birth. His historians, having brought him into the world in a supernatural manner, were obliged to take him out again in the same manner, or the first part of the story must have fallen to the ground."

Thomas Paine's opinions of the Bible (the New Testament) were diametrically opposed to those held by Max Muller. What Paine said about two hundred years ago has yet to be disproved to the satisfaction of the unattached, unsubsidized and uncommitted scholarship. He had said:

"Did the book called the Bible excel in purity of ideas and expressions all the books that are now extant in the world, I would not take it for my rule of faith, as being the word of God, because the possibility would nevertheless exist of my being imposed upon. But when I see throughout the greater part of the book scarcely anything but a history of the grossest vices and a collection of the most paltry and contemptible tales, I cannot dishonour my creator by calling it by his name." Again: whenever we read the obscene stories, the voluptuous debaucheries, the cruel and tortuous executions, the unrelenting vindictiveness, with which more than half the Bible is filled, it would be more consistent that we called it the word of a demon than the word of God."

Was the Bible really authentic and trustworthy as Max Muller had claimed: Paine had given his verdict on this point too in his typical manner:

"It has often been said, that anything may be proved from the Bible, but before anything can be admitted as proved by the Bible, the Bible itself must be proved to be true; for if the Bible be not true, or the truth of it be doubtful, it ceases to have authority, and cannot be admitted as proof of anything."

Max Muller used to boast that the Bible taught loftier ethics and purer morals. Paine seems to have considered this point also. He had asked: "What is it that we have learned from this pretended thing called revealed religion?" He went on to answer his own question: "Nothing that is useful to man, and everything that is dishonourable to his maker. What is it the Bible teaches us? — rapine, cruelty, and murder. What is it the Testament teaches us? — to believe that the almighty committed debauchery with a woman engaged to be married, and the belief of the debaucherv is called faith."

It would not be difficult to quote other authorities, more readily acceptable than Max Muller, to show beyond doubt that what Max Muller had told Mozoomdar was not the whole truth; it was professional gerrymandering freely indulged in by a scholarly person covertly engaged in the hideous task of somehow subverting someone's religious beliefs. Astonishingly Thomas Paine seems to have possessed the capacity to look into the future. Paine had said:

"It is impossible to calculate the moral mischief, if I may so express it, that mental lying has produced in society. When a man has so far corrupted and prostituted the chastity of his mind, as to subscribe his professional belief to things he does not believe, he has prepared himself for the commission of every other crime. He takes up the trade of a priest for the sake of gain, and in order to quality himself for that trade, he begins with a perjury. Can we conceive any thing more destructive to morality than this?"

These were prophetic words spoken by Paine in 1794 and it looks as if Paine had anticipated what Max Muller was to write to Mozoomdar in 1899. But how little Paine would have then realized that he had caricatured Max Muller who after about a hundred years would be subscribing in a letter to a Hindu his professional beliefs which he himself did not believe to be true.

Max Muller wrote several letters to Protap Chunder Mozoomdar of the Brahmo Somaj each time with the cannibalistic intention of persuading him and, through him, his followers to become Christians. As he was nearing the end of his life he made bold to come out of the shell of philosophy to show himself up in his true colours of a Christian missionary. He wrote to Mozoomdar on "3 August, 1881":

"My Dear Mr. Protap Chunder Mozoomdar, ... I must tell them all what I told you when we parted at Oxford, 'if you really want me, I shall always be ready.'.... I have watched your struggles in India for many years, and I have often pleaded your cause in England with friends who were frightened by what they heard about Keshub Chunder Sen. Yet I trusted in you and in the goodness of your cause, and remained silent, at least in public.... That Cutch Bihar marriage was a misfortune, but what has it to do with the great work that Keshub Chunder Sen has been carrying on? Suppose even he was to be blamed, can no one be allowed to carry on a great religious reform, unless he is himself entirely blameless?....

"... his most generous and liberal minded friends, are in despair about some of the outward religious ceremonies which he has sanctioned. His asceticism, his shaving his hair, his carrying a flag and singing in the streets, his pilgrimages — all are considered quite shocking! To tell you the truth, I am not fond of such things; ...ll

Max Muller had wanted to visit India and, if possible, to reside there for some years so that he could have masqueraded there as Nobili had done before him. He had expressed this desire to Bunsen in a letter he had written to him in 1856 but he did not pursue his plans for the only reason that by then (1856) he had come to know of Swami Dayanand. Max Muller mortally feared that if he ventured to go to Hindustan and dared to open his mouth Dayananda was sure to tear to smithereens Max Muller's mask of Sanskrit scholarship.

Max Muller had repeatedly tried to bring about a change of religion of the members of the Brahmo Somaj at the helm of which then stood Protap Chunder Mozoomdar. In June 1899 — hardly about sixteen months before his death — the again addressed a long epistle to Mozoomdar. The letter is too interestingly revealing for any part of it to be left out. He had said:

"My Dear Friend, — You know how many years I have watched your efforts to purify the popular religion of India, and thereby to bring it nearer to the purity and perfection of other religions, particularly Christianity. You know also that I have paid close attention to the endeavours of those who came before you, of men like Rammohun Roy, Debendranath Tagore, Keshub Chunder Sen, and others, in whose footsteps you have boldly followed, and whose work you have faithfully carried on, as far as circumstances allowed you to do so. What I have much admired, both in yourself and your noble predecessors and fellow workers, is the patience and the even temper with which you have prosecuted your religious and social reforms. I know that you have met with many disappointments and many delays, but you have never lost heart and never lost patience. I confess that I have several times felt very unhappy about the mischances that have befallen your good cause; but even when Keshub Chunder Sen was forsaken by a number of his friends and followers, on utterly insufficient grounds, as far as I could judge, and again, when he was taken from us in the very midst of his glorious work, I never lost faith in the final success of his work, though I began to doubt whether I should live to see the full realization of his hopes.

"If you once know what truth is, you also know that truth is in no hurry. Truth is, truth has been, truth will be, whether it is accepted by the whole world or by a small minority only-----Your departed friend, Keshub Chunder Sen, had the firm conviction that the way in which he and his predecessors had indicated was the only possible way out of the present state of confusion, and out of the misunderstandings that had arisen between him and many of his own countrymen, and likewise out of those which still separated him from his Christian friends and sympathisers.

"Now it seems to me that the first things you have to do is to try to remove the differences that still exist among yourselves, and to settle how much of your ancient religion you are willing to give up, if not as utterly false, still as antiquated. You have given up a great deal, polytheism, idolatry, and your elaborate sacrificial worship. You have surrendered also, as far as I can judge, the claim of divine revelation which had been so carefully formulated by your ancient theologians in support of the truth of the Vedas. These were great sacrifices, for whatever may be thought of your ancient traditions, to give up what we have been taught by our fathers and mothers, requires a very strong conviction, and a very strong will. Brit though this surrender has brought you much nearer to us, there still remain many minor points on which you differ among yourselves in your various samajes or congregations. Allow me to say that these differences seem to me to have little to do with real religion, still they must be removed, because they prevent united action on your part. ... If you are once united among yourselves, you need no longer trouble about this or that missionary, whether he come from London, Rome, Geneva, or Moscow. They all profess to bring you the Gospel of Christ. Take then the New Testament and read it for yourselves, and judge for yourselves whether the words of Christ, as contained in it, satisfy you or not.

"I know that you yourself, as well as Rammohun Roy and Keshub Chunder Sen, have done that. I know one countryman of yours who wrote a searching criticism on the Old and New Testaments, and then joined the Christian Church, as established in England, because there was something in the teaching and life of Christ, which he could not withstand. I know this is not an argument, yet it is something to reflect on.

"Christ comes to you as He comes to us in the only trustworthy records preserved of Him in the Gospels. We have not even the right to dictate our interpretation of these Gospels to you, particularly if we consider how differently we interpret them ourselves. If you accept His teachings, as there recorded, you are a Christian. There is no necessacity whatever of your being formally received into the membership of one or the other sect of the Christian Church, whether reformed or unreformed. That will only delay the growth of Christianity in India. All that has grown up in the Church after the death of Christ, or the Apostles, does not concern you. You will want, no doubt, some kind of constitution, some government, some Church or Somaj. Have a baptism, or Upnayana, if you please, as an outward sign of that new life which baptism signified among the early Christians, and which was well known also to your great teaches of old. Remember, before all things, that you can be followers of Christ, without being Roman Catholics, Anglo-Catholics, or Greek orthodox Catholics, without assuming the names and fashions of Presbytarians, Congregationalists, Unitarians or any other Dissenters. Keep aloof of all of them, they have proved only stumbling blocks in the progress of Christianity. Keshub Chunder Sen used to say that, after all, Christ was in many respects an Oriental and was better understood by Orientals than by Occidentals. Whether this be true or not, you have, at all events, as much right to constitute and regulate your own Church, your own Parishads, your own Samgha, as the Greeks, in their time, had at Alexandria, or the Romans at Rome. You have nothing to do with popes, bishops, priests, ministers, et hoc genus omne, unless for some reasons or other you wish, besides being Christians, to belong to one'of the historical associations also that have sprung up. ... I do not like to appear sailing under false colours. I am, myself, a devoted member of the English Church, because I think its members enjoy greater freedom and more immunity from priestcraft than those of any other Church. There are, no doubt, many things in that Church also, which still require reformation. But though we are not altogether free from the evils that seem inseparable from the establishment of any priesthood, we have thrown off many of the hideous accretions which nearly took the life out of Christianity during the long night of the dark ages. The real Church, you should remember, before you take any steps towards forming a constitution of your own, consists of the laity alone. It was the laity that appointed its ministers, but these original ministers — such is human nature — have almost invariably become the masters of their masters. The English Church, however, though it has sometimes forgotten the supreme and indefeasible rights of the laity, has never surrendered them formally and altogether; and the highest seat of authority, in matters of faith as well as of public worship, has always remained with the laity and the civil powers, and has never been surrendered formally to the clergy. . . . Try whether you cannot join the Church of England as lay members, but have nothing to do with their ecclesiastical constitutions, and keep aloof of all discussions on so called Orders or their validity. Lay members of the English Church are perfectly free, and I have never repented having joined it

"Only remember, that there is no reason whatever, why you, hi forming your own Christian Church, should join any of the European Churches. That idea is what has delayed your progress so long. You have declared in so many words (New Dispensation, March 5, 1899): "We regard the words of Jesus Christ as our authority, and consider him (not Him — Br. D. Bh.) to be our Master." How can any one dare to call men who say this pagans, to be converted like so many negroes or Hottentots? What keeps these men away from us? They tell us themselves in the same paper: "We cannot accept the teachings of popular Christianity, that is, of the missionaries in India.

Their teaching seems to us too anthropomorphic. We are asked to believe in a Deity who does one thing today and repents of it tomorrow. Today He blesses the children of men; tomorrow He sees their sins, and becomes vindictive, curses them, and seeks their destruction." These may be the teachings of certain missionaries in India, but students of the Bible might easily convince themselves that they are really exaggerations of some of the Jewish views of the Deity, surrounded by a legendary mist. The doctrines of the Atonement also, as preached by certain missionaries has evidently proved a great stumbling block to many who felt drawn toward Christ. . .. But surely this, too, is a one-sided and exaggerated view of the Atonement, it is the view of certain theologians, but not of the Gospels. The very name oft atonement never occurs in the Gospels, and but onee in the New Testament, namely in Romans, V.7, and means there no more than reconciliation.

You would be surprised if you knew how many honest Christians feel exactly what you feel about the Atonement, and that in this case also, those who compass sea and land to make one proselyte, are the very people who prevent you from becoming proselytes, from coming to Christ and to us.

And if there is nothing that should prevent the Brahmos, with all their objections to certain theologians and missionaries, from coming to us, let us see how whether there is anything that should prevent us from going to them. We read in the same paper: "The Brahmos believe in a perfect and Immutable God, whose beneficient purposes in creating man can never be frustrated. If God has created man to be saved, he is doomed to be saved. In virtue of his free will, he may for a time resist the Divine will, but he cannot for every carry on a war with the infinitely wise and infinitely loving God. The Bible distinctly says: The Lord will not cast off for ever. . . . God, who is unchangeable, and in whom there is not a shadow turning, loves the sinner, whether he sins, or becomes a saint. The change is in man. Whenever man sins, darkness comes over his soul. He trembles and cannot see the smiling face,of God. He discerns only terror and fierceness in His countenance. But whenever he repents and resolves not to disobey, the cloud passes away and the light of the benign face shines upon the sinner, and he finds reconciliation or forgiveness.

I can see nothing in this view of the Deity that is not Christian, and would be objected to by any bona fide Christian. You do not see how near you really are to us, and how it is a mere fiction of your own minds that the preachings and teachings of certain missionaries and bishops could possibly form a barrier between you and Christ. Every religion, nay, every philosophy also, varies according to those who receive it and teach it. Neither missionaries nor bishops ev^n are infallible authorities. Christianity is free to all men, every man has his own Christianity in his own heart, and in the Gospels, as understood by him. Neither the Pope nor the Archbishop of Canterbury is infallible, yet both are Christians; then why not you and your friends? The people of Europe at the time of the Reformation did what you ought to do. When they saw that the old Church of Rome did not teach the pure original Gospel, they protested and become once more true Christians, yielding to no authority but to that of Christ, as preserved in the Gospels. If you think that our various missionaries, reformed or unreformed, do not bring you pure Christianity, why should you hesitate to do what our Reformers did, go back to the Gospels and establish your own Christian Church, and defend it against all comers whether from East of from West. You are fond of saying that Christ himself was an Oriental, not an Occidental. Then why not have your own Oriental Christ, your own Oriental Christianity? Only beware from the very first of the leaven of Oriental pharises. Every religion has been founded by laymen, by men of the people; and every religion has been ruined by priests!

I have told you already that Keshub Chunder Sen, in intimate conversation, told me that to all intents and purposes he was a disciple of Christ, and when I wrote to you, and when I think of you, I cannot resist the feeling that you too are a true followers of Christ-----

Tell me some of your chief difficulties that prevent you and your countrymen from openly following Christ. I shall do my best to explain how I and many who agree with me have met them, and solved them. I do not hesitate to say that on some of these points we may have to learn from you more than we can teach you, and I say this honestly, and from personal experience. That too will be a lesson difficult to learn for our bishops and missionaries, but in Christian humility they will have.to learn it. From my point of view, India, at least the best part of it, is already converted to Christianity. You want no persuasion to become a follower of Christ. Then make up your mind to act for yourselves. Unite your flock, and put up a few folds to hold them together, and to prevent them from straying. The bridge has been built for you by those who came before you. Step boldly forward, it will not break under you, and you will find many friends to welcome you on the other shore, and among them none more delighted than your old friend and fellow labourer. F. Max Muller."

This letter of Max Muller to Protap Chunder Mozoomdar had "remained unanswered for some time" for the reasons perhaps that Mozoomdar had felt strongly incensed at the manner in which Max Muller had attempted to impose himself upon Mozoomdar. Instead of acknowledging the letter from a person who had adopted the attitude of a patron Mozoomdar caused Max Muller's letter to be published "in some of the Indian papers" along with a rejoinder only a part of which appeared in Max Muller's first biography. Mozoomdar's rejoinder clearly showed the suppressed disdain which Mozoomdar carried in his heart for the proselytizing philologist from Germany. In his rejoinder Mozoomdar had said among some other things:

"What disconcerts me is the half-expressed contempt which Christian leaders, even of the liberal school, seem to have of

the Hindu ideal and spirituality. When I express my ardent love for Christ and Christianity, they are kindly in sympathy: but the moment I say that christ and His religion will have to be interpreted in India through Indian antecedents and the Indian medium of thought, I am suspected of trying to bend Christianity down to heathenism. So we must either renounce our own national temperament, ... or renounce Christ, ... or re-embody our faith and aspirations under a new name, and form, and spirit. We have taken this third course."

Mozoomdar's rejoinder to Max Muller's letter produced some strong reactions in a few Indian quarters. One writer who identified himself only as a "Hindu" maintained that "the early belief in God, as shown in the Fedoy.waS enough to adequately satisfy the craving of the human heart after a high ideal, so as to render recourse to Christian teaching, and change in one's faith unnecessary and undesirable.

A missionary paper, Duyanodya, published from Bombay was more forthright. It tore the mask off Max Muller's face when it commented in its reply; "Every one hitherto had claimed that Max Muller thought that Hinduism is as good as Christianity, and that it is not important to be a Christian. No one can fairly say so again. He is not satisfied with even the reverence paid to Christ by Brahmos. He appeals to them to openly take the Christian name."

Another missionary paper, The Enquirer, made a similar charge and accused Max Muller in an unambiguous language when it "spoke of Max Muller as coming to the aid of the missionaries, and making his own characteristic contribution to mission work.' All this stablishes beyond doubt the fact that there were many Indians who not only saw clearly through the hideous Christian game which Max Muller had all along been playing but they also strongly resented the manner in which he had been manoeuvering and manipulating his covert moves.

When at last Mozoomdar made up his mind to reply to Max Muller's long epistle he unloaded his mind more in the fashion of an apology which is usual with those who, for being under some obligation, have neither the will to own their fault and failure nor the courage to tell an unsavoury truth to their patrons. He meekly wrote to Max Muller: "A wholesale acceptance of the Christian name by the Brahmo-Somaj is neither possible nor desirable, within reasonable time; it would lead to misconception, which would do only harm. But the acceptance of the Christ spirit, or, as you term it, "the essential religion of Christ," is not only possible, but an actual fact at the present moment. Liberal souls in Christendom will have to rest content with that at least now, and let the name take care of itself."

In Mozoomdar's letter Max Muller had perhaps seen a ray of hope while there was hardly any. Like a drowning man trying to catch at a straw Max Muller, in spite of his illness, gathered together all his strength once again to write to Mozoomdar. He wrote on November 3, 1899 from Oxford and it is likely that this letter crossed Mozoomdar's letter to him. Max Muller's letter revealed once again beyond doubt that he was only a secular Christian missionary who had all the time been hiding his true identity behind a facade he had meticulously rigged up for this purpose in the name of scholarship and philology. In this letter Max Muller came out more unabashedly:

". . . of course, I have been abused by the Indian papers, by the journals in England. Let me answer one point in your very kind letter. The name to be adopted by your own reformed Hinduism would be a merely geographical expression. Hinduism as a religion would mean the religion of the Hindus or of India, and thus would comprise every variety of religion practised in India, Durga worship, Buddhism, Mohammedanism, &c. It would be the name of a mere congeries. You object to anything like Christian, even Christian Brahmos is not satisfactory to you. But discomfiture seems to have its origin in the painful discovery that he made so late that the Christian leaders who were trying to guide him and other Brahmos were nothing but wolves in sheep's clothing.

surely you owe much to Christ and Christianity, your very movement would not exist without Christianity. One must be above public opinion in these matters, and trust to truth which is stronger than public opinion. However, the name is a small matter. Only I thought that truth and gratitude would declare in favour of Christian Brahmos, or Christian Aryas."

Max Muller was very particular that the Brahmos should call themselves some sort of Christians but he did not approve for them the nomenclature of Brahmo Christians; he wanted them to be known as Christian Brahmos. While making these suggestions in his letter he virtually reduced himself to the level of a part time catechist some of whom can be seen at times even today roaming about in some villages of Tamil Nadu and Kerala in search of converts. Max Muller especially appealed to "truth and gratitude" when he chose "Christian Brahmos" as the new name. Truth there was hardly any and we do not known to what gratitude and toward whom he had hinted in his letter. But like Nobili, Dubois and Carey this Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of scholarship also failed and went to his grave without winning any notable proselytes.

Max Mullet's letter in which he had counselled Mozoomdar that he and the other Brahmos could be Christians without, if they so wished, accepting the divinity of Christ, had raised a storm in theological and Christian circles of London and of Oxford. In consequence "an attack was made upon him in the Diocessan Conference in Oxford. . . . The attack made on him by the Principal of Pusey House was to the effect that, in his letter to Mozoomdar, Max Muller had urged the members of the Brahmo Somaj to call themselves Christians without believing in "that central doctrine on which the faith and life of the Church was founded." Max Muller was, however, not without his supporters. One of his well wishers, a vicar, supported him but what he said to vindicate Max Muller exposed him on the contrary completely as a Christian missionary who had been covertly touting for Christianity to gather converts. In his letter to the Oxford Times, the Vicar had said inter alia:

"For many years the conversion of enlightened classes of India has interested the Professor; during the last few months since his illness, it seems to be absorbing more and more of his thoughts, and the letter referred to above and addressed to Rev. P.C. Mozoomdar, was the very last literary effort which he has been able or perhaps will ever be able to make. It is published in the current journal of the Brahmo Somaj, which is now before me, and I venture to quote a few lines of Mozoomdar himself in the same journal in justification of the Professor's attitude in offering him and his followers the right hand of Christian fellowship... .""

The proselytizing philologist had perhaps' been feeling very uneasy under his skin for having failed to bring about any noteworthy conversions in spite of his lifelong masquerades in the secular field of literature. So in his last bid to achieve his missionary goal he wrote yet one more letter (Oxford March 11, 1900) to Mozoomdar — less than eight months before his death and while he was really very unwell. He begged:

"My Dear Friend, — You ought to know me enough to know that I am not trying to convert you and your friends to Christianity. If you are not a Christian, you must not call yourself a Christian. But I confess when I judge from Keshub Chunder Sen's writings, I thought that he was, and you were, more of a Christian than many who call themselves so. And if that is so, then the name of Brahmos or Hindus seemed to me a mere misnomer, and so far not quite honest....

"I do not want you to join any existing Church or sect, I only wish you to give honour to the name of Christ, to whom you owe the best part of your present religion. If you have more truth to bring into the Christian Church, do so by all means. Tell me what doctrines you wish to profess, and it would not be difficult to tell you whether they are compatible with Christianity or not. But you will have to speak definitely, so that we may understand each other

"Do not be afraid, do not leave things unsaid which you hold to be true, but which will not be popular in India. There is great work open to you, a work that must be done, and which may include Brahmans and Mohammedans as well as Christians. But to do that work well will require perfect sincerity, we require men like Keshub and like yourself.

"I wish I were younger and stronger, but as lon£ as I can I shall fight for religion in the true sense of the word. Religion should unite us, not separate us... .

Max Muller bewailed and bemoaned that he was not younger and stronger to carry on the work of conversion of the Brahmos and like a truly bigoted Christian missionary he believed that Christianity alone was the true religion. Mozoomdar who had been actively connected with the Brahmo Somaj for a long time had taken over as its leader only after (Keshub Chunder) Sen's death. In his lifetime Max Muller had written to Sen also when Sen was alive. In his epistle to Sen he had said among other things:

"You have no doubt observed among your English friends in India how easily those who call themselves Trinitarians fall into worship of three gods, and how often those who call themselves Unitarians are no better than Jews in their conception of deity. The true relation between God and man had been dimly foreseen by many prophets and poets, but Christ was the first to proclaim that relation in clear and simple language. He called himself the son of God, and he was the first-born son of God in the fittest sense of that word, and in the new and true sense of the word he was God. If you ask me whether I am a Trinitarian, I say NO; if you ask me whether I am a Unitarian, I say NO. And why? Because I believe in Christ as the son of God."

In more than one respects Max Muller happened to be an accursed man. As is usually the case with every hireling he was unloved by those who had hired his services. His own countrymen despised him like a near traitor and the knowledgeable Hindus held him in utter disdain for his clandestine efforts to denigrate, denounce and destroy Hinduism. He laboured hard to win over influential Brahmos to the side of Christianity but all his efforts in this direction met with constant rebuff. The Brahmos — one after another — spurned his offers and baited hooks with characteristics indifference for the simple reason that they just did not believe the man. Every member of the Brahmo Somaj whom he approached and whom he tried to cajole and entice into accepting Christ as his personal saviour ignored Max Muller's suggestions as coming from someone who had an axe to grind.

In his lifetime Max Muller had also made some overtures to Satyendra Nath Tagore who was another important Brahmo and was the first Indian to have passed the I.C.S. examination. On his return to India Satyendra Nath Tagore wrote to Max Muller as he had promised to do. His letter must have rebuffed the great philologist to his great dismay and disappointment. In his letter Tagore had said (May 13,1865):

"I promised when I had the pleasure of seeing you at Oxford, to send you some information on the tenets and working of the Brahmo Somaj. . . . Rammohun Roy, whom the Bengal Theists regard as the great founder of their religion, has left no works from which its distinct tenets could be gathered. The fundamental principle of all his teaching was certainly monotheism. . . . His was, for the most part, a negative task, that of pulling down the later idolatry of the Puranas. . . . His numerous treatise on the Vedas, Upanishads, &c., I think would bear out my assertion that his leading idea was to establish the doctrine of Monotheism, but that he failed to build up a positive system of religion.

". . . . The present President and Acharya, (Keshub Chunder Sen) of the Brahmo Somaj had his mind first awakened, and his spiritual aspiration fed, by some of the sublime portions of those wild treatises. When he first took them up (he took up a torn leaf accidentally which excited his curiosity) and began to pore into their pages with an earnestness which he never felt before, what struck him most was the complete absence of idolatry in what he read. Many of the Pantheistic doctrines contained in them were easily construed into Monotheism, and the result of the President's studies was a collection of passages from several of the Upanishads, breathing sublime and pure sentiments towards the Supreme Being. This collection is called the Brahma Dharma, and may be considered as the text book of the Liturgy and forms of worship of the Brahmas. It has supplied texts to the Acharya, when acting as a minister and preacher of the Somaj, for sermons in Bengali which could scarce be surpassed anywhere in sublimity of thought and vivid representation of spiritual truths. There is another little book in Bengali, prepared by me from his oral lectures, called Doctrines and tenets of the Brahma religion. A Brahma before he can be recognized as such, is called upon to subscribe to a settled form of faith and practice. The cardinal points of this faith are:

1\. In the beginning there was nothing but the one true God. He it was who created the universe.

2\. He is the true, the good, and the living God; present in all time, pervading all space, infinite, omnipotent, everlasting.

3\. In His worship is our true happiness in this world, and only salvation in the next.

4\. Our true worship consists in loving the Lord, and fulfilling the duties he has enjoined.

You will perceive that the four doctrinal points as I have given them, include to the full the doctrine of Immortality. It is written that the soul's true worship of its Maker, dependence on H.is absolute goodness, and fulfilling of the Law, are the means of its salvation in the world to come. Our solid hope of immortality proceeds from our consciousness of that union with the divine, which has its beginning only in this world....

"In regard to the doctrine of atonement, the Brahmas are, I am disposed to think, at variance with some of the Christians. We are told by the missionaries that we are sinners no ... religion has ever doubted the fact. Then we are reminded that nothing can save us from our sins but belief in Christ crucified. The Brahmas hold that it is God's absolute goodness, from which alone we can hope for our salvation. We cannot undo that which we have done — all we can do is to repent for our past sins and try with all our strength to amend our future... some of these missionaries would turn us out of God's mercy-seat, by frightening us with the awful name of Justice, as if it is something incompatible with His goodness and mercy.
