If God's mercy and goodness will not and cannot save us, the Brahmas ask, what will? 'Christ's blood' is the answer. Tell us how? Christ was innocent, and as you say, perfectly pure. He took upon Himself, it is said, all our sins, and died a torturing death. This was God's dispensation to save shiners. We cannot reconcile this with idea of God's justice. Kill the innocent to save the guilty,51 kill him not merely in a physical sense, but thrown on him all the sins of all the world, and drown him to death in an ocean of sins, that all sinners might escape! We are landed in this by the ordinary theory of atonement. We have a full faith in immortality, without which religion itself would be unmeaning. . . . the Brahmas, I may remark by the way, is horrified at the idea of eternal hell-fire, with which (the minister of Christ) so often threatens refractory individuals.

Satyendra Nath Tagore's letter would have enlightened any honest enquirer after truth but it could not have reformed a Christian missionary like Max Muller who incorrigibly thought himself never to be in the wrong. Max Muller had only wanted to gather intelligence about Brahmo Dharma in order only to discover its weak points but what Max Muller got back from Tagore was only a subtle slap in the face.

Max Muller failed both with Keshub Chunder Sen and with his lieutenant, Protap Chunder Mozoomdar, but his missionizing activities were by no means confined to the Brahmos alone. He remained active, more often from behind the scene, in his field of activity but he never succeeded in his mission of uprooting Hinduism.

## 10- Max Muller's Epistles to Malabari

It has often been claimed by some research scholars that Paul suffered from hysteria to which Dr. WA. Brend attributed Paul's conversion to Christianity. Mandslay went as far as to suggest that Paul was mad or at least partially mad and Dr. Jung seemed to be in full agreement with these findings. However, all this notwithstanding all rationalists throughout the world believe that there is no substance in the story of Paul telling people that Jesus spoke to him. Yet the fact remains that Paul was able to convince a number of people that he had told nothing but truth. A willing concurrence of Paul's listeners further confirmed Paul in his belief that Jesus had really spoken to him! All this made Paul into a saint who began advising people, who were already in a state of dazed mind, what was Christianity. And Paul's epistles to the Romans, to the Corinthians, to the Galatians and to the Ephesians as handed down to posterity set down Paul's views on the subject.

It seems, like Paul, Max Muller had been in a similar state of mind. He did not suffer from hysteria (there is no record) or epilepsy but he did suffer at intervals from such incapacitating migraine that he confessed having been a martyr to its paroxysmal attacks. Like all fanatics he believed he had a mission to fulfil. Some of his epistles portray the loathing he carried deep down in his heart for the religion of the Vedas and this made him a near religiouse. Though he did not, like Paul, turn into a persecutor the manner and the extent to which he was prepared to go to try to destroy Hinduism clearly marks him out as a Christomaniac.

The scholar extraordinary of Sanskrit from Germany nearly burned with the desire of leaving his mark in Christian missionary history and to be able to do it he wanted to convert some Hindus (Brahmos) of note to Christianity. Time and again systematic attempts have been made in the past by Christian proselytizers, some historians and many a writer, to try to cover up this most controversial side of his life and instead present him to the world as an intellectual whose first and only love was Sanskrit language and the Vedas. The mystique designedly built around him has often fooled large number of people but as time passed truth kept peeping out: "The matter (conversion of the Brahmos - Br. D. Bh.) was very near to Max Muller's heart, and his illness was already beginning when his letter was written, it has something of the nature of a dying message to the Brahmo Somaj."

Max Muller had been moving in high circles and he knew many an English statesman of his time. His writings, speeches and private discussions and conversations had raised such high hopes in the hearts of the English colonialists that he became extremely popular and a welcome figure in literary and theo-political circles that mattered. This afforded him an enviable opportunity to hob-nob and rub shoulders with even those English elite who, in those days, would have ordinarily kept a German at a distance. Since Max Muller allowed himself to be used freely and willingly as a tool in their hands they encouraged and tolerated him more than it was necessary. For his part, Max Muller paid back all these favours by acting more like a retainer touting all the time to win converts for the Church and toadies for the British crown. He went out of his way to give the impression that he was more loyal than the Englishman in trying to saff "uard Englishman's theo-political interests.

the notorious Illbert Bill had produced in India at the time of its introduction strong and widespread protests. Though the matter did not in any way concern Max Muller he voluntarily came out in support of British political policies in India. Lord Ripon on noticing Max Muller's letter which had appeared in the Times wrote to Max Muller in very condescending terms.

"Government House, September 3, 1883. Dear Professor Max Muller, — I hope that you will excuse me for troubling you with a few lines to thank you very much for the able letter which you have addressed to the Times in reply to the Report of the Judges of the Calcutta High Court upon the so-called "Illbert Bill", and also for the very valuable support which you have given to my policy in this country. I can assure you that I appreciate very highly the assistance which you have rendered to me Believe me, Yours faithfully, 'Ripon'.

Following exactly in Paul's footsteps Max Muller tried repeatedly to tell some Hindus what was Christianity; how it was the only true and pure religion; how Jesus was the only begotten son of God and some other things. His epistles to the Brahmos are outstanding examples of his missionary efforts which he made in his lifetime to convert by correspondence" these Brahmos (Hindus) to Christianity.

He wrote a few epistles to another Indian, Byramjee Malabari, and these also show very clearly in which direction his mind had been moving and what had been the purpose of his life. His one epistle to "B. Malabari Esq."p is dated "Oxford, January 29, 1882." In this he told him:

"As I told you on a former occasion, my thoughts while writing these lectures (the Hibbert) were with the people of India. I wanted to tell those few at least whom I might hope to reach in English, what the true historical value of their ancient religion is, as looked upon, not from an exclusively European or Christian, but from an historical point of view. I wished to warn against two dangers, that of undervaluing or despising the ancient national religion, as is done so often by your half-Europeanized youths, and that of overvaluing it, and interpreting it as it was never meant to be interpreted of which you may see a painful instance in Dayananda Saraswati's labours on the Veda. Accept the Veda as an ancient historical document, containing thoughts in accordance with the character of an ancient and simple-minded race of men, and you will be able to admire it, and to retain some of it, particularly the teaching of the Upanishads, even in these modern days. But discover in it "steam engines and electricity, and European philosophy and morality," and you deprive it of its true character, you destroy its real value, and you break the historical continuity that ought to bind the present to the past. Accept the past as a reality, study it and try to understand it, and you will then have less difficulty in finding the right way toward the future".

While writing all this to Malabari he had only been feigning to look friendly and a well wisher of the Hindus an impression which unfortunately has been carried by some Hindus even to this day. In addition Max Muller had laboured hard and insidiously tried to create a lobby in India which would contest, counter and repudiate the claim made by the Hindus that the Vedas were divinely inspired books.

Max Muller had tried hard to contrive to transform the Vedas from the books of Hindu religion (Hindu Dhanna Shashtras) to books of mythology and history. He had wanted some Indians to join him in this literary conspiracy but no one, except those who had either an axe to grind or those who suffered from ignorance, lent him their support in this tirade against the Veda.

The great philologist from Germany addressed another letter (September 5, 1884) to his Indian friend in which he clearly tried to play upon Malabari's fear complex:

"I truly regret all the agitation in India. It will ruin your country. . . . What would become of India if there were a second mutiny, and a successful one, it is fearful to contemplate. You will have civil war, plunder, utter barbarism. . . . With regard to your countrymen, I wonder that they care so much for the Indian Civil Service. If I were a Hindu, I should look out for very different work to benefit my countrymen. To tell you the truth, I do not believe in the efficiency of a mixed Civil Service Oil and water will not mix — let the oil be at the top, there is plenty of room for the water beneath."

Max Muller had once reiterated that his interest in religion was "chiefly historical" in order only "to see what has been, in order to understand what is" but this was more a clever spoof invented by Max Muller. As a stooge and hireling of Christian imperialism he was very much interested and fully involved in the theo-political conspiracies designed to perpetuate the English stranglehold on India. He tried to put in Malabari's heart a psychological fear of the likely consequences of another uprising by the Indians against the English. In a subtle move he had in fact been manouevring to win over Malabari to his side so that he might become his mouthpiece in Indian circles. This has all along been the common line of argument advanced by every English colonizer though all the while he has continued solemnly to profess and pretend to be an unattached and uncommitted friend of the Indians.

"The exactions and the quarrels of the native princes alone would set the country in a blaze, and every city of the north would be a scene of civil disorder between the adherents of the chief rival creeds." (Problems of Greater Britain, p. 350).

There is no doubt that Max Muller was on the side of Christian imperialism and that he wished and wanted India to be perpetually in bondage.

Max Muller's pleadings seemed to have produced no satisfactory response or result. He made, therefore, one more attempt and wrote again to Byramjee Malabari from Oxford on October 27, 1887. Pandita Ramabai who is alleged to have become a Christian by this time had impressed Max Muller considerably by her vast knowledge of Hindu Dharma Shashtras. He found her proficiency in Sanskrit really amazing more for the reason perhaps that he himself was no great scholar of the language. In this letter he threw a new bait to Malabari and counselled him:

"... Here you might combine with Ramabai. I suppose she will soon return to India. She has become a Christian, but she is not narrow minded, and may be made useful." Max Muller did not stop at that. He elaborated his plans in another letter (May 16, 1894) which he addressed to Byramjee throwing in some hints as to how Ramabai could be made useful. He wondered but advised:

"... Would the law allow widows to escape from their families, and take refuge in such houses as Ramabai and others have opened for them? You know best whether anything can be done, and what is the right thing to do. Is not a person who is able to contract a marriage, able to make a contract with Ramabai, to live at her house, and to fit herself for useful work in Life? I shall wait to hear from you on the subject, and I am willing to do what I can. Lord Rcay is now Under-Secretary of State for India, and he would help us, I think."

The secular-Christian missionary turned a philologist had been plotting to lay an alarmingly dangerous trap for Hindu widows to be enticed and seduced away from their homes. He knew it too well that, in those days, a Hindu widow who once stepped out of joint family shelter invariably found the door shut on her for good compelling her in consequence to fall at the mercy of obliging

Christian missions. Macaulay had made use of the medium of education as a means to bring about a clandestine conversion of the Hindu students of India and Max Muller was planning to take covert help from Christian jurisprudence to provide clandestine encouragement to Hindu widows to flee their homes before going over to Christianity. Both attempts were equally contemptible as both aimed to achieve unethical and immoral goals. But since a Christian missionary believes, usually, that the end sanctifies the means neither Macaulay nor Max Muller saw anything wrong in these theo-political moves which they so adroitly made on the chessboard of religion.

All his lifetime Max Muller ceaselessly worked hard and to a design to fulfil his Christian mission. He chose literature as his field of activity for furthering the cause of Christianity but likp Nobili, Abbe Dubois, Carey and Middleton he failed to achieve his goal and like them he too went to his grave with his life's ambition unfulfilled. None the less, he left behind some dangerous trails which other Christian missionaries who came after him eagerly picked up to continue the work which Max Muller had left unfinished.

## 11- The Unknown Max Muller

Max Muller was born in Germany where he went to school. Later, he struggled hard to find for himself a profession or a vocation but he failed. He made an attempt in Germany to find a publisher for his translation of the Veda but once again he failed because the German publishers considered his offer as too expensive and the proposition not economically viable. At this time of his life he suffered from want to an extent which hurt. In search of two square meals he migrated to Paris where only by collating and copying old Sanskrit manuscripts was he able with great difficulty to eke out only a humble living.

Encouraged and prompted by Bournouf who had taken some liking to him Max Muller moved to London where Bunsen took him under his wings. Mainly as a result of recommendations from Bunsen and Wilson, especially the former, the Directors of the East India Company agreed to hire him for the work of translating the Rigveda from Vedic-Sanskrita into English.

Max Muller was a scholar of unusual calibre; possessed sharp intellect and had been born with the rare instinct which unerringly told him where his mundane interests lay and how best he could nurse and nourish them. He did the job that had been entrusted to him as best he could and it has to be admitted that he did that job better than it could have been done by anyone else then living.

He was a master wheedler; a polished diplomatist and a subtle opportunist and a prolific writer who always knew how to say things which might with great ease be interpreted in more than one ways by different people in different situations at different times to serve different ends.

He was more than ambitious without having either the means or the wherewithal to achieve those ambitions. This made him miserably dependent upon those who showed inclination and willingness to help him and provide him a start in like when he needed it most.

Since he had no other way open to him and had no other options to exercise and since his life's ambition was somehow to have his translation of the Rig. published for which project he himself could neither arrange nor find sufficient finance he agreed to pawn his pen, intellect and scholarship in order to gain fame in the extremely difficult field of Sanskrits scholarship.

At the age of about 23 (he was born in 1823) when the East India Company finally agreed in 1847 to hire his services he might have thought it possible that he would be able, over the coming years in future, to win over the Englishman and sufficiently to endear himself to him if he suitably humoured him with care. But to his great dismay Max Muller soon discovered that the Englishman was far much more astute and clever than he had first imagined; in spite of all the worldly favours which the Englishman bestowed upon the scholar from Germany he continued to treat Max Muller as an alien not worthy of his trust. All this had made Max Muller feel having been neglected and left out and for this reason he very much grieved at heart.

In the early stages Max Muller had gone out of his way to throw feelers to the high-ups in the English socio-political set up suggesting that he had completely identified himself with the Englishman and his imperial (theo-political) interests. Once while writing to the Duke of Argyll he grovelled before the Duke in the manner peculiar to all hirelings. He said: "I have sometimes regretted that I am not an Englishman, and able to help more actively in the great work of educating and improving the natives."

It seems likely that Max Muller was prepared even to give up his German nationality if only he could be sure that someone would offer him British citizenship on a platter. As against this the Englishman had perhaps wanted Max Muller to ask for this favour which as a proud German he was not prepared to do with the result that he died very much a German on the English soil. While the Englishman did not love him as his own people, the Germans, nearly despised him and threatened to have him hanged if he returned to Germany.

As time passed Max Muller became painfully conscious of the reality that he was not only not recognized but he was also being carefully and methodically ignored and neglected. England had given him a home; the Englishman had given him a vocation but honour and respect eluded him and this was in spite of the best, as Max Muller thought, he had given the Englishman in return. Max Mullcr admitted that he was in Oxford on "sufferance". He also complained: "I think I have been treated without the fairness and consideration which, as a rule, are generally shown by Englishman to Englishman: but though I may have made a mistake in settling in England, and spending here the best years of my life, I shall always be thankful for having passed through this school of life."

Max Muller was born free but as he grew into a youngman he began to feel and experience the helplessness of a slave. He was completely down and out when. The reached London in 1846. According to his own admission without Bunsen's help in money and material it would have been impossible for him to stay any longer in London. Nevertheless, fate happened to smile on him and he was able to net the assignment for translating the Rig. and this work promised him more money than he had ever expected or deserved.

For more than fifty years he heartily enjoyed English hospitality and merrily basked in favours freely bestowed upon him by English politicians and the Church in return for the good work he did for the British Raj and for Christianity. But all the while he lived the life of a freed slave rather than a free man. He realized it full well that the Englishman's sympathy and his show of regard and esteem for him was only skin deep. Max Mullet" knew it instinctively and too well that the Englishman treated him no better than an alien. None the less in his struggle for his bread and butter which he no doubt received in plenty from the Englishman he lost his soul to John Bull; he lost his freedom as a man and as a scholar. And this made him bitterly sour inside and grieved at heart.

For a long time Max Muller carried the secret in his bosom but at last his patience gave way. In a letter to Keshub Chunder Sen he opened out his heart as would do a freed slave who had carefully suppressed for long his feelings against his master who otherwise had happened to be benevolent. Max Muller admitted:

"Stanley had one great advantage in starting in life, an advantage which is not sufficiently appreciated by those who possess it, because it is rightly considered very honourable if those who do not possess it surmount the difficulties of life without ever forfeiting their self-respect. Stanley belonged to a good family, and was always pecuniarily independent. He never knew the temptations of a poor struggling man, who has often to choose between sacrificing the chances of obtaining a position of usefulness and influence in the world, to which he has a good right, and making certain concessions which, however small, colour his character through life. A man who has once been silent when he ought to have spoken, or who has ever spoken when he ought to have been silent, will never be the same again, not perhaps in the eyes of the world which calls speech silver and silence gold, but in his own eyes, if he has eyes to see. And these temptations are great, so great that we ought to be very lenient in judging a poor man who may see actual want, loss of influence, loss of usefulness on one side, while one small concession to the world would lead him to the bench of judges or Bishops, or any other place for which he is the fittest man.

"There is one expression in English which I have always liked very much. 'He has an independence', that is, he has sufficient to live without caring for frowns and favours."

The first biography of Max Muller had appeared in the name of his widow, Georgina Max Muller, in 1902 — in less than two years of his death. It was in two volumes and contained a large number of letters which Max Muller had written in his lifetime to his friends, mentors, patrons and acquaintances. Looking back at the progress which the technique of printing had made by the end of the nineteenth century it looks incredible that his widow, who, after the death of her beloved husband, had been in mourning for several months was able to complete the prodigious and exacting task of collecting, collating and editing all this vast material in such a short time." And the publishers were able to place in the hands of the readers the two-volumes biography by September, 1902!

Mrs. Georgina Max Muller was able to achieve this literary feat in spite of the fact that she was, by no known standards, either an established author or a great writer. While some other similar biographies edited and written by more widely acknowledged men of letters took much longer to take shape to reach the readers the two-volumes biography of Max Muller the scholar extraordinary of Vedic-Sanskrita had made its appearance in an astonishingly short period of less than 24 months. This leads us to the only safe inference that Max Muller himself had secretly written, while he lived, the story of his own life and of his scholarly achievements which he and many others considered to be something out of the ordinary.

Though it might sound a bit too monstrous such an exercise would not be beyond a scheming diplomatist like Max Muller. Nevertheless, the possibility of Max Muller having been his own biographer will need further examination before truth can be established. Max Muller who was more than a scholar simple and pure was an indefatigable writer. He was a scheming genius, an able scholar, a master courtier and a diplomatist all rolled bto,one. He was insatiably ambitious and, above all, he was prepared and determined to do whatever lay in his power to gather for himself fame in a difficult field. As a result he feared that if someone else wrote his biography the writer might present him to tne world after his death in colours in which he did not like to appear. As the risk was too big to be taken he planned well in advance to write the story of his life which later appeared, in less than 24 months after his death, in the name of his widow. Apparently it was unmistakably a formidable task for his widow first to regain tranquility of her mind and gather her mental faculties after her dear husband's death and then to get all the material ready for the two-volume biography in a short period of 23 months. And this was despite the fact that numerous royalties, statesmen, bishops, authors, philologists and religious leaders who lived outside England with whom Max Muller had exchanged correspondence and some of whom wrote to him in languages other than English and German had to be contacted and approached before the manuscript could be got ready for the publishers.

A remarkable note in the beginning of the Life And Letters Of The Rt. Honourable Friederich Max Muller K.C. lends further credence to the hypothesis that his first biography had been handiwork of no one else but Max Muller himself. The note says: "In England the letters to Carlyle, Seller, Froude, Sir A. Grant, and other intimate friends have been burnt." Georgina Max Muller voluntarily admitted: "It is a matter of regret that much valuable correspondence has been destroyed." Would it be natural or normal for a biographer, in this particular instance the widow of a scholar of world-wide fame, to destroy and burn all correspondence which the biographer's dead husband had exchanged in his lifetime with some intimate friends?

What did motivate and prompt Georgina Max Muller to take this unusual, extraordinary and decisive step to burn and destroy all this correspondence after the death of her husbandl Did she really know what exactly was to be burnt and destroyed and what had to be saved? Did she burn it? Was it not Max Muller himself who burnt all this correspondence before did died? Does it not point to the one and the only possibility that the fateful decision to burn and destroy some evidence which existed in letters exchanged with certain intimate friends had been taken by none else but Max Muller himself in his lifetime. There are strong reasons to believe that since some of this correspondence which Max Muller exchanged with these important persons contained observations and opinions which have exposed Max Muller unfavourably he took the decision not to leave behind this evidence for posterity. All these letters, only these letters, were, therefore, burnt. Or, perhaps and this is more likely because these men of robust and fiercely independent judgement had criticized and censored the views and opinions expressed by Max Muller in a manner and to an extent which he found it neither possible nor convenient to refute and swallow he took the canny decision to burn all correspondence exchanged with them and with it the evidence. Thus he chose a safe and an easy way of imposing his own views as the only valid opinions to support his wily research in the field of religion.

In the pages of the Life and Letters we get another proof to substantiate the claim that Max Muller himself had authored his first biography viz.: "And now after four years of labour in collecting the materials, the first volume of the Hymns of the Rigveda, with Sayana's Commentary, was printed and nearly ready for publication. The text had been prepared from MSS in the Royal Library of Paris, the East India House, and the Bodleien Library, for in the whole of Germany no MSS were to be found except some very old and imperfect copies of the text and a few worm-eaten fragments of Sayana at Berlin. The MSS. in France and England had been collected and copied in Max Muller's own hand, entirely without help. The MSS. of Sayana were most imperfect, made by copyists who did not understand their subject, and therefore full of mistakes, which had all to be rectified to produce a correct text. It abounds moreover in obscure quotations, and many of the works quoted had not been edited at this time, and yet every quotation had to be verified and explained. It was a gigantic undertaking for so young a scholar, and in his Preface he gratefully acknowledges the constant encouragement he had received from men like Bournouf and Wilson"

All this is supposed to have been written by Georgina Max Muller in the year 1901 or 1902. She was narrating an event which occurred about 10 years before she married Max Muller. Apparently she had no first-hand knowledge of this occurrence. This apart, as the event h^.d occurred more than 50 years earlier some mis-statement due to sipse of memory cannot be ruled out. But this is not the case. She nearly repeated what her husband had written and noted down. Moreover, since she knew no Sanskrit for her to give a verdict that the MSS. of Sayana was most imperfect and had to be rectified to produce a correct text is highly enigmatical. She herself had no means to make out that Sayana's MSS. were most imperfect and much less was she qualified to produce a correct text. All this points only to one likely conclusion that Max Muller had written all that and later it came to be included by Georgins Max Muller in the biography of her dead husband. Max Muller was very vainglorious and the language ("had to be rectified to produce a correct text") gives us the only positive impression that it was Max Muller himself who was trying, as usual, to project himself as a great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita.

In the circumstances it was not very unnatural that both Max Muller and his first biographer should have used the same language. Max Muller said: "Sayana quotes a number of Sanskrit works which at the time when I began my edition, had not yet been edited." His biographer, Georgina Max Muller told the world equally authoritatively: "It abounds moreover in obscure quotations, and many of the works quoted had not been edited at this time. . ." The words "which at the time when I began my edition" used by Max Muller are significant. But Georgina Max Muller writing in 1901 or 1902 asserted (she used the verb abound in its present tense) as if Sayana's commentary continued to abound in obscure quotations even after a lapse of about 50 years although much headway had since been made by scholars in understanding these quotations from Sayana which in the year circa 1847 had appeared obscure to Max Muller.

The possibility that Max Muller had told his wife, Georgina Max Muller, about the most imperfect nature of Sayana's MSS cannot be ruled out. In such a case he must have chivalrously bragged before her that he had to rectify Sayana's MSS. to produce a correct text and she would have, in perfect ignorance, accepted the claim made by Max Muller at its face value. Had this been the case Georgina Max Muller would have surely qualified her statement. She might have written something like this: the MSS. of Sayana, as Max Muller told me more than once, was most imperfect... or, the MSS. of Sayana, as he so often used to point out, was most imperfect.... But she said nothing of this type; she almost echoed Max Muller's words. En passant it might here be mentioned that in spite of the fact that "over and over again" Max Muller was "stopped by some short enigmatical reference to Panini's grammar or Yasaka's glossary, which he could not identify and in spite of often having been "in perfect despair, because there was some allusion in Sayana which he could not make out, and which no other Sanskrit scholar, not even Bournouf or Wilson, could help him to clear up," Max Muller knew that Sayana's MS. was most imperfect\*. He did not understand Sayana yet he corrected him. Max Muller must have taken great pains indeed and expended extraordinary efforts to achieve this feat of correcting Sayana whom he did not understand. He admitted: "It often took me whole days, nay, weeks, before I saw light." This speaks volumes for Max Muller's extraordinary scholarship and knowledge of Vedic-Sanskrita. That is how whenever a Veda mantra did not make any sense to him he made nonsense out of it.

It might quite legitimately be asked: Why did Max Muller adopt such a course and choose to author his own biography? The question , needs to be answered. Like all insatiably ambitious men who more often than not sooner or later turn megalomaniacs fame was Max Muller's last resort. Though his writings, speeches and lectures had made him famous and known in his lifetime as a great scholar of the Rig. and as a philologist the real Max Muller, the man in him, remained esoterically hidden and unknown. As a shrewd man of the world he knew it too well that what he had written and spoken as a scholar was likely to be reviewed in due course and as a result to be found wanting in more than one respects. The lurking fear which he constantly carried in his heart incessantly reminded him that his researches which then looked extraordinary were likely to be brushed aside in the near future by some more weighty objective findings. The thought was too much for him to bear. The feeling that he might soon be forgotten and forsaken by the world constantly haunted him like a nightmare from wfyich he found no way to escape.

At one time Max Muller had sincerely believed, and piously wished, the British rule in India to last for all times to come and though he thought he may not live to see it he hoped India — the Hindus - to be soon christianL.;d. He guessed and perhaps saw dreams suggesting to him that if somehow he could leave behind an authentic record and account of his invaluable but silent contribution toward the task of converting the Hindus of India to Christianity he would be remembered for ever in gratitude by entire Christendom as a new prophet. The temptation to carve a niche for himself in history was too great for Max Muller to resist and he was prepared to do anything to succeed. As a result he lived in a paradise of his own making and continued to apply his mind and energies to achieve the goals he had set for himself.

Max Muller had been masquerading all his lifetime from behind the mask of philology and Sanskrit scholarship. He could ill afford to let the world know, while he lived, the truth about his missionizing activities but, he argued, it would hardly matter if the world came to know of his secret motives after he was dead. That he fully subscribed to this philosophy is clearly borne out from a letter he had written to Protap Chunder Mozoomdar in which he quoted Keshub Chunder Sen, after Sen had been dead, to have said: "And if fifty years hence people should find out that I have been doing the work of Christ, what harm is there?" He often broke and transgressed the healthy norms of honest scholarship by holding back on purpose information the disclosure of which did not suit his convenience. Once he admitted: "When I began to write down some of my recollections of the Dean (Dean Stanley - Br.D. Bh.) which I thought might be of interest and possibly of use to Keshub Chunder Sen, they soon grew beyond the limits of a letter, and I could only send him a few of my notes from time to time. Some have been published, others are not quite fit for publication yet."

According to Max Muller it was Keshub Chunder Sen who had first mooted this possibility ("it fifty years hence....") but when Max Muller first made this important disclosure Sen had been dead. The possibility, therefore, that Max Muller had only tried to put words into Sen's mouth cannot entirely be ruled out. Whatever be the truth the fact remains that Max, Muller himself willingly accepted this philosophy. Like most diplomatists who often thrive on double standards Max Muller too immensely enjoyed this exercise. In a letter which he wrote on the death of Dean Stanley he had given himself ^way by-admitting that he had held back some information because he dill not think the time was ripe for its disclosure!

It might safely be said that at heart Keshub Chunder Sen was a follower of Christ but, it seems, he did not have the moral courage openly to admit it. As Max Muller saw nothing wrong in living a double life like Sen he too would have confided in Georgina Max MulJer and told her: if after his death people should find out that he had been doing the work of Christ, what harm will there be?

Max Mullet fought his religious and scholarly battles from behind fortifications especially rigged up for his protection. Whenever he knew he was on a firmer ground he was outspokenly bold and harsh in criticizing his opponents. However, if ever he even slightly expected the outcome to go against him he invariably either withdrew or kept discreg% silent.

Col. Ingersol- (b. 1833-d. 1899), a great thinker on matters concerning the Christian religion, was a contemporary of Max Muller. His writings and speeches on Christianity had created an unprecedented interest among free thinking men and women and equal indignation in the hearts of the Christians - ecclesiastics and laymen alike. In the year 1881 Ingersol entered into a debate on Christian religion in the pages of the North American Review whose editor had invited some Christian theologians and ecclesiastics of high standing to join in these disputations. Henry Ward Beecher and Jeremiah S. Black were the only two who offered to defend the Christian side; Max Muller preferred to keep out despite his profound reverence aira admiration which he persistently and constantly used to exhibit in his writings and speeches for Christ and his Christianity.

In the year 1887 and 1888 yet another debate (in print) took place between Ingerf ol on one side and on the other side Field, Manning, and Gladstone. Ingersol's rejoinders were so conclusively smashing and incisive that these left his opponents defending Christianity no possible ground to stand upon and virtually gasping for breath. As the editor of the North American Review who sponsored these debates was "well acquainted with most of the leading men of his time in America and England" we have every reason to believe he was equally acquainted with Max Muller and with the work the German scholar had been doing for the past 30 years at Oxford. On this account we can safely presume that an invitation had been sent to Max Muller to participate. Astonishingly, however, Max Muller who otherwise had always been in the forefront and always too vociferous in giving a good chit to the Bible and to Christian religion again chose, mysteriously, to keep away from these debates.

Discretion, it is said, is the better part of valour and a mercenary unsure of his ground would nearly always turn more nobly discreet when he forsees nothing but disgrace and humiliation to follow if he chose to be brave. Max Muller neither dared to take part in these disputations nor did he venture later to cross swords with Col. Ingersol who in his own inimitable manner had demolished all arguments offered in support of those Christian myths and fibs which as an ardent Christian and as a devout missionary Max Muller used always to uphold as trustworthy, historic and authentic.

Despite a large number of books written by him and numerous speeches he delivered Max Muller had remained nearly undiscovered as a man. The world knew him more either as a scholar ofSanskrita literature or as a clever exponent of the Bible. But the number of people who really understood him and his many faceted personality must indeed be, even today, microscopically small.

Max Mullet the German from Dessau has often been described either as a great scholar of Vedic-Sanskrita or as a philologist. At least one writer called him "profoundest Vedic scholar". His second biographer named him a scholar extraordinary. Some ill informed Hindus of the 19th and also of the 20th century thought him to be a benefactor of Hinduism. Swami Dayananda likened him to a child who was still learning to walk. A small number of the knowledgeable Hindus have given him the distinction of being the greatest enemy of the Hindus. All this notwithstanding there is yet another side of his character which remains shrouded in mystery.

Honesty is a virtue and blessed is the man who can remain honest in spite of trying circumstances which tend to compel him to be less virtuous. To be honest with his pen is a duty which is incumbent upon a scholar who takes upon himself the obligation of advising people and accursed must be he who wilfully tries to foist his wrong findings upon the innocent and the unwary who have neither the means to protect themselves nor the will to fathom the truth.

There is no doubt whatever that Max Muller was a great scholar and an intellectual of a very high calibre. Whether he was also honest is very much a moot point. The evidence available form Max Muller himself unmistakably points to the only irrefutable conclusion that in spite of all these great qualities he knowingly refused to be an honest scholar.

He had his reason for twisting the meanings of many a Veda mantra and for misinterpreting them to suit his and his masters' convenience. He can easily be forgiven for having adopted this course. He was human and anyone else placed in similar circumstances might have as readily succumbed likewise. But no one wanted or expected him to do what he himself voluntarily admitted to have done without any outside pressure. In a letter which he had written to Professor Deussen of Kiel on December 4, 1899 - less than a year before his death - Max Muller had confessed:

"Of course, Alexandria was so close to India and so close to Athens that spiritual intercourse was quite possible. But what good can possibilities do to us? The Syrian Bardesanes had heard something about India, much sooner than Alexandrians, but as it seems, to me, from ambassadors to Rome from India. / have so far carefully withheld from the idea of Indian influence on Alexandria, but of course the possibility must be faced."

The scholar extraordinary of Vedic-Sanskrita from Germany was extraordinary in one more respect, not much known to the world. He had his own mental reservations and because he did not want to give credit to Hinduism he withheld with care truth and prevented it by design from reaching the people. He was like that dishonest and unscrupulous scientist who having discovered, after long research, a cure for cancer or diabetes or tuberculosis designedly withheld the results of his research only because he did not want a particular person whom he did not like to be benefited! Max Muller did not like Hinduism and, therefore, was not willing to give credit to Hindu India which it legitimately deserved. He, therefore, held back the results of his researches in his field of activity. While he agreed that although in due course the possibility of truth coming out will have to be faced he himself was not prepared to let the truth be known as long as he could help. It was a despicable exercise in which the secular Christian missionary schemingly indulged only to satisfy his own dislike of Vedic Hinduism and its influence on the world at large.

He had known it too well that Alexandria had not influenced Vedic India and that on the contrary Vedic Hinduism had influenced Alexandrian, Syrian and other ancient civilizations but he withheld the truth from being known. And this was despite the fact that he himself had admitted:

"After having thus carefully examined all the traces of supposed foreign influences that have been brought forward by various scholars, I think I may say that there really is no trace whatever of any foreign influence in the language, the religion, or the ceremonial of the ancient Vedic literature of India."

Max Muller was a Christian by birth. He grew as a Christian and belonged to one of the several Churches which then thrived in Germany. After having lived for some years in England he switched over his religious loyalties to the Anglican Church and became a member of the Church of England.

For more than fifty years he had been doing the work of translating the Rigveda and some other sacred texts of Hinduism. In the course of his professional work he frequently and repeatedly had a chance to meditate and reflect upon the philosophy of Vedic Hinduism as enunciated by the Vedas, by the Bhagwadgita and by some Samhitas. Fifty years is a long period and it was not possible and normal for a man like Max Muller with his sharp intellect to come in constant and regular contact with the lofty teachings of the Vedas and yet to remain unaffected and uninfluenced; these teachings left an indelible impact on his mind.

Max Muller was born a Christian and he lived like a Christian but it might be said without any fear of contradiction that he died a Hindu without, of course, having undergone any formal conversion (shuddhi). There is ample proof to support this contention. Once Max Muller himself admitted: "Here in Oxford I have once more have violently attacked as a heretic. Woe to him who is not a heretic, who does not choose for himself."

Many a Christian ecclesiastic believed that Max Muller had turned a heretic and anyone who understands Christianity a little bit knows it too well that no heretic can ever be, has ever been, a Christian. In the past, heretics were burnt on the stake or were stoned to death; Max Muller was spared only because the Christian Church and the missions considered him too valuable an acquisition as a means for the conversion of the Hindus to Christianity. The Church and the Christian missions were not prepared to sacrifice larger Christian interests; they agreed among themselves to suffer willingly the indignity of having among them a heretic as a necessary evil because it served their theo-political purpose.

At an early stage during the course of his contact with the teachings of the Rig. Max Muller had started doubting the validity of Christianity as laid down in the Bible. His Preface to his Chips From A German Workshop carried the significant passage: "He must be a man of little faith, who would fear to subject his own religion to the same critical tests to which the historian subjects all other religions." There is no doubt that when Max Muller subjected Christianity to the same tests as were applied by Christian missionaries to Hinduism he found Christianity wanting in many respects. As a result, doubts started springing up in his mind. It was for no other reasons that he was abused for being a heretic.

There is some more evidence extant in the pages of Max Muller's first biography to establish the fact that he had turned, in his lifetime, a Hindu - a follower of the religion of the Vedas. He made the admission, though only tacitly, while paying a tribute to the teachings of the Upanishads. He had said:

"During March Max Muller delivered three Lectures at the Royal Institution on the 'Vedanta Philosophy.'. . . . The Lectures were an attempt to interest an English Audience in the philosophy of the leading school of the thinkers of ancient India - the school that appealed most to the mind and heart of the lecturer, so that he could, as the result of his own experience during a long life devoted to the study of many philosophies and many religions, endorse the words of Schopenhauer, 'In the whole world there is no study so beneficial and elevating as that of the Vedanta philosophy, as contained in the Upanishads. It has been the solace of my life, it will be the solace of my death.' Max Muller was most anxious to impress on his hearers that there was nothing esoteric in the Vedanta philosophy, that it was open to all; and he closed his lecture by repeating the Sanskrit line in which a native philosopher formulated the whole teaching of the Vedanta philosophy, which Max Muller translated 'God is true, the world is fleeting, man's soul is God and nothing else.' "

The first biography of Max Muller has more than one proofs to establish the truth that as a result of his long and intimate contact with the study of the Vedas he had sloughed off his belief in Christianity and turned a follower of the Vedic Dharma. In the second volume of his first biography we get this to read:

"One of the best reviews of Max Muller's work and life appeared in the Open Court, published at Chicago, from which the following extracts are given by permission: -

"As to his personal belief, we may say generally that Professor Max Muller was a Vedantist. He was a believer in the Brahman doctrine of the atman, or soul-in-itself, the monad soul; he believed in a "thinker of thoughts," a "doer of deeds," a Self within the person, which was the carrier of his personality, and a Self without, which was the carrier of the world, "God, the highest Self; these two are ultimately the same Self.'

Without, however, some more authentic proof and evidence it would be neither fair nor reasonable to dub Max Muller a Hindu and claim that he had become a follower of the religion of the Vedas. After his death his wife had to write to many people to seek clarifications and togather material for his biography. In reply to one of her enquiries the Vicar of St. Gilse's had written back to her in September 1902 a letter in which he completely and emphatically laid bare the truth that Max Muller did not believe or accept as true the biblical miracles described in the New Testament - The Vicar had told Mrs. Georgina Max Muller:

"Although his faith in Christ was so firm, and his acceptance of Him, as indeed the Son of God, was so heartfelt and sincere, it is hardly necessary to say that it was not based upon the miraculous element contained in the Gospels, much less upon any form of ecclesiastical authority."

The Vicar had made it very clear that Max Muller did not accept Christianity on the basis or strength of miracles supposed to have been performed by Jesus. This is exactly what Rammohun Roy had believed when he wrote the Precepts of Jesus. He had carefully and meticulously excluded from his narratives all accounts of biblical miracles (the virgin birth; the resurrection and the subsequent ascension) and it was precisely for this reason that the Serampore missionaries had called Rammohun Roy a heathen. Even if his non-belief in biblical miracles did not make Max Muller a heathen it certainly singled him out as a non-Christian.

To be a non-Christian is one thing and to be a Hindu Is entirely something different. Since a non-Christian need not necessarily be a Hindu it is not safe to assume that Max Muller had turned a Hindu only just because he was not a Christian. We must have some more authentic proof to support this hypothesis. Fortunately we do have an irrefutable evidence in support of our contention though for some strong and valid reasons he did not take the undue risk of proclaiming his apostasy. We can be sure such an admission on his part made in the open would have brought untold socio-economic miseries upon the entire Max Muller family.

The Christian Church has systematically slogged and worked hard to create a lot of confusion around Jesus. Even the Christians, by and large, do not really understand the essential difference between Jesus and Christ. Most Christian writers, columnists and historians regularly continue to add to this confusion by starting their narratives with Jesus which on purpose they end with Jesus Christ or just with Christ. The intention is nearly always to make confusion worse confounded and not to let the laity think clearly and know the truth.

One thousand nine hundred and ninety years ago Jesus was a common name in Israel and there then lived several Jesuses when the story of the New Testament is supposed to have unfolded itself. More than one miracles are supposed to have been performed by one of these Jesuses who also then lived in that country. First, he is said to have been born of a virgin mother. Second, he came back to life after he had actually been dead. And third, after coming back to life it is said he bodily ascended up into the heaven above where he is now supposed to be sitting on the right hand of God. These miracles, and these miracles alone, imparted to this Jesus divinity which in turn made this Jesus divine - a Christ.

All other Jesuses were and continued to be mortals. Because they performed no miracles they remained ordinary humans; they were not divine. Therefore, anyone, especially any Christian, who does not accept or who refutes the validity of these miracles truly denies the divinity of Christ. And Christ bereft of his divinity becomes one of the several Jesuses who then lived in Israel and all of whom died like any other ordinary human being.

Max Muller's faith in Christ "was not based upon the miraculous element contained in the Gospels...." Max Muller did not subscribe to the view that one of the several Jesuses who then lived in Israel had performed these miracles. In other words he did not believe that Christ was divine. This unmistakably meant that while he accepted Jesus he rejected Christ of the New Testament. In short he was not a Christian; he was either a heathen or a heretic. Since, however, Max Muller did believe in One God he cannot be called a heathen. He must have been the follower of another theistic religion.

Is it not possible to know Max Muller's religious beliefs? Did he leave no indications or proofs? We already know it was not Christianity and there is nothing to suggest that he was a follower either of Judaism or of Buddhism or of Mohemmadanism. On the basis of information we already have we know that not only he himself was a Vedantist he had also wanted his audiences to accept Vedanta as their way of life. But what was it that Max Muller himself believed! We have it on authority from Max Muller himself:

"I believe in the Continuity of self. If there were an anhilation or complete change of our individual self-consciousness we might become somebody else, but we should not be ourselves. Personally, I have no doubt of the persistence of the individual after death, as we call it. I cannot imagine the very crown and flower of creation being destroyed by its author."

Max Muller had been more than clear and explicit in explaining what he believed. He believed in the continuity of self. Self cannot by any stretch of imagination mean the body because it is common knowledge that the body does not continue for the reason that it is a compound of several elements. It just cannot continue. There must, therefore, be something else represented by "self which continues.

When a Christian dies they say he or she gave up the ghost which means the body gave up something. This is perfectly in conformity with the Christian belief that only those who believe on Christ and are baptized shall be saved and that they alone shall again bodily rise after death. In this age of science and secularism the claim sounds highly risible, the body of a Christian when buried soon begins to decay and disintegrate and finally it disappears. It cannot, therefore, bodily rise again. It is thus clear that it is not the body that continues or can continue; it must be something else which continues. The thing that continues, no matter what it might be called, must be the master and it must own and possess the other thing called the body which does not continue. The body which cannot, and does not, continue cannot give up the other thing Jthe ghost) because one cannot give up what one does not possess.

As against this the Hindus believe that when someone (whether he or she is a Hindu, a Christian, a Jew, a Mohemmadan or even an atheist) dies his or her body ceases to exist - it comes to an end; it stops to live. They describe this state of ceasing to be as dehant (^?T?T) . The word is a combination of two words deh fa) meaning body and ant (SRT) which means end. So death means the end of the body. They further believe that the soul (atman) which possessed the body leaves the physical being and departs and that the soul (atman) continues to be as it had been as before. The soul (atman) gives up the body which the soul (atman) possessed and as a result of this giving u.p by the soul the body dies. The body dies and comes to an end; the soul (atman) the master of the body continues. Max Muller called it Self.

Max Muller believed in the continuity of Self which was his way of saying that the soul is eternal - it neither dies nor is it born. He made it clear that personally he had no doubt that the individual persisted after death. This again is the same what the Hindu say to express their undoubt.ng belief in the phenomenon of transmigration of soul. The Hindu knows that when a man dies it is only his body that dies and comes to an end. There is something apart and distinct from body which was before death and which continues to be (persists) after death. The Hindu calls it atman (soul) which according to his religious belief neither dies nor takes birth — it continues and persists as before and as it has always been. The lofty teachings of the Vedas and the perpetually true and really universal tenets of the Vedic Dhanna would leave a favourable impact and impression on any mind honestly in search of truth and an intellectual like Max Muller who had so closely been in touch with those lofty and pristine teachings for more than half a century could not have remained without being converted. That he did not formally declare himself a Hindu is altogether another thing which is easily understandable. He did not want to be thrown overboard and humiliated by the society which had given him hearth and home. There was no point in it and if he did not formally convert he did nothing wrong. After all Hinduism does not look much with favour on religious conversions.

It is often said and claimed by Christian missionaries that Christianity is acceptable to the Hindus. The claim is at times supported and echoed by some Hindu leaders for divers reasons. Nevertheless, the claims so made are totally hollow because no knowledgeable Hindu will ever accept Christianity if he understood what Christianity really was. Christianity depends entirely, exclusively and conclusively upon the divinity of Christ which divinity in turn rests precariously on the story of Jesus having performed the three miracles of virgin birth, resurrection and the subsequent ascension. Resurrection is the single most important corner-stone on which the entire edifice of Christianity is rested ("And if Christ be not risen, then is our preaching vain, and your faith is also vain" and "And if Christ be not raised, your faith is vain; ye are yet in your sins" and again "For if the dead rise not, then is not Christ raised:" -Corinthian XV: 14,17,16). The Christians believe that as Christ came back to life so will all of the faithful who believe on him shall also bodily rise. Today the world knows it too well that when a person dies his or her body which is a chemical compound dies and comes to an end. It just cannot come back to life.

The Hindu knows that Christian beliefs and dogmas are diametrically opposed to Hindu beliefs and to scientific truths. He knows it for certain that when a person dies, no matter whether he or she happened to be a Hindu or a Christian or an atheist, his or her body dies; the soul (atman) which possessed that body continues and persists. And Max Muller believed it too like a Hindu. All this makes a Hindu a Hindu and marks him a separate religious being apart from a Christian. Since Max Muller believed like a Hindu in the transmigration of soul (continuity of Self) he was very much a Hindu. But very few people seem to be able to comprehend this truth to know the unknown Max Muller.

Like a true Hindu without wearing any outward signs and marks of popular Hinduism he died a true Hindu believing so ardently and devoutly in the Vedanta and the Vedas. The Vedanta had been the solace of his life while he lived and it truly was the solace of Max Mullet's death. It was a truly Hindu finish to a Christian masquerade.

## 12 - Trails Left by Max Muller

Christian missionary gasconade and magniloquence has consistently tried to present one of their tribe, Max Muller the secular missionary, as a friend of the Hindus and a benefactor of Hinduism. In reality he was neither; he was perhaps the greatest enemy of both. Max Mullet's effectiveness as the arch enemy of Hinduism lay dangerously hidden in his subtle equivocations and in his ability, which seemed congenital, successfully to pass a counterfeit coin without being caught while performing the feat. His capacity to look honest and sound sincere on surface was prodigious.

The Hindus of the nineteenth century failed to discover and understand Max Muller and his Maxmullerism because, by and large, being too gullible they believed whatever Max Muller wrote and said. Since he had been in addition, covertly supported and encouraged by the Englishman who then ruled Hindustan he was able to mount his veiled attacks on Hinduism again and again from behind the convenient cover of philological research.

Max Muller's capacity to control his emotions was amazing and this made him fit more to be a career diplomatist than a philologist or a student of Vedic-Sanskrita. Nevertheless, on some rare occasions he did give himself away. In spite of his disarming and affable manners which too often threw his adversaries and critics off their guard at times he did sound insincere and look suspect. He chose his words with meticulous care and this made his writings and speeches highly diabolic to the extent that each one of his readers and listeners felt immensely happy and satisfied to extract the meanings which suited his preconceived whims and prejudices most.

The German philologist was an equivocator par excellence. Like most experienced hypocrites he excelled in simulations. As a clever (secular) missionary he masqueraded from behind the mask of scholarship so well that no one doubted his credentials. Though like most other Christian missionaries of the nineteenth century he too harboured great aversion for Hinduism unlike most of them he was extremely careful and circumspect whenever he chose to criticize and censor the Veda and its religious teachings and beliefs. In his endeavour to provide convert guidance to other Christian missionaries of his time and also to those who were yet to come on the scene after him he left behind more than one leads which were eagerly and faithfully taken up by all those who, like him, wished and wanted to uproot Hinduism.

Max Muller was perhaps the first Christian missionary who not only wanted to destroy Hinduism but also planned the operation carefully. How could he do it, he asked himself? He came to the conclusion that it could be done if he could successfully denigrate the Vedas — the most important Dharma Shastras of the Hindus — and ridicule their teachings. He, therefore, decided to give wrong and twisted meanings to Veda mantras. In a letter which he wrote to his wife (December 9, 1866) he said (in translation): ". . . this edition of mine (there were several other translation already in existence but he was sure only of his edition to do the job — Br.D.Bh.) and the translation of the Veda will hereafter tell to a great extent on the fate of India (by India he meant only the Hindus — Br.D.Bh.), and on the growth of millions of souls, in that country. It is the root of their religion, and to show them what the root is, I feel sure, is the only way of uprooting all that has sprung up from it during the last 3000 years" (the scholar extraordinary seemed also to be sure that the Veda was only 3000 years old-Br.D.Bh.).

Max Muller had thrown a mischevious hint which many another Christian missionary eagerly lapped up. As a result they all set for themselves a common goal — to uproot Hinduism; denigrate the Veda and run down Vedic edicts.

There have been more than one Christian missionaries who too obediently and faithfully followed in the footsteps of Max Muller and some of them displayed greater zeal only perhaps because they were less informed. For example:

"The faith of the Hindus in their sacred writings was unbounded, and about 40 years ago (he alludes to the year 1847 when Max Muller was hired by the East India Company — Br.D.Bh.) some of the ablest of the missionaries thought if this faith in the divine origin of these books could be destroyed, they could then reasonably hope that the people (the Hindus — Br.D.Bh.) would listen with unprejudiced minds to their statements respecting Christianity. The mind of India was like jungle-covered land; this jungle needed to be cut down before the soil could receive the good seed of the kingdom (of Jesus Christ—Br.D.Bh.). The mind of India was wrapped in the grossest darkness.it was necessary for light to enter that they might be able to see and appreciate "the unmeasurable richep of Christ." (Daily Life and Work in India by W.J. Wilkins, p. 251-52).

Max MulJer too had very adversely commented on the Veda while he was talking about Rammohun Roy. Max Muller had jibbed at the divine origin of the Veda and said: "To Rammohun Roy the Veda was true, because it was divine; to his followers it was divine because it was true. And which of the two showed greater faith?"2 When he made these remarks he had in his mind Keshub Chunder Sen whom he wanted to extol because Max Muller had been trying hard to convert Sen to Christianity.

"The Rik is polytheistic, but contains numerous phrases which show in which direction the minds of the worshippers were tending". (The Crown of Hinduism by J.N. Farquhar, p. 72-3).

In this sense, then the religion of Christ is the spiritual crown of the religion of the Rigveda. It must die into Christianity, (ibid. p. 51-4 and 77).

I have followed the advice of a skilful missionary who says: Get illustrations, arguments, parables and similes. Beg, borrow, steal and invent, and cultivate the habit of drawing them (the Hindus-Br.D.Bh.) from your surroundings at any moment. ... I am under special obligation to the volumes of Monier-Williams, Mitchel, Hopkin, Wilson, Murdoch, Padfield, Hunter, Wilkins, Vaughan, Thompson, Caldwel, Row, Waren, Spurgeon, Wolbing, Max Muller and others too numerous to mention. (The Arsenal or Christian Soldiers in India by Rev. J. Fr. Stacker).

"The Hindu religion resembles the Christian as a counterfeit coin resembles the true one", (ibid. p. 493).

Max MulJer had been commissioned to do a particular job and he honestly felt committed to deliver the goods. He had wanted to be true to the word he had given to the Board of Directors of the East India Company that he would do his best to uproot Hinduism from India and plant, in its place, the religion of Christ. To achieve this great Christian end he had essentially to do two things viz. to run down Hinduism (Vedic Dharma) and to applaud and extol Christ and his Christianity.

In the course of an interview Max Muller gave to Catholic Commonwealth he said:"... there is no doubt, however, that ethical teaching is far more prominent in the Old and New Testament than in any other sacred book".3 In reply to another question of the interviewer "would you say that any one sacred book is superior to all others in the world?" Max Muller had boldly replied: "It may sound prejudiced (he knew the truth! — Br.D.Bh.), but taking all in all, I say the New Testament. After that I should place the Koran, which in its moral teaching is hardly more than a later edition of the New Testament. Then would follow, according to my opinion, the Old Testament, the Southern Buddhist Tripitaka, the Taote-King of Leo-tze, the Kings of Confucious, the Veda and the Avesta".4

It seems Max Muller had developed pathological aversion for the Veda. The reason? He wanted to extol the Bible but in the presence of the Veda which himself he declared as the oldest (religious) book in the library of mankind, he found it not easy. As he read and studied the Veda more and more greater and more intense became his discomfiture. He often wondered how was it possible for the Veda to be preserved in its original and purest form even though there then existed no art of printing? The thought that this made the Veda clearly and easily much, much more posterior in time to the Bible kept gnawing at his heart.

Max Muller had discovered that the remarkable feat of preserving the Veda, the oldest book in the library of mankind, had been made possible due entirely to the ingenuity of the Brahmins of Hindustan who for generations had been specialising in the task both of learning and teaching the Veda. To his great amazement and dismay Max Muller discovered that the Brahmins had been committing to memory the entire text of the Veda and kept on passing it to their progeny — from one generation to another and yet to another. He felt flabbergasted and this made him nearly sick inside. Though he accepted and admitted the unmatchable ingenuity of the Brahmin he, for the same reason, despised him most. He felt as if he had been cheated at the game. He made up his mind that if Hinduism was to be destroyed it was necessary to destroy the Brahmin first. His dream of declaring to the world that the Bible was the oldest scripture had crumbled to dust. The edifice on which he wanted to rest the superiority of the Bible lacked strong foundation. As a result he turned against the Brahmins of India and became their arch enemy.

In the year 1869 a Frenchman, Louis Jaco.liot, who had been a chief judge in Chandernagore (erstwhile French India) wrote a book, La Bible dans I'lnde, in French which was translated the following year into English. Jacolliot had said: "Land of ancient India! Cradle of humanity, hail! Hail, revered motherland whom centuries of brutal invasions have not yet buried under the dust of oblivion. Hail, Fatherland of faith, of love, of poetry and of science, may we hail a revival of thy past in our Western future".

The praise that, Jacolliot showered on India was too much for Max Muller to chew and digest. While reviewing the book he gave his acrimonious opinion: "the author seems to have been taken in by Brahmins in India". The book had come to the notice of the British Prime Minister, W.E. Gladstone, to whom Max Muller wrote in a letter on July 9, 1869 saying: ". . . Jacolliot's book, La Bible dans I'lnde, which I looked at, is beneath criticism, it is simply untrue. The author has been deceived, has deceived himself, and tries to deceive others". La Bible dans I'lnde made Max Muller more bitter against the Brahmins in India. Whenever he got a chance he railed against the Brahmins and remonstrated against them till anti-Brahminism became a cult with the Christian missionaries.

As a Christian missionary Max Muller not only touted for the Church and the mission agencies he also spoke and wrote about the Veda and the Brahmins disparagingly. He was a scholar-colporteur. What he said and wrote to revile Brahmins slowly became a creed and an article of faith with the Christian missionaries and writers who willingly and readily joined in the chorus.

"Brahminism, therefore, must die out. In point of fact, false ideas on the most ordinary scientific subjects are so mixed up with its doctrines that the commonest education — the simplest lessons in geography — without the aid of Christianity must inevitably in the end sap its foundations." (Modern India and the Indians by Monier-Williams, p. 261).

"When the walls of the mighty fortress of Brahminism are encircled, undermined, and finally stormed by the soldiers of the Cross, the victory of Christianity must be signal and complete." (ibid. p. 262).

"My Dear Viceroy, — That most essential duty of a Christian prince, namely, attention to the interest of religion. . . moves us to issue the following order: That all idols shall be brought out and destroyed, and severe penalties shall be laid upon all as shall dare to make an idol. . . or shall shelter or hide a Brahman." This order had been issued by King Joao II of Portugal. Quoted in A History of Missions in India by Julius Richter, pp. 53-4).

"When one considers that this disproportionately numerous Brahman sect, with its selfish class interests, is inseparably bound up with Hinduism, its gods and its temples, its language and its literature, and when one consider further that Hindu India has for fifteen hundred years and more (he supposes that the Brahmans and Hinduism have existed only for about 1500 years and more — Br.D.Bh.) been accustomed to regard Brahmins as its leaders both in religion and in mental culture, one can understand what a mighty rampart is here raised against Christian mission" (Modern India and the Indians, p. 254).

". . . the conversion of India in the widest sense of the word, is the first and the greatest mission to which Western Christendom is called. The faithful and mistaken attempts of the Early Church, the long neglect or cruel intolerance that succeeded these up to a century ago have made Brahminisrn, with its offspring (this seems to be a misprint for offsprings—Br.D.Bh.) more powerful enemies of Christ"... (The Conversion of India by George Smith, p. 2. He was a missionary in Vizagapatnam in 1869).

"The Vedic religion gives birth to Brahtninism." (ibid. p. 5).

"Through Brahminism the Hindus have been missionaries of evil to the aboriginal people of India... (ibid. p. 5).

"So early as the first year of the century Carey (of the Baptist Mission in Serampore near Calcutta — Br.D.Bh.) saw the need of English education as a weapon in the warfare with Brahminism ..." (ibid. p. 134).

"In Britain, America, Germany, the cry for results that can be tabulated and for success that is evident, the preference of methods which produce immediate fruit in individuals to those which work for the destruction of Brahminism itself and the creation, of Christian nations, are of this subtle nature." (ibid. p. 216).

In order to denigrate and denounce the teaching of the Veda Max Muller had invented Henotheism. Some of more ardent followers and admirers belonging to the same tribe went a step further. They equated Hinduism with Brahminism e.g.

"It is this contamination which has led scholars (Western scholars - BrJD.Bh.) to divide the Indian (Indian here means the religion of the Indians — Hinduism-Br.D.Bh.) religion under three heads — Vedism, Brahminism and Hinduism as generally understood. ..." A Short History of Religions by R.E. Kellet, p. 390).

"And out of this philosophical, ethical and transcendental Brahminism grew the modern Hinduism, in which the transcendentalism has largely disappeared, and into which all sorts of elements, from here, there and everywhere, have been introduced. Hinduism, as has been tersely said is Brahminism run to seed", (ibid. p. 397).

The tirade and the silent crusade which Max Muller started against the Brahmin has gone on unabated during the past one hundred years. Even in modern times we hear its echo in very subdued form clothed in polished language. The crusade against the Brahmins of India has gone well into the twentieth century — almost near to its close. Very few Indian seem to be knowing that more than one Anti-Brahmin movements in Andhra (old Hyderabad), in Karnataka (old Mysore) and in Tamil Nadu (old Madras) have been, and are being, regularly funded and subsidized by alien Christian mission agencies. In this connection it would indeed be very interesting to know that (Winston) Churchill too belonged to the anti-Brahmin brigade. He said: "They are making arrangements that the greatest bluff, the greatest humbug, and the greatest betrayal, shall be followed by the greatest ramp.

Nepotism, back scratching, graft and corruption in every form will be the handmaids of a Brahmin domination". (Speech in Royal Albert Hall, London, March 18,1931). Churchill did not stop there; he went a step further: "There can be no doubt, therefore, that the departure of the British from India, which Mr. Nehru demands, would be followed first by a struggle in the North and thereafter by reconquest of the South by the North and of the Hindus by the Muslims. This danger has not escaped the crafty foresight of the Brahmans. It is for this reason they want to have control of a British army, or failing that, a white army of janrusaries officered, as Mr. Gandhi has suggested, by German or other Europeans", (ibid).

At a meeting of the Ecumenical Study Conference for East Asia held in Lucknow in December 1952 a proposal was discussed and "approved" to issue an interpretative volume on "Christianity and the Asian Revolution". In 1954 the book made its appearance which advanced the view: "The standard of revolt is raised by the Dravida Kazhagam in South India , with open defiance and even some show of violence. The Kazhagam repudiates Hinduism because it perpetuates Brahmin supremacy and imposes an alien Aryan culture and Sanskritic traditions on the Tamils. The Kazhagam wants all the Tamil religious classics to be reinstated as the primary source of scriptural authority; it wants the Brahmin priests and their vedic rituals to be discarded; it urges that the entire fabric of contemporary society be completely changed so that social prestige, economic strength and political influence are taken away, if necessary by violent revolution, from Aryan Brahmins and given back to Dravidian non-Brahmins". (Christianity and the Asian Revolution ed. by Rajah B. Manikam Pub. The Joint East Asia Secretariat of the International Missionary Council and the The World Council of Churches, Funds for publication provided by the World Council of Churches).

Only a year earlier another book had made its appearance (the United States and India and Pakistan) in which its author, W. Norman Brown made a positively one sided remark when he said: "Aryan has now for three thousand years (the idea that the Aryans, their language and Dharma are only 3000 years old is borrowed directly from Max Muller-Br.D.Bh.) been encroaching upon the Dravidian". (p. 209).

Max Muller left more than one dangerous trails all of which were eagerly picked up by those Christian zealots and missionaries — secular as well as regular — who followed him. He had the peculiar knack of providing them with subtle and deceptively veiled suggestions which guided their minds into desired grooves. Max Muller was engaged on the work of translating the Veda from Vedic-Sanskrita into English. As such he had apparently nothing to do with politics. Nevertheless, the adroit diplomatist and courtier that he was he knew how to twist politics to make it look something out of this field. While writing to Protap Chunder Mozoomdar he did exactly what only a hardboiled politician would do to advance his long range political interests. He told Mozoomdar with his tongue in his cheek: "Of course I have been abused by the Indian papers, and by the journals in England. Let me answer one question in your very kind letter. The name to be adopted by your own reformed Hinduism as a religion would mean the religion of the Hindus or India, and this would comprise any variety of religion practised in India, Durgaworship, Buddhism, Mohammadanism &c. It would be the name of a mere congeries. You object to anything like Christian, even Christian Brahmos is not satisfactory to you. . . only I thought that truth and gratitude would declare in favour of Christian Brahmos or Christian Aryas".

When Max Muller wrote this epistle to Mozoomdar he did not have in his mind only the Hindus but members of all religions in India —the geographical India — the country India. He used, therefore the expression India would be the name of a mere congeries. What is congeries? The Concise Oxford Dictionary of Current English defines congeries to mean" collection, mass, heap".
