In Max Muller's estimation India was a heap which did not matter; India was a mass which did not count and India was collection of followers of so many faiths which carried no weight! And he thought no one need take any notice of this congeries. The contempt he carried deep down in his heart for India reflected his scholarly feeling towards this heap. End sanctifies the means is an important edict of Society of Jesus whose members proudly used to flaunt the letters S.J. after their names till about ten years ago. Max Muller though he was not a Jesuit was nevertheless in full sympathy with the-end-sanctifies-the-means dogma. He had an end in mind which was to denounce and denigrate the Veda, the Vedic Dharma and destroy the Hindus by converting them to Christianity. He had chosen a contemptible end to be achieved and since the end sanctified the means the contemptuous scholar in him chose, without any compunction, contemptible means to achieve the end. The hint thrown by him that India was a mere congeries was assiduously picked up by Christian writers, historians and politicians alike and they soon started barking up the wrong tree that India was not a nation! The Christian missionaries soon joined this brigade and India is not, and was not, a nation soon became their battle cry.

"There are two commonplaces in the discussion of Indian problems, upon which, though much has been said, much remains to be said. The one is that general observation upon India that are invariably mistaken, because India is a continent rather than a single country; . . ." (Problems of Greater Britain by Sir Charles Wentworth Dilke p. 403).

". . . . for even the rulers of the native states are in most cases foreigners...." (ibid. 404).

"Practically, British India now consists of nine great provinces or, in reality, different countries : Bombay, Madras, Bengal the United Provinces of Agra and Oudh, the Punjab, Burmah, Eastern Bengal and Assam, the Central Provinces and finally the North West Frontier Province. The fact that the first two of these are administered by governors, the next five by lieutenant governors, and the last two by chief commissioners, must not conceal from us that here are a set of what may be termed separate aations in embryo." (The Future of England by Hon. George Peel, p. 221, emphasis added).

"Therefore, when we are justly told, by some who know, that India is not, never will be, a nation, and that she consists of many nations at eternal variance with one another and parted by the profound abysses of race, creed, language and history, we can assent." (ibid, emphasis added).

"I have spoken of the different countries of India, but they are not countries in the ordinary European sense." (India, Its Administration and Progress by John Stratchey, p.4. Sir Alfred Loyal in \\i\\& Asiatic Studies was of similar opinion).

". . . . It may sound a paradox, but it is British rule and the English language alone that have suggested the idea of Indian nationality. ... I come back to the question: Is there any reason why there should be an Indian nation?" (The Dilemma in India by Reginald Graddock, p. 2).

"India, apart from British rule in it, is simply a geographical expression - a large piece of the earth cut off by high mountains from the rest of Asia, and conveniently spoken of under one name - peopled by tribes of different tongues, different origins, different religions, constantly (in old times) at war with each other, an aggregation of the least similar constituents that were ever crowded together on the face of the earth". (The Growth of the Empire by Arthur W. Jose, p. 5).

"If intelligent men in England would make themselves acquainted with "the real truth of things," they would understand how purely imaginary is that "Indian nation" and that "people of India" of which we hear so much". (India, Its Administration and Progress by John Stratchey, p. 501).

". . . the fundamental fact is that India had no jealousy of the foreigner, because there was no India, and therefore, properly speaking, no foreigners". (The Expansion of England by Prof. Seeley, p. 206).

In his letter to Mozoomdar Max Muller had made yet another point. He had suggested to Mozoomdar to adopt the name either of "Christian Brahmos or Christian Aryas"; he had not approved nor suggested Brahmo Christians nor Arya Christians. This is exactly what has been happening in India. The Indian Christians are asked by the Church to call themselves Christian Indians and not Indian Christians and for reasons known only to the converts they find it difficult to resist the demand made by the Church!

The converts to Islam too call themselves Muslim Indian and not Indian Muslims.

Since Max Muller was a secular Christian missionary he was neither under any vows nor was he attached to any religious Order. He was not a Jesuit but in practice he was, in many respects, as good as, or better than, many a Jesuit when it came to practise prevarication, equivocation or simulation. None the less he was a genius though only an evil one. He wanted to denigrate the Veda and its teachings. In order to achieve his end he often put wrong, misleading and at times wilfully mischievous interpretations of Veda mantras. Most of the muck that he passed on as translation was, however, in most cases, the result of his incompetence and ignorance otSanskrita language and its grammar. To this extent he might easily be absolved of the responsibility of having distorted these meanings. Nevertheless, he knew it too well that he and no matter who and how many more Christian missionaries, as crafty as he himself or even more cunning than he had been, came and tried to destroy the Truth enshrined in the Veda texts they shall not succeed. The Truth shall prevail and was sure to be re-discovered. What was the way out? he argued in his mind. He came to the conclusion that the Vedas could be destroyed if only the Hindus, who followed Vedic Dharma, could be eliminated, and this could be done by converting them to Christianity. He further argued in his mind that if Asia was to be christianized the Hindus of India must first be made to accept the religion of the Bible. His mind thus made up he wrote in one of his letters (August 25, 1856) to Bunsen:" "Whatever finds root in India soon overshadows the whole of Asia, and nowhere could the vital power of Christianity more gloriously realize itself than if the world saw it spring up there for a second time, in a very different form than in the West, but, still essentially the same".

Max Muller thus believed that if Asia was to be converted to Christianity it was necessary first to convert India — the Hindus. This Maxmullerian hypothesis has been echoed extremely faithfully and diligently by more than one Christian missionaries working in India for collecting harvests of converts. Some of them have been held in great esteem in missionary circles. The Church and her missionaries have sincerely, and perhaps too honestly, believed that unless Christianity succeeded in converting the Hindus of India it stood hardly any chance of coverting other nations of Asia. As a result the Church and her hordes have since then fully recognized the paramount importance of India as a mission land. Some of them so dutifully have echoed the words of Max Muller that they really look as his minions. One of them, E. Stanley Jones, while writing the Foreword for a book written by a Christian professor said inter alia: "India is important — very. She is the key to the whole non-communist Asia. For whichever way India goes the whole non-communist Asia goes. India has risen to leadership in Asia in a very remarkable way. Hundreds of millions will follow her lead. . . I commend this book to those who want to understand one of the most important spots of the world — India, and one of the most important things in India — the Christian movement". In all fairness E. Stanley Jones cannot be accused of indulging in the dirty and unethical sport of plagiarism. Certainly he was not. He was only repeating like a parrot what the ace Christian missionary of the nineteenth century had said and laid down as a guideline for those who were yet to come after him.

E. Stanley Jones was not the only Christian missionary who battologized Max Muller's thoughts on the subject. There were others who repeated their master's voice. In 1963, a year before the 38th Eucharistic Congress was held in Bombay one Fr. Tempra of the Pious Society of St. Paul (Bombay) went to America to collect funds (dollars) to finance the work of proselytization of the Hindus in India. He carried with him an undated letter of introduction and recommendation from Cardinal Valerian Gracias. While in the USA Fr. Tempra gave an interview to the Catholic Home Messenger during the course of which he too repeated Max Muller's arguments. This interview, though it was kept a secret in India, was given good publicity in the USA and in Canada. Its copies were freely distributed among the likely donors along with a 4-page leaflet, India For Christ. This leaflet carried inter alia a message which was only a 1963-version of what Max Muller had written to Bunsen in 1856. Fr.

Tempra pleaded: "The people of India, a great people, may easily set the direction of humanity in the future. For India is strong of almost 400 million souls (he seemed to be counting only the Hindus! — Br. D.Bh.) in search of God and progress. If the people of India do not find God (Christian God — Br.D.Bh.) now, the free world will have lost a formidable ally, and 400 million more souls may fall under domination of Communism".

The Christian Church and mission agencies have been working in India for more than 300 years and have used every means and artifice to convert the Hindus to Christianity. They have met, however, with only little success. Among the missionaries who have acted as guides for others the names of Abbe Dubois from France and Max Muller from Germany stand out prominently. While the former was a regular missionary (priest) and a Jesuit the latter was only secular who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of scholarship. Both have left behind their writings which if pursued with care would reveal to any impartial student of Christian missionizing efforts in India the painful truth that both failed in their mission. While Dubois admitted his failure Max Muller was more hopeful that his dream of the Hindus being converted to the religion of Christ would come true if not in his lifetime assuredly after he had left for heavens.

Abbe (JA.) Dubois was a Jesuit and a fugitive from France and he laboured hard for more than thirty years in and around Mysore state (now Karnataka) but was not able to win over the Hindus to Christianity. He left behind a long narrative of his labours in a book called Hindu, Manners, Customs and Ceremonies which was first published in 1817 by the East India Company. In this edition appeared the following passage:

"But, degraded as the Hindu women are in private life, it must be admitted that they receive the highest respect in public. They certainly do not pay them flat and frivolous compliments which are used among us, and which are the disgrace of both sexes; but on the other hand, they have no insults to dread. A woman may go wheresoever she pleases; she may walk in the most public places (must I except those where the Europeans abound?) and have nothing to fear from libertines numerous as they are in the country. A man who should stop to gaze on a woman in the street, or elsewhere, would will invariably be hooted as an insolent and most lowbread fellow". (Hindu, Manners, Customs and Ceremonies, p. 220,1817 edn.).

A missionary would as easily call white black if he could be sure that by so doing he would be serving mother Church and that he might save some souls! In the true missionary tradition the above passage was suitably altered and edited when 1897 third edition was printed by Clarendon Press, Oxford. The uncomplimentary reflections on contemporary European manners were omitted.

What was the purpose of writing Hindu, Manners, Customs and Ceremonies? Abbe Dubois himself explained:

"There is one motive which above all others has influenced my determination. It struck me that a faithful picture of the wickedness and incongruities of polytheism and idolatry would by its very ugliness help greatly to set off the beauties and perfections of Christianity. It was thus that the Lacedaemonians placed drunken slaves in the sight of their children in order to inspire the latter with a horror of intemperance". And "Nevertheless I am persuaded that the notes which I have taken so much trouble to collect will afford some useful material to others more favourably situated than myself. (Author's Preface, p. 9,1897 edn.).

There is a yet one more startling fact about Hindu, Manners, Customs and Ceremonies which many people in this land (Hindustan) may not be knowing. The third edition of the year 1897 carries a PREFATORY NOTE to the book of Dubois by another Christian missionary who is none other than Max Muller the scholar extraordinary. As often happens with Christian missionaries it is nearly always a matter of you-scratch-my-back-I-scratch-your. Max Muller recommended the book of Dubois to missionaries in particular saying: "As the Abbe went to India as a missionary, and was a man remarkably free from theological prejudices (knowledgeable people know this too well! - Br.D.Bh.) missionaries in particular (yet to come-Br.D.Bh.) will read this volume with interest and real advantage". (Prefatory Note, p. vii, 1897 edn, emphasis added).

Dubois and Max Muller did their best by their writings to influence and direct the thought currents of the Hindus into Christian grooves. Their books became text books for younger missionaries aspiring to go to India to make it their field for Christian labours. But, alas as they both failed they must now be turning in their graves very uneasily indeed.

## Epilogue

An average Christian missionary is very vainglorious. Whenever he finds himself on good wicket he becomes impulsively overbearing. If ever he happens to be in a tight corner or in a company not sympathetically disposed towards him or his mission he at once becomes docile and circumspect. In the words of a Christian historian known for his independent views "the chief characteristic of the missionaries is the love of maximising and belauding all their own efforts, and in order to secure the advantages of their position". This is not all. They, nearly all of them, take pleasure in preaching good morals and good ethics but when judged by their own standards many a missionary among them would often be found wanting. They would take meticulous care to hide and cover up one anothers' shortcomings. None the less, their zeal for converting people of other religious faiths to Christianity remains uppermost in their minds. And Max Muller was no exception.

Max Muller cannot be said to have been a regular missionary like Nobili, Dubois and Carey. He had as his main aim of life the conversions of the Hindus of India and to achieve this Christian goal he chose for himself the field of religious literature as his main activity. Only for this reason his second biographer, Nirad C. Chaudhuri, called him a scholar extraordinary. Another writer described him as the "profoundest Vedic scholar. Though he was neither he earned for himself these highly laudable and praiseworthy appellations in spite of the fact that he himself admitted more than once that he did not know and understand Samkrita acclaimed by scholars of world renown as the mother of all other languages. All this not withstanding he had been busily engaged, though only from behind the scene, in the missionizing work of evangelizing and proselytizing the Hindus of India. That he was so engaged remained a near secret during his lifetime but as soon as his first biography, The Life and Letters of The Rt. Honourable Friedrick Max Muller (in 2 volumes) edited by Georgina Max Muller, his widow, became available Max Muller the man came out from behind Max Muller the scholar. He was discovered.

The title page of the New York edition of The Life and Letters, carries a quotation from Carlyle: "Blessed is he who finds his work: let him ask no other blessedness". When the Board of Directors of the East India Company agreed (1847) to hire Max Muller for translating the Rigveda from Vedic-Sanskrita into English Max Muller had been assured of his English bread and butter for the next eight years. He no longer faced the problem of somehow making both ends meet and finding for himself where withal for two square meals a day. But this was not much of a blessing; the greater blessing that came his way was the chance to uproot Hinduism. He was thus doubly blessed because he had found his work.

From the very start Max Muller thought it was not much of a task to convert the Hindus of India to Christianity. In his letter to (Chevalier) Bunsen (dated, Sunday, July 1850) he said inter alia (in translation): "I have no doubt whatever, that something can be written about the Veda which would reach even the dullest ears. Whether Dr. Wilson (Wilson then occupied the Boden Chair of Sanskrit at Oxford - Br. D. Bh.) can undertake that task is another question. ... 7 of course shall be glad if the Rigveda is dealt with in the Edinburgh Review, and if Wilson (Wilson was 57 years senior to Max Muller - Br. D. Bh.) would write from the standpoint of a missionary, and would show how the knowledge and bringing into light of the Veda would upset the existing system of Indian theology it become of real interest". (The Life and Letters, Vol. I, p. 117, New York Edn.). Max Muller had wanted Wilson to write something about the Veda from the standpoint of a Christian missionary. No one can deny that the Christian missionary view point was only one: to uproot Hinduism. Max Muller himself did not want to do this because he was not prepared, at this point of time, to divulge the closely guarded secret that behind the facade of a scholar lay hidden a Christian missionary!

In another letter to Bunsen (August 25, 1856) Max Muller showed up himself nearly completely. He said (in translation) inter alia: "After the last annexation the territorial conquest of India ceases — what follows next is the struggle in the realm of religion and of spirit, in which, of course, centre the interests of the nations. India is ripe for Christianity more than Rome and Greece were at the time of St. Paul. The rotten tree (of Hinduism - Br. D. Bh.) has for some time had artificial supports, because its fall would have been inconvenient for the government. But if the Englishman comes to see that the tree must fall, sooner or later, then the thing is done, and he will mind no sacrifice either of blood or of land. For the good of this struggle I should like to lay down my life, or at least to lend my hand to bring about this struggle". (The Life and Letters, Vol. I, p. 191, New York Edn.) Like a true fanatic Max Muller was prepared to lay down his life in the struggle for bringing down the tree of Hinduism. There can be no greater proof to establish the painful truth that he was only a hardboiled Christian missionary masquerading from behind the mask of Sanskrit scholarship.

There is sufficient evidence available in the pages of Max Mullet's biography to prove to the hilt that the conversion of the Hindus of India had been his life's ambition. A friend of Max Muller, a Vicar, had sent a letter to Oxford Times in which the Vicar had freely and without any hesitation confirmed that Max Muller had been actively and intimately interested in the conversion of the higher caste Hindus. The letter ran: "For many years the conversion of the enlightened classes of India has interested the Professor; during the last few months since his illness, it seems to be absorbing more and more of his thoughts. . . ." Truth is often stranger than fiction. Whatever Max Mullet's supporters now say to absolve him of the charge that he masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the mask of scholarship merely iooks like bland fiction. There is thus nothing to support the claim so facetiously made more than 80 years after Max Mullet's death that "Max Muller of 1898/99 had moved a long way from the position which he took when he wrote to Baron Bunsen in the fifties, to Lord Argyll in the sixties, and when he lectured at Westiminister Abbey on "Missions" in 1873". In fact the evidence on record goes to prove that after the first four decades of his (working) life i.e. after 1887 (he was engaged by the East India Company in 1847) he had been more active in missionizing activities.

The Church and the mission agencies nearly always assign particular jobs to each missionary. As Adam working with the Serampore Mission had been given the responsibility of bringing Rammohun Roy to Christ so had Max Muller taken upon himself the responsibility of converting only the leaders of the Hindus. Being a litterateur Max Muller operated on the intellectual front and as a master missionary he used ingenious baits to hook Indian converts from among the enlightened classes of India. As an ace evangelist he was not interested in expending his time and energy on smaller fish -individual Hindus of no consequence; he aimed much higher at those who after they would have been converted might bring their followers with them. He was more interested in influencing and converting leaders believing that once he succeeded to have them on his side their herds would automatically follow their herdsman. To achieve this end Max Muller would often simulate great friendship with the leaders to win their trust, confidence and esteem while behind the scene he would carry intelligence and recommend all likely prize converts to such of his English masters and patrons who would by virtue of the official position they held could bestow favours in case they showed inclination to renounce their ancestral religion and accept Jesus Christ as their personal Saviour.

It would be very wrong, however, to conclude that the Englishmen whom Max Muller approached were really, in all cases, his friends. Max Muller seems to have not understood a basic trait of English character that an Englishman never tries to purchase friendship and the English man did not accept Max Muller as a friend for the simple reason that he had paid Max Muller his price! In fact, they used Max Muller only as a lever to achieve their own theo-political ends hoping, in the process, to kill two birds with one stone. Once Max Muller wrote to Prime Minister, Gladstone, (1882) that he wished that Mozoomdar could have seen him because "he is the right hand of Keshub Chunder Sen . . . because he thought they are working in the right direction, and it is pleasure to help them in ploughing, sowing and watering, though we can never hope to see the harvest".7 His fears turned out to be true. He was not able to gather any harvest in spite of the time, energy and money that he so assiduously and unnoticed put in to prepare the soil in India for the conversion of the Brahmos.

Max Muller was particularly interested in the conversion of Keshub Chunder Sen.8 He wanted Sen to declare himself a Christian because at that time Sen commanded considerable following among the intellectuals in Calcutta. He recommended Sen, without Sen ever coming to know of it, to more than one Englishmen in India who were equally interested in converting the Hindus of India to Christianity. When Sen was still only about 30 years young he was invited by Lord Lawrence to Simla which undoubtedly was a singular gesture of hospitality on the part of a tyrant. This was intended, however, to raise unparalleled hopes in the young bosom of a political slave suffering from unbounded ambition to make a mark and leave some footprints behind. About two years after his sojourn in Simla Keshub Chunder Sen reached London where Max Muller and some other Christian missionaries took charge of him to nurture him as a future convert.

Max Muller had his own supporters and admirers. Even in the face of incontrovertible evidence one of his supporters, J.H. Voight, an indologist from Germany, quite cheekily dared not only to give a good chit to Max Muller the scholar extraordinary, but also chastise Max Muller's critics. While holding brief on behalf of Max Muller he said inter alia: "There have been many divergent views on Max Muller's religious faith. He has been accused of having supported the idea of Christianizing India and even of having striven hard and planned meticulously to kill Hindu Dharma and Sanskrit. On the other hand Max Muller was praised for having embraced Hinduism, though not by conversion but by actually professing. . . . None of these views is correct. The first one is, moreover, mischievious in character."

The apology offered, more than 67 years after Max Muller's death, to absolve him of the charge that he had been engaged in missionizing activities all his lifetime, has no legs to stand upon unless one is willing to be cajoled into believing that Max Muller did not take part in the work of missionizing in the later part of his life. When Max Muller's first biography (The Life and Letters) became available it was the first time that Max Muller had been discovered as a man though all his lifetime he masqueraded as a scholar! His biographer, Georgina Max Muller, his widow, made public the letters which Max Muller had written to Mozoomdar less than about eight months before his death. These are the letters to which the Vicar, Max Muller's friend, had referred in proof of Max Muller's zeal for tile-work of missionizing till the last days of his life.

Ift-the earlier stages of his life Max Muller was naturally very cautious. He did not want to be discovered and branded another Nobili. Therefore, he continued to move stealthily measuring his every step wjth care and caution. But since by the years 1898/99, after four decades of practical experience, he had grown not only immensely more confident but also financially independent he was able to afford to show up himself without running the risk of being hooted down or stigmatized as apadri. To this part of his life belong his concerted efforts to convert Protap Chunder Mozoomdar to the religion of the Bible.

There is more evidence available in the pages of The Life and Letters to substantiate the charge that Max Muller in truth was a secular Christian missionary. "The Enquirer, another missionary paper, also spoke of Max Muller as coming to the aid of the missionaries and making his own characteristic contribution to mission work."

The Life and Letters, Vol. II, pp. 125-6, New York edn, emphasis added.

"Harvest" is a special phrase which in the missionary parlance means "gathering converts". It had received Mahatma Gandhi's attention who once said: "Conversion now-a-days has become a matter of business, like any other. I remember having read a missionary report how much it cost per head to convert and then presenting a budget for "the next harvest". (Young India, April 23,1931.b. 1838- d. 1884.

That Max Muller was interested and actively engaged in the work of religious conversions from the earlier years of his life was confirmed by Dr. Pusey in his letter to him: "Your work will form a new era with efforts for the conversion of India and Oxford will have reason to be thankful that, by giving you a home it will have facilitated a work of such primary and lasting importance for the conversion of India... .

It is very common for Christian missionaries to blow out of proportion the virtues of Christian religion. They gloat over Christian teachings and ethics so much that at times they make themselves a laughing stock of the intelligentsia. One of them who was more outspoken and whose mind had perhaps been deadened during his more impressionable years by sermons preached to him by someone like him quite brazenfacedly declared: "There is only one religion, which was revealed from God. Mohammadanism, Confuncianism, Judaism, Brahamanism (he meant Vedic Dharma of the Hindus -Br.D.Bh.), and others so called religions are the invention of man. One has come down from heavens; the others are of the earth, earthly. One is divine revelation from the creator of the universe, the other arc moral philosophy... . The Bible, the inspired word of God is the only necessary text book. It contains enough truth and knowledge to employ students during their lifetime, and it would be better for them to stick to it rather than waste their strength and time in the study of other creeds which can be of no use whatever to them. If theologians or students have curiosity to know what has been taught by impostors and the inventors of false religions, they can do so in connection with the department of history or philosophy." Another Christian missionary called Christianity an "unique religion".

What did make Christianity an unique religion? The answer is given by another Christian authority: "A great obstacle to the important understanding of Christianity, its origin, and the conditions influencing its survival is that it is the official creed of a large part of the ruling races of the world. Every professing Christian is eager to apply methods of scientific criticism to other religions, living or dead, but shrinks from a like treatment of his own creed". He (Yearsley) has not been the only scholar to express his honest views on Christianity and on the Christian scriptures. There have been several others who unlike Max Muller were neither committed nor commissioned and subsidized to sing praises of the Bible and its religion.

"Which is the worse, the Old or the New Testament? If bad books were burned, the largest bonfire should consist of the Bibles."

— Charles Smith

"I do not believe telling lies for the glory of God.....The Bible

itself destroys the claim to God's omnipotence."

— Rupert Hughes "The Bible is hopelessly pre-evolutionary."

— George Bernard Shaw

"I know of no other book (barring the Bible) that so fully teaches the subjection and degradation of women."

— Elizabeth Cady Stanton

"The real oppressor, enslaver and corrupter of the people is the Bible."

— Col. Robert Ingersol

"Philosophically there was nothing extraordinary in Christian principles. ... It is impossible for me to regard Christianity as a perfect religion or the greatest of all religions."

— Mahatma Gandhi

"The greatest evil in the world today is the Roman Catholic Church.”

-H.G. Wells

"The incomprehensible jargon of Trinitarian Arithmetic, that three are one and one is three...."

Tliomas Jafferson, one of the Founders of the USA "I call Christianity the one Great Curse, the enormous and innermost perversion, the one great instinct of revenge, for which no means are too venomous, too underhand, and too petty — I call it the one immortal blemish of mankind."

— Nietzsche, German Philosopher

"Really no religion has ever preached things so evidently incompatible with contemporary knowledge or so immoral as the doctrines preached by Church Christianity . . . what can be more absurd than that the mother of God was both a mother and a virgin...."

— Count Tolstoy

Yearsley told only half the story when he said that Christianity owed its present position only to the fact that it happened to be the official creed of the ruling races of the world. Here a rider might safely be added that Christianity owed its present position only to the fact that it has been the official creed of the coloniser races who before they founded colonies had been political brutes but later, after they had consolidated their political power, they turned religious beasts! The inhuman torture and humiliating punishments the Portuguese colonizers, who were the more zealous and pious Christians, inflicted on innocent Hindu men, women and children in Goa, during the period of the Inquisition supervised and directed by Saint (!) Francis Xavier, is well known to all students of religious history. This was not all. The followers of the unique religion often bought their converts; they gave monetary inducements to the unfortunate and the less affluent to accept Christian religion and the result has been that no one with some means or intelligence willingly agreed to join the ranks of the followers of the only true religion of the world!

The Examiner, the official mouthpiece of the Roman Catholic Church in India, lamented:

"Conversions in the last four centuries have almost been completely from the Scheduled Castes and Tribes and from

Communities not belonging to the Brahman or the Kshatriya or the Vaisya Community."

Most of these conversions had been brought about by offering monetary inducements to converts. The practice of offering inducements to converts in order to entice the needy people, ignorant and the gullible, had been practised from the very beginning. We have an account of this in some old records of the East India Company. In February 1659, writing to Madras the Court stated:

"It has pleased a worthy member of our society, Mr. Thomas Rich, to present us with six volumes of books, containing the Old and New Testament in several languages, it being his desire that they may be sent to remain in your factories, hoping it may be a means to propagate and spread the Gospel in those parts, which is his and our earnest desire by all means possible to advance and further. We have, therefore, laden them on the ship Smyrna Merchant, and recommended them (as a choice gift) unto you, to be carefully preserved and made use of for that intent and purpose for which they are designed.

Another letter was sent by the Court to the East India Company in 1663 to Madras (Fort St. George) saying:

"We now send you a supply of one hundred Bibles and two hundred catechisms, which we would have you dispose of there and at our subordinate factories, to such as you think will make the best use of them; and when any shall be able to repeat the catechism by heart, you may give to each of them two rupees for their encouragement... ,"

The efforts to convert the Hindus of India to the religion of the Bible had been made even before Max Muller came on the scene but it was Max Muller who gave the whole movement a look of respectable credibility. He decided directly to attack the brain of the Hindu by misrepresenting according to a carefully planned design the Hindu Dharma Shastras. The intellectual approach which Max Muller made though it had dangerous potentialities failed to achieve its Christian purpose. Whatever little success it achieved was mostly due to the cruelties perpetrated by the Christian ruling classes, or the subtle deceipt practised by Christian missionaries and in greater part by the irreligious (though not un Christian!) use of monetary inducements offered by the Church and the Christian rulers to people suffering in penury.

Nevertheless, in final analysis the Hindu, Hinduism and the Hindu Cause suffered more by default on the part of the Hindus themselves than by the combined efforts of the theo-political intrigues working against them. The Hindus have been too complacent and tolerant to a fault. The intelligent class among the Hindus failed to rise to the occasion and tell the Hindu masses that the coin which the Church and the horde of her missionaries had been endeavouring to put into circulation was counterfeit.

The efforts to convert the Hindu to Christianity are still going on but in more sophisticated manner. The Christian missionary and the servants of Lord Jesus Christ though have become more circumspect they still fail to heed the warning given by Hon. George Peel:

"Beyond this the thinker may ponder over a still more audacious hypothesis. If we kill their (of the Hindus — Br.D.Bh.) faith, they may try to kill our faith too. They will have no Metz." They will sally out for a religious sortie in force. They will argue that this one life on which, in our view, all our future status hinges, is, to them, more scientifically, but a link in the long chain. We desire consciousness; they would be rid of it. We presuppose, they deny, the soul. We anchor ourselves on a Personal Godhead; to them such personality of the divine is the most repugnant of heresies. As for immortal life, they draw back with horror from its texture in its bliss. Such is the arch of meditation through which they would have us gaze on the vista of reality. Such is the height, terraced by wisdom, or by folly, which they would have us tread. Such is the forbidden opium of metaphysics which they will export West."

In the present scenario the Church and her emissaries seem also to fail to understand the significance of the biblical warning (Mt. XXVL52) "for all they that take the sword shall perish with the sword" and also that those who try to live by deceipt shall also perish by deceipt. The final outcome of the struggle between Christianity and Hinduism (the religion of the Veda) is still hidden in the womb of time. The Hindu giant has already begun to turn up. Things are bound to be very much different when the giant is fully awake and back on his feet. The giant may not only try to kill the faith of the jannisaries who have often been after the blood of Hinduism the giant may actually kill their faith and Christianity may meet its Waterloo in Hindustan — the land of the Veda. The world will wait and watch the outcome with bated breath.

## Appendices

Robert de Nobili, The Jesuit Missionary

Max Muller described Robert de Nobili as "the first European Sanskrit scholar." In the year 1973, National Herald carried a serialized article, Society of Jesus, in its three issues dated July 22, 1973; July 29, 1973 and August 5, 1973 by Fr. T A. Mathias with the ostensible purpose of lauding and applauding some of the past members of the Society of Jesus to which the author himself belonged. Among those who received special commendation was Robert de Nobili. The author of the article said in the course of his narrative:

"The second episode is the extraordinary initiative taken by Robert de Nobili in Madurai, de Nobili was one of the greatest Christian missionaries who ever lived. The significance of his work is that it marks a radical departure from missionary methods followed in India for the previous 100 years. ... He decided to strip Christ's message of its Western trappings and to clothe it in Indian form, using Indian custom and language in worship. . . . He further completely adopted the Brahman priests' way of life, became a vegetarian, used no furniture, shaved his head, dressed in an ochre robe, wore a scared thread (with a cross at the end of it) and all the other signs of a sanyasi. . . . Even till this day de Nobili's example exerts a fascination for Jesuits and so it is no surprising that at least three Indian Jesuits live today like Hindu sadhus and call themselves names like "Swami-Animananda."

The one sided remarks of Mathias attracted adverse criticism from some non-Christian readers among whom happened to be the present author. Among the several observations made by these critics the most important was the one which squarely accused Nobili for having forged a Veda with the clandestine purpose and intention of misleading the Hindus of Madurai into accepting Christianity. This particular piece of criticism which exposed Nobili as an impostor provoked one S.G. Mamp.lli to send in a rejoinder to the newspaper making a far fetched claim that there was no historical truth behind the accusation made against Nobili.

Fr. TA. Mathias had tried hard to rehabilitate Nobili as the greatest or at least as one of the greatest Christian missionaries who had ever lived and worked in India in the service of Christianity and Lord Jesus Christ. In his sincere endeavours to make Nobili look like an honest Christian he very discreetly introduced the eight words "with a cross at the end of it" in parenthesis. By this device he tried, and hoped, though in vain, to be able to establish the dubious reputation of missionary Nobili but the fact remains that the interpolation remained transparently too clear. Nobili had been engaged on a secret mission and had taken great pains on that account to hide his true identity. He was neither so naive nor so stupid as knowingly to dangle a cross, the unmistakable Christian symbol, at the end of the sacred thread which he schemingly sported to establish his identity as a Hindu. He would do nothing that might give him away to the very people whom he had been trying to fool and deceive. This apart, no other historian has ever been in agreement with Fr. TA. Mathias in his novel assertion that Nobili carried a cross around his neck at the end of the sacred thread.

Nobili was a "nephew of Cardinal Bellarmine and nearly related to Pope Marcellus II." He went to India in 1605 with the one desire to convert as many Hindus as possible to Christianity. Nobili settled down at Madurai which at that time was a great seat of Hindu learning and pilgrimage. In the year 1606 Nobili was appointed to work with the Roman Catholic Missionaries who were then collectively known as Madurai Mission especially engaged in the work of converting Brahmins and other high-caste Hindus of Madurai to Christianity. Nobili was soon made the head of the Mission.

Nobili immediately applied his mind to the problem facing the Mission and approached it with the precise preparation which always precedes a well thought out plan of political subversion and espionage. He had a problem on hand: "How can Christianity be brought within the reach of the people of India independent of efforts after territorial aggrandisement? How can it be so presented to them so that they may be in a position to examine it objectively to accept it for its own sake?"

He argued in his mind and seemed to have arrived at a theoretically correct answer: "The missionary must be, as Paul said, an Indian to the Indian," and he determined to follow up this path in two directions. "On the one hand he would severe all connections with the Portuguese and on the other in all the concerns of his life he would endeavour to appear purely and simply a native of India." In this manner Nobili went about laying down the foundations of the greatest literary fraud ever committed in the annals of sacred literature and of which the Christian Church often speaks in laudatory language whenever her spokesmen find it possible to get away with it.

It is not uncommon with Christian writers and historians to blow up out of proportions the achievements of the Christian Church and her missionaries and simultaneously to endeavour to cover up their misdeeds. Some history books written by Christian writers seldom make mention of the Inquisition in Goa (India) which killed thousands of innocent Hindu men, women and children only because they did not agree to accept Christ as their personal saviour. Likewise, Christian historians, nearly all of whom are either commissioned or subsidized in one or the other way, have tried again and again to cover the black deed of Robert de Nobili. However, to their great dismay and discomfiture the truth has kept coming out into the open. Whenever the spokesmen of the Church have been confronted with the truth they invariably have chosen to take refuge under the one convenient plea that the Nobili case has no historical truth behind it.

Nobili had undoubtedly heard of the methods followed by Matthew Ricci in China. He decided to experiment on the same lines and to carry them even further." What had Ricci done to win over the Chinese to Christianity? "In 1582, Ricci, an Italian Jesuit and an eminent mathematician. . . convinced as he was that influence throughout China could only radiate from Peking, he reached the Chinese capital in 1595. Unsuccessful at first he slowly won his way to favour at the court by making presents of chiming clocks and other scientific toys. . . early in Chinese studies Ricci had detected the conflict between Buddhism and Confucianism. Realising that the greatest obstacle to Christianity was Buddhism, he sided with the Confucians and attacked the Buddhists. He quoted from the Confucian texts in support of the Christian doctrines and tried to show that Confucian doctrines did not conflict with Christianity." Taking his cue from Ricci he (Nobili) made up his mind to try to show to the Hindus (Brahmans) of Madurai that Christianity was not in conflict with their Hinduism. And in order to achieve this end he decided to palm off to the gullible and the non-suspecting Hindus a forged Veda contents of which he had very carefully interspersed with Christian dogmas.

The story of this gigantic literary fraud is told in a small book of 72 pages (History of the Jesuit Mission in Madurai South India in the Seventeenth, and the Eighteenth Centuries by J.S. Chandler M.A., published by M.E. Publishing House, Madras). In his Preface the author of the book mentioned sources from which he got his materials. According to him:

"The materials from which this book has been made are largely from five volumes of a work entitled "Letters from Jesuits in

Madura in Latin, Portuguese and Italian translated into French in 1848."

Again:"The history is remarkable one and it is due to the missionaries of that Madurai Mission that we let them tell their own story."

Again the author goes on:

"My grateful acknowledgements are due to Father Pages of the

Society of Jesus for kindly lending me the volumes of letters, now out of print and difficult to obtain; also to Dr. Harriet F. Farther of the American mission for bringing her excellent knowledge of French to my aid in rendering to me the historical portions of the letters."

The author of the History of the Jesuit Mission in Madurai made mention of a letter from Loerzio, a co-worker of Nobili, who quoted Nobili having said: "I also will make myself Indian to save Indians." This was in accordance with the advice given by Paul to Christians that they should be all things to all men. In pursuit of his desire to save Indians, as if it was a duty placed on his shoulders by God, Nobili went about with his great exercise in impersonation.

Nobili had tried and he seems to have succeeded in creating a favourable impression on the aristocracy in Madurai. As a result he was helped by some local people. For example: "He was aided in this plan by a gift of land in the Brahman quarter from the chieftain Erumeikathi. On this land he built a church and presbytery. He affected a mysterious solitude, never leaving the house, and receiving visitors only with extreme reserve; "the true method," as his superior said, "of attracting all the world by curiosity." To callers a disciple would say, "The father is not visible, he; is praying, he is studying, he is meditating on the divine law." Only after a long time, and at the second or third attempt, was a visitor admitted."

In the same letter (November 20, 1609) Loerzio made another statement which proves that Nobili made greater use of Tamil than of Sanskrit. This gives a lie to the assertion made by Max Muller that Nobili was a great scholar of Sanskrit. Loerzio said: "The father charmed all by his conversation, by the purity and perfection of his use of high Tamil,... ,"

On page 13 of the book the author of History of the Jesuit Mission in Madurai... unfolds the central plan of the fraud which Nobili had perfected. He quotes a letter (December 24,1608):

"De Nobili's knowledge of the books of the Hindoos gave him great power. He found that they had four Vedas or laws, of which they taught only three; the fourth was nearly lost, though in a manner combined with the other three. The lost Veda, he told them, was necessary for the salvation of the soul, but they had never found a man wise enough or holy enough to recover this lost law. He cited the very words of their sacred books to show that to save themselves they must listen to such a guru and learn the lost spiritual law, and thereby become his disciples. The custom allowed each to choose his own guru.

If in the modern age of secularism an independent and unattached psychiatrist were to probe and pry into the working of Nobili's mind he would no doubt be inclined to declare that Nobili, the Jesuit, either had criminalistic tendencies or he had suffered from some hallucinations. He might pronounce Nobili to have been a schizo. The moment Nobili came to know that one of the four Vedas was reported to have been nearly lost he at once saw a rare opportunity in this situation; he decided to recover this nearly lost Veda. He conjured up the possibility in his mind that if he could replace this lost Veda by a forged one he stood very good chance of being listened to and of succeeding in his mission of misleading the Hindus of Madura in the manner it suited his convenience. The ease and the speed with which he was able instinctively not only to judge and evaluate this possibility but also to take a decision clearly point to a mind that was infested with criminalistic propensities. He found it not at all difficult to come to a decision and he at once decided to turn this situation to his advantage. He launched out on the project to concoct a veda and then in due course and at an opportune time to pretend to have discovered the Veda that had been nearly lost. Later, he palmed off his forgery on the gullible Hindus of Madura claiming that he had recovered the lost law. Since he had wanted to make use of his forgery as a medium for propagating Christianity he very cleverly and cunningly interspersed and interpolated his forged veda with carefully picked up Christian dogmas.

Nobili tried his best to hoodwink and dupe the world in general and the Hindus of Madura in particular but his dirty experiment failed. The author of the History of the Jesuit Mission in Madura . . . again tells the tale. "The year 1610 was said to be rich in persecution. The assembling in Madurai of Parava and other low caste Christians from surrounding regions excited continual opposition. And the first storm was aroused by a Parava Christian from the coast, who mysteriously announced to the Christians of Madurai that by baptism they had fallen from their caste to that of the Parangi's and Paravans; for the sake of which result he declared that Nobili had put salt in their mouth and performed other ceremonies. He also declared that he Nobili was himself a Parangi.

"The Christians (the Hindus who had been converted by Nobili under deception — Br.D.Bh.) were thrown into consternation; they preferred death to the dishonour of being called Parangis; for in the eyes of the people generally a Parangi was a gross, ignorant person, incapable of any learning, especially philosophy.....ll

This was a signal to Nobili to get ready to wind up. His Christian bubble had been pricked and he quickly lost his calm exterior. Since he feared great harm might come to him he tried to make his position clear.

"Like Job, dc Nobili protested his innocence by a scared oath, on a palm leaf that some were spreading black columnies against him, and continued, "I am not a Parangi, and was not born on their soil, nor am I allied to their race. I was born in Rome; my family are of the rank of noble rajas in this country. The holy spiritual law does not oblige a man to renounce his caste. He who says this law is peculiar to Paravans or Parangis lies." This oath was pasted on the trunk of a great tree near de Nobili's house, and brought peace to the Christians; and the seceders all returned.

Robert de Nobili who had discovered the lost Veda had to flee Madurai to save his precious life. He died a blind man in Madras.

The forgery committed by Nobili and the manner in which he planned the whole operation has received the attention of more than one historians. One of them made the observation:

"Nobili withdrew himself from his colleague, Fernenddz, procured himself a private house . . . fitted it up so as to resemble in its minutest details the house of a Brahmin. He donned the light yellow robe of a Sanyasi (penetent) Brahmin, engaged Brahmans as his servants, and confined his menu to the vegetarian diet of the Brahmans. . . those who were baptized maintained all the forms and ceremonies of their old caste; they continued to wear the sacred thread, which Nobili himself now did, the only difference being that the Christian "sacred thread" consisted of three golden strands, symbolic of the Holy Trinity, and two silver ones, typifying the human and divine nature of Christ. But the uninitiated could not perceive the difference... .H

While engaged in this masterly act of novel impersonation Nobili had taken to serious study of Tamil, Telugu and Sanskrit. He declared himself a Brahmin and a guru from Italy and carried on his forehead the caste mark like the Brahmins of Madurai. Whenever there appeared any opposition to him "he mollified the opposition of influential Brahmins by means of bribes" which goes once again to prove the charge so often made against Christian missionaries that they too frequently offer material inducements to get converts.

There was opposition to Nobili from more than one quarters. Parava Christians opposed him perhaps most till one of them disclosed Nobili's closely guarded secret to some of his converts that "Nobili was really a Parangui." This nearly infuriated the people among whom he had been working surreptitiously to wreck their Dharma from within. "The resulting storm was violent in the extreme. ... In order to defend himself, he wrote a declaration in Tamil on olais, the strips of palm-leaf locally used in place of paper, and had it nailed to a tree in front of his house."

"I am no Parangui. I was neither born in their country, nor am I a member of their caste. In this God is my witness, and if I lie, I am willing, not only to be deemed a traitor to God and to be given over to the pains of hell hereafter, but also to suffer every conceivable chatisement in this world. I was born in Rorpe. My family there holds rank corresponding to that of the most distinguished rajahs over here. From my youth I have made choice of the calling of a Sannyasi; I have studied philosophy and the holy spiritual law. . . . The holy spiritual law which I proclaim obliges no man to renounce his caste or do anything incompatible with his caste-honour. This law which I proclaim has been preached in this very land by other men, Sannyasis and saints alike. Whoever maintains that this law is peculiar to the Paranguis or the Pariahs, commits a great sin; for, since God is Lord of all castes, His law must likewise be observed by all."

While Nobili had been learning Sanskrit and studying several Hindu texts he had busied himself in planning an ingenious forgery.

"He called himself a Rajah from Rome, a Guru or Teacher of Religion, a Sannyasi or Penitent, and from 1611 onwards, a Brahman to boot. He claimed to be the bringer of a fourth and lost Veda which he termed the spiritual law: this alone could impart eternal life. Its contents were partly interspersed among those of the other three Vedas; to a very great extent, however, they had been up to the present wholly lost; this lost Veda he now restored to the Hindus. To support this fiction he acquired with astounding industry a knowledge not only of Tamil and Telugu, the two languages principally spoken in Madura, but also of Sanskrit.

In this fashion the fourth veda handed down by Nobili the Jesuit to the Brahmins of Madura contained parts of texts culled from the three other Vedas with clever interspersions of Christian dogmas and doctrines from the Bible. And he declared with rare boldness that this was the lost Veda and he did all this in a manner which can be expected only from a master impostor.

There have been repeated attempts made in Christian quarters to water down the immensity of this great fraud and to make Nobili look innocent. More than one efforts have been made to re-open the question with a view mainly either to absolve Nobili of all responsibility or to simulate an exercise to determine whether what Nobili did, if he did it, was or was not a right thing for a Christian missionary to do. It has never been claimed, however, that he did not do what impartial, unattached, uncommitted and unsubsidized scholars and historians were wont to accuse him to have done. The evidence against Nobili is overwhelmingly conclusive.

By some clever Jesuitic manipulations a copy of this forgery was somehow made to reach Voltaire who mistook it to be genuine. W. Cooke Taylor discussed this episode in these words.

"The Jesuits tried to convert persons belonging to the most perfect order of the Brahmans, who are called the "Suniasi." Those zealous missionaries adopted the dress and manners of living of the Brahminical Sunniasi, that is, they wore orange-coloured clothes, and abstained from using as food, flesh, fish and eggs, and sustained the other painful observances as well as the hardships and austerities of the caste. They were obliged to classical Tamil. To these he was later able to add Te.ugu and Sanskrit." (A History of Christian Missions, p. 184.). K.M. Paniker in his Asia and Western Domination, p. 281, says: "... he acquired a fair mastery of Sanskrit." Max Muller said that Nobili had mastered the Sanskrit language.

bathe in a public tank every morning and before every repast, of which they have but one each day. In order to carry out their object, they studied the Tamil and Telugu languages so profoundly as to be able to write them in a masterily manner. An instance is often quoted of an Indian work which was praised by Voltaire as containing the purest doctrines of Christianity, and which was, as he stated, many hundred years old. It has since been discovered to have been the production of an Italian missionary,... ."

The Nobili forgery had received attention also of Adolf Kaegi according to whom "The Ezour (Yajur) Vedam presented by Voltaire to the Royal Library in Paris in 1761, published in 1778 by Sainte-Croix, and also translated into German, is a forgery made in the 17th century by a Jesuit missionary, perhaps Robertus de Nobilibus." And Kaegi continues to throw some more light: "It is well known with what enthusiasm Voltaire, in his writings, especially in Essai sur les moeurs et L'espirt de nations, repeatedly praised the ancient wisdom of the Brahmans which he thought to have discovered in the Ezour-Vedam, brought to his notice from India about the middle of the last century. But even Voltaire's eloquence persuaded but few of his contemporaries of the authenticity of the book. Although scholars were not in a position to disprove its genuineness, they preserved a suspicious and skeptical attitude toward it. Soon after Voltaire's death, J A. Harder, in the tenth book of his Indeen zur Geschichte der Menschheit, unhesitatingly expressed his opinion that whatever knowledge Europeans had hitherto gained of the mysteries of the Indians, was plainly only modern tradition; "for the real Weda of the Indians." He adds "as well as for the real Sanskrit language, we shall probably have long to wait."

The forgery which Nobili had tried to foist upon the world was well within Max Mullet's knowledge. He also knew too well what Harder and Kaegi thought of Nobili and his forgery but Max Muller never came out in support of truth. Himself being a Christian missionary who masqueraded all his lifetime from behind the secular mask of scholarship Max Muller did not like Nobili being labelled an impostor. Christian missionaries belong, by and large, to a clan whose one member would nearly always support another member whether right or wrong. Max Muller tried, therefore, to give to the entire controversy a clever Maxmullerian twist. He said: "The Ezour-Veda is not the work of Robert de Nobili. It was probably written by one of his converts. It is in Sanskrit verse, in the style of the Puranas and contains a wild mixture of Hindu and Christian doctrines.

The French translation was sent to Voltaire and printed by him in 1778: L'Ezour Vedam traduit du Sanscritam par un Brame.' Voltaire expressed his belief that the original was four centuries older than Alexander, and that it was the most precious gift for which the West had been ever indebted to the East. Mr. Ellis discovered the Sanskrit original at Pondicherry. (Asiatic Researches, Vol. XIV). There is no evidence for ascribing the work to Robert, and it is not mentioned in the list of his works." (Bertrand, La Missionda Madure, Paris, 1847-50, torn iii, p. 116; Mullbauer, p. 205, note).

We shall here take liberty to put to test the statement made by Max Muller in defence of Nobili and then, on the strength these of findings alone, call spade a spade. Max Muller made an emphatic statement ("Ezour-Vedam is not. . .") but in the next breath he expressed his doubt ("It was probably written..."). This is certainly very strange for a great research scholar like Max Muller to make two contradictory statements in one breath. If it was not the work of Nobili it must have been written by someone else. There would then be no element of doubt and no room for words like probably, perhaps and very likely. Secondly Max Muller maintained that Ezour-vedam had been written (in Sanskrit) by one of Nobili's converts but he did not take the trouble to explain how then Ezour-vedam came to be so cleverly and astutely interspersed and interpolated with carefully chosen and picked up Christian doctrines from the Bible. Surely none of his converts would have been so well acquainted with Christian dogmas as to be able to achieve this remarkable feat. Max Muller was discreetly silent on this point because he realized it too well that these interpolation could only have been made by someone who had devoted long number of years, as all Jesuits do, to the study of the Bible. And there was no one else in that crowd who knew the Bible and the biblical dogmas more intimately and thoroughly than Nobili.

Max Muller had tried to pull yet another fast one on the world of scholarship. He agreed with Bertrand and Mullbauer: "there is no evidence for ascribing the work to Robert, and it is not mentioned in the list of his works." It is only natural for Nobili's forgery not to be included in the list of his works. If it did, which Max Muller did not expect, it would have defeated its own purpose; the world would have come to know from the horse's mouth that it was a Nobili forgery. Anyone who sets out to palm off such a big fraud on the world of scholarship would surely take precaution not to include it in the list along with his other writings. If he did he would himself be leaving behind an unimpeachable evidence against his own misdeed. Nobili was not that stupid; he was a Jesuit.

Professor A.H. Sayce happened to be the Deputy Professor of Comparative Philology in Oxford at the time when Max Muller was the Professor. Like some other scholars Sayce did not seem to have agreed with Max Muller that the forgery was not the work of Nobili. As a result, Sayce put down his disapproval on record: "Another Italian, Roberto de Nobili, who went to India in 1606 as a missionary, actually transformed himself into a Brahman, in order to win over the Hindus; and after acquiring a knowledge not only of Tamil and Telugu but also of Sanskrit, "showed himself in public, dressed in the proper garb of the Brahmans, wearing their cord, and the frontal mark, observing their diet, and submitting even to complicated rules of race." One of his converts — so at last Professors Max Muller thinks — composed the curious Ezour, or fourth Veda, which professes to be the lost Veda that he came to preach, and "contains a wild mixture of Hindu and Christian doctrines."

Max Muller had hoped that everyone would swallow the untruth believing him that the Ezour-vedam had been forged by one of Nobili's converts and not by Nobili himself. But it was only a clever and mischievous attempt and only slightly less reprehensible than the forgery itself to find a scapegoat with the only dishonourable intention of shifting the blame for the misdeed to the shoulders of an Indian — a Hindu — with the clandestine purpose of shielding a fellow member of the missionary tribe to which Max Muller himself covertly belonged. It is understandable that even if what Max Muller claimed were to be true the Indian convert (a Hindu) would not be able to mix up so well the Christian doctrines with the tenets of Hindu Dharma. Even if a Hindu were to make such an attempt he would have been very easily found out. It is thus obvious that whatever Max Muller said in defence of Nobili was nothing but a piece of clever sophistry meant only to cover up a gigantic literary fraud attempted by a polished impostor.

The part which Nobili played as head of the Madura Mission in India had received the attention also of Rt. Rev. Henry Whitehead who was Principal of Bishops' College, Calcutta between January, 1884 and October, 1899. Though he called the Nobili era as "romantic and heroic" he also admitted that "de Nobili set himself to win to Christianity the Brahmans, the higher castes and the learned men. He adopted the mode of life of a Brahman, wore the sacred thread, studied Sanskrit and Hindu philosophy, mastered the vernacular and cut himself off from all intercourse with Europeans and low caste Indian Ghristians, including even the Jesuit priests who ministered to them. He went as far as to forge a fifth Veda in Sanskrit and insert into it Christian truths. His fellow workers adopted similar methods... ,"

It is clear that Nobili did forge a veda to mislead the Hindus of Madura off the Vedic path into the biblical labyrinth by dishonest and treacherous means. Whitehead has gone on record: "His (of Xavier — Br.D.Bh.) successor, the great Jesuit missionary Robert de Nobili, who worked at Madura at the beginning of the seventeenth century, adopted very different methods." After describing Nobili's method of imposture he goes on further to say: "He went so far as to forge a fifth Veda in Sanskrit and insert into it Christian truths. His fellow workers adopted similar methods and the records of their work during the seventeenth century forms one of the most romantic and heroic chapters in the history of Christian missions in India."

Though repeated attempts have been made in the past in Christian circles to absolve Nobili of all responsibility in this matter these efforts have failed to give him a clean chit. At times, Christian writers make no mention at all of Nobili and his imposture. Nevertheless some of them, more honest than others, have indicted Nobili for his nefarious deed. One of them was more forthright in his condemnation of Nobili. He said: "Still more extraordinary, indeed pre-eminent in the annals of fraud, is the history of the Jesuit mission in Madura, headed by Robert de Nobili, a nephew of cardinal Bellarmine. This man and his companions, after carefully studying the language and religion of the Hindu Shastras, assumed Sanskrit names and gave themselves out as European Brahmins, came to exchange ideas with their brethern in India. They adopted the yellow dress, wore the caste marks on their foreheads, and practised frequent ablutions. They observed also the vegetable diet of the Brahmins, even exceeding the natives in the severity of their ascetic observances. To these descriptions de' Nobili scrupled not to add the forgery of a parchment in the ancient character, showing that the Roman Brahmins were older than those of India, and that the Jesuits were descended in a direct line from Brahma. These allegations he dared to confirm upon oath in an assembly of Brahmins at Madura. Nor was this the most audacious forgery resorted to by the Jesuits. Singular as the design appears, they actually completed a new veda, written in the ancient Sanskrit with extraordinary skill, and interweaving with other matter some account of Christianity. This remarkable production was palmed upon the natives as genuine, and becoming known in Europe was adduced by Voltaire as proving the superiority of the Vedas to the genuine scriptures."

There are some Christians, not a small number by any standards, who will neither accept nor own the great wrong committed by Nobili. They will continue to defend him and keep telling, at times, unabashedly the less informed Indians, Hindus and Christians alike, that Nobili has only been maligned by anti-Christian Hindus. They will wilfully shut their eyes because by design they refuse to accept what is true. Nevertheless, we shall quote a few more authorities in order to drive the truth home.

At the time of delivering one of his ffulsean Lectures in 1949 before the University of Cambridge E.G. Dewick told his listeners: "As an example of this, we may recall de Nobili in India, in the seventeenth century with his Brahmin caste-mark, and his forged imitation of a Hindu sacred Veda." Though this assertion too might be dismissed by some Christian missionaries, by their friends and colporteurs as a figment of imagination the truth shall keep coming out as usual.

An official attempt to absolve Nobili of the charge of imposture and of fraud seems to have been made in an article in Indian Church History Review. The writer endeavoured to resurrect the reasoning which Nobili himself is said to have used in the year 1619 when he argued his case before the Goa Conference held to consider the complaint against him. He had pleaded: "there is very little hope of reaping any harvest, if the neophites are not allowed to live according to their customs and keep the badges of their caste." Although the Christian Church might claim and pretend that she does not approve of the methods and means employed by Nobili and her hordes to gather converts these methods are still being employed by many a Christian missionary. These methods are collectively known as "accommodation theory" which has been helpful in converting some Hindus to Christianity. It has since been admitted that "apart from success as Robert de Nobili and his colleagues achieved in Madura, where the "accommodation theory" fascilitated conversions the tale is one of almost unrelieved retrogression in the work of missions." This artifice, as invented by Nobili, is, therefore, being continuously put into use even today in several parts of India to gather converts for Christ.

The imposture to which Nobili had resorted is still being enacted though only quietly without giving rise to any suspicion. In fact very few people really are conscious of the existence of these impostures until they are told to the point of conviction. A living Christian historian, an Indian, acclaimed by the Church as "an outstanding figure in Indian Catholic world" confirmed the historicity of Nobili's fraud in yet another fashion:

". . . the eminent Jesuit. . . entered into serious discussions with the Brahmins, ... . He even wrote a new Veda to expound the Christian faith through a fitting medium. He claimed to be Italian Brahmin, not a Phiringi. Being a priest of aristocratic lineage, he felt he was not indulging in deceipt by passing himself off as a Brahmin. As he was an Italian he could similarly dissociate himself from the Portuguese." According to this Christian historian Nobili did not forge a veda; he only wrote it!

Later, an article, Foreign Missions in India, by the same author appeared in the Illustrated Weekly of India in which he said: "The Italian Jesuit, Robert de Nobili (1577-1656), was the first European scholar who according to Max Muller, could quote from Manu, the Puranas, and even the little known Apastamba-Sutra. He carried on dialogue with learned pundits in Sanskrit and won many of them over to his faith. He even composed a Christian Veda.

Among the Christian authorities quoted in this Appendix to prove the historicity of the gigantic literary fraud and imposture of Fr. Robert de Nobili, the opinions of Scares need a little more attention and deserve to be analysed at some great length. Both in his book and in his article he made a discreet and purposeful use of 'even' to express his admiration of Nobili's knowledge of Sanskrit and also of Hindu Dharama Shastra (he almost used the language of Max Muller - Lectures on the Science of Language, p. 167, Revised Fifth ed., 1866). Scares views this fraud and imposture as a great achievement of a Christian missionary but the change of language resorted to by him is immediately noticeable. In his book published in November 1964 he said: "He even wrote a new Veda to expound the Christian faith" but in just about four years time he changed it to read. "He even composed a Christian Veda." It is evident that Nobili did commit a forgery which Christian writers have all along struggled hard to explain away for more than 200 years.

What is a Christian veda? It is a term concocted by some Christian writers to cover up the misdeeds of a Christian missionary. Are the Christians not satisfied with their Bible that they should feel the need and the urge to forge a Christian veda in Hindustan; a Christian Talmud in Israel and perhaps a Christian Qur'an in Arabia? And what was the need and how could a veda serve the purpose of expounding the Christian faith? Was the Bible not enough for this purpose? Had the Bible failed to expound the Christian faith? The truth always keeps glaring in the face of all those who continue to support untruths. Yet when some people want some untruth to be believed every time they repeat it they tell it the louder.

Scares tried to be extra cautious is not making a straight admission that Nobili did commit a literary fraud. Nevertheless, he did acknowledge the truth of the fact that Nobili was inseparably connected with that veda with which Nobili's name is so notoriously linked. But Scares gave the whole affair a novel twist by saying that Nobili even wrote a new veda while in fact he wrote no Veda; he only very schemingly/0-gerf one.

Though some cohorts of Nobili have applauded him and his achievement pre-eminent among vhich stands his great literary fraud they have, as always happens, failed to fool all the world for all the times. Now, the world knows it too well that Robert de Nobili, an Italian member of the Society of Jesus forged a veda with the ignoble intention and clandestine purpose of inveigling the gullible Hindus of Madura into accepting Christianity. In his pious attempt to make Nobili look like a great and a pioneer Sanskritist Scares twice tried to transform fraudulent deception into a quality and thus lend respectable credibility to a piece of sin which had been committed for the furtherance of a purely parochial cause.

Though the gigantic literary fraud committed by Nobili and "the imposture was successful for a time" some wide awake independent scholars had felt suspicious about its genuineness from the beginning. There was at least one great scholar who instead of censoring Nobili for his misdeed nearly patted him on the back in a singularly novel fashion. This scholar was none else but Max Muller who instead commended Nobili's example in a scholarly whisper. How did he do it? and what did he say abci": Nobili's great imposture and fraud? We shall let Max Muller speak for himself.

"The first certain instance of a European missionary having mastered the difficulties of the Sanskrit language, belong to a later period — to what may be called the period of Robert de Nobili, as distinguished from the first period, which is under the presiding spirit of Francis Xavier. Robert de Nobili went to India in 1606. He was himself a man of high family, of a refined and cultivated mind, and he therefore, perceived the more quickly the difficulties which kept the high castes, from joining the Christian communities formed at Madura and other places. These communities consisted chiefly of men of low rank, of no education, and no refinement. He conceived the bold plan of presenting himself as a Brahman, and thus obtaining access to the high and noble, the wise and the learned, in the land. He shut himself up for years, acquiring in secret a knowledge not only of Tamil and Telugu, but of Sanskrit. When after a patient study of the language and literature of the Brahmans, he felt himself enough to grapple with his antagonists, he showed himself in public, dressed in the proper garb of the Brahmans, wearing their cord and their front mark, observing their diet, and submitting even to the complicated rules of caste. He was successful, in spite of the persecutions both of the Brahmans, who were afraid of him, and of his own fellow-labourers, who could not understand his policy. His life in India, where he died as an old blind man, is full of interest to the missionary. I can only speak of him here as the first European Sanskrit scholars. A man who could quote from Manu, from the Puranas, nay from works such as the Apastamba-Sutras, which are known even at present to only those few Sanskrit scholars who can read Sanskrit MSS., must have been far advanced in a knowledge of the sacred language and literature of the Brahmans; and the very idea that he came, as he said, to preach a new or a fourth veda, which had been lost, shows how well he knew the strong and weak points of theological system which he came to conquer. It is surprising that the reports which he sent to Rome, in order to defend himself against the charge of idolatry, a id in which he drew a faithful picture of the religion, the custom, and literature of the Brahmans should not have attracted the attention of scholar. The 'Accommodation Question' as it was called, occupied cardinals, and popes for many years; but not one of them seems to have perceived the extraordinary interest attaching to the existence of an ancient civilization so perfect and so firmly rooted as to require accommodation even from the missionaries of Rome.

While Max Muller gave a good and clean chit to Nobili, voluntarily and of his own volition, he was in fact scratching the back of a fellow missionary and there is no doubt Nobili would have equally obliged Max Muller had Nobili been then alive. But the testimonial which Max Muller gave Nobili betrays Max Muller nearly as much as Nobili's forgery unmasked him. Max Muller made two such self contradictory statements that these at once give rise to the irrepressible feeling that what Max Muller told was only a slick lie. According to him in spite of the persecutions both of the Brahmans, who were afraid of him, and of his own fellow-labourers, who could not understand his policy, Nobili was successful. How anyone who is afraid of someone can also simultaneously be his persecutor is an enigma which Max Muller did not care to explain. While admitting that the experiment made by Nobili was bold he threw a hint to Christian missionaries at large that it was full also of interest to missionaries and that the method deserved to be emulated and adopted by them.

Truth is often stranger than fiction and the story of Nobili's great literary fraud has always held great fascination for those who have felt interested in the subject. How all of them have looked at it might easily be too laborious a task and the space at our disposal here will not permit this exercise. Nevertheless, we shall mention a few more out of the many just to illustrate how they view Nobili's efforts made in the service of Christianity.
